## PREFACE

These notes are based on a course I gave at the University of Chicago in the Spring Quarter of 1964. They were originally published in 1965 in the Lecture Notes of the Mathematics Department of the University of Chicago under the title of "Topics in Ring Theory." This present version is a reworking of these notes—many of the proofs in the first chapters have been changed, and some material has been added.

The subject matter divides itself naturally into three distinct parts. In the first three chapters, I treat the structure of a simple associative ring and some of its subsets as Lie and Jordan rings and use these results to obtain theorems about the associative nature of the ring itself. Chapters 4, 5, and 6 are devoted to the theorems of Goldie and some of their consequences for rings conditioned by some appropriate chain condition. The material of the last chapter is concerned with recent examples and counterexamples; we find there the Golod-Shafarevitch theorem and examples due to Bergman and Sasiada.

The overlap of the material here and in my Carus Monograph "Non-Commutative Rings" is not large; it consists of one of the proofs of Goldie's theorem, ultra-products and Posner's theorem, and the Golod-Shafarevitch theorem.

## Chapter 1

### Lie and Jordan Structures in Simple Rings

In this course, we shall study certain aspects of the theory of associative rings. From time to time, we shall have to refer to results proved in our set of notes "Theory of Rings" (University of Chicago), which we shall refer to as (N), or to Jacobson's book "Structure of Rings," referred to as (J).

We start things off with:

### Lemma 1.1
Let \( R \) be a ring and \( 0 \neq \rho \) a right ideal of \( R \). Suppose that, given \( a \in \rho \), \( a^n = 0 \) for a fixed integer \( n \). Then \( R \) has a non-zero nilpotent ideal.

#### Proof
The argument we use is a variation of one given by Levitzki. We proceed by induction on \( n \).

Let $a \neq 0$ be in $\rho$; let $A = a\rho$, where $\rho$ is a subset of $R$ and represents a specific set of elements under consideration. Suppose for the moment that $A \neq \{0\}$, where $\{0\}$ explicitly denotes the zero ideal. If $x \in R$, then $(a + ax)^{n} = 0$ since it is in $p$, where $p$ is a prime ideal of $R$. The reasoning for this is that $p$ is prime, and $(a + ax)^{n}$ vanishes due to the nilpotency of $a + ax$ in $p$. Hence, on expansion, we get $(ax)^{n-1}a = 0$. This follows because $(a + ax)^{n} = 0$ implies that each term in the expansion, including $(ax)^{n-1}a$, must vanish. Thus, $(ax)^{n-1}A = \{0\}$. 

Let $T = \{x \in A \mid xA = \{0\}\}$. To show that $T$ is an ideal of $A$, note that for any $x, y \in T$ and $r \in A$, we have $xA = \{0\}$ and $yA = \{0\}$, so $(x + y)A = xA + yA = \{0\}$. Similarly, for $xr \in A$, $(xr)A = x(rA) = xA = \{0\}$. Therefore, $T$ is closed under addition and multiplication by elements of $A$, proving that $T$ is an ideal. Moreover, as we have just seen, $y \in A$ implies that $y^{n-1} \in T$ because $(y^{n-1})A = (y^{n-1}A) = \{0\}$. 

Therefore, in $\bar{A} = A/T$, every element satisfies $\bar{y}^{n-1} = 0$. This follows because $y^{n-1} \in T$ implies that $\bar{y}^{n-1} = 0$ in the quotient algebra $\bar{A}$. 

By our induction hypothesis, which assumes that for any quotient of $A$ by an ideal, the resulting algebra has a nilpotent ideal, $\bar{A}$ has a nilpotent ideal $\bar{U} \neq \{0\}$. The base case of the induction is the trivial algebra, where the result holds. Let $U$ be the inverse image of $\bar{U}$ in $A$. Since $\bar{U}^{k} = \{0\}$, it follows that $U^{k} \subset T$, and hence $U^{k+1} \subset TA = \{0\}$. Also, since $\bar{U} \neq \{0\}$, we have $U \not \subset T$, which implies that $U \cdot U \neq \{0\}$. Finally, $UA = Uap \neq \{0\}$, where $Uap$ is defined as the product of $U$, $a$, and $p$, and this is a nilpotent right ideal of $R$.

# Lemma and Structures in Associative Rings

## Lemma Proof

Suppose that $a \in \rho$, and $a^{2}=0$ implies that $a \cdot \rho = \{0\}$. Here, $a \cdot \rho$ refers to the set $\{a \cdot x \mid x \in \rho\}$. For any $x \in \rho$, since $x^{n}=0$ for some fixed integer $n$, we have $\left(x^{n-1}\right)^{2}=0$, and so $x^{n-1} \cdot \rho = \{0\}$. Let $W = \{x \in \rho \mid x \cdot \rho = \{0\}\}$. Clearly, $W$ is an ideal of $\rho$. If $W = \rho$, then $\rho^{2} = \{0\}$, and $\rho$ would provide us with a nilpotent right ideal, where a "nilpotent right ideal" is defined as a right ideal $I$ such that $I^{m} = \{0\}$ for some positive integer $m$.

In $\overline{\rho} = \rho / W$, we have $\overline{x}^{n-1} = 0$. By induction, this gives us a nilpotent ideal $\overline{V} \neq \{0\}$ in $\overline{\rho}$. If $V$ is the inverse image of $\overline{V}$ in $\rho$, then $V \neq \{0\}$ and $V$ is nilpotent since $\overline{V}$ is nilpotent. Let $V_p$ denote the subset of $V$ corresponding to elements related to $p$. Since $V$ is nilpotent, $V_p$ is also nilpotent. Thus, we have shown that $R$ must have a non-zero nilpotent right ideal.

If $R$ has a non-zero nilpotent right ideal, it follows that $R$ has (almost trivially) a non-zero nilpotent ideal. This proves the lemma.

## Induced Structures in Associative Rings

Given any associative ring $R$, we can induce on $R$, using its operations, two new structures: the Lie structure and the Jordan structure, by defining the new products:
- **Lie product**: $[x, y] = x \cdot y - y \cdot x$
- **Jordan product**: $x \circ y = x \cdot y + y \cdot x$

We propose to investigate the relationship between the associative structure of $R$ and these induced Lie and Jordan structures.

## Subrings in Associative Rings

We say that a subset $A$ of $R$ is a **Lie subring** of $R$ if $A$ is an additive subgroup such that for $a, b \in A$, $[a, b] = a \cdot b - b \cdot a$ is also in $A$. Similarly, we define a **Jordan subring** of $R$ as a subset $A$ of $R$ that is an additive subgroup such that for $a, b \in A$, $a \circ b = a \cdot b + b \cdot a$ is also in $A$.

# DEFINITION

Let $A$ be a Lie subring of $R$. The additive subgroup $U \subseteq A$ is said to be a Lie ideal of $A$ if whenever $u \in U$ and $a \in A$, then $[u, a] = ua - au$ is in $U$.

We similarly define the concept of a Jordan ideal of a Jordan subring of $R$.

Our first objective will be to determine the Lie and Jordan ideals of the ring $R$ itself in the case when $R$ is restricted to be a simple ring.

We begin with the Jordan ideals of $R$, which are a good deal easier to characterize.

# LEMMA 1.2

If $U$ is a Jordan ideal of $R$, then for all $a, b \in U$ and $x \in R$, $(ab + ba)x - x(ab + ba) \in U$.

## Proof

Since $a \in U$, for any $x \in R$, $a(xb - bx) + (xb - bx)a$ is in $U$. But

$$
\begin{aligned}
a(xb - bx) + (xb - bx)a & = \{(ax - xa)b + b(ax - xa)\} \\
& \quad + \{(ab + ba)x - x(ab + ba)\}.
\end{aligned}
$$

The left side and the first term on the right side are in $U$, hence $(ab + ba)x - x(ab + ba)$ is also in $U$, proving the lemma.

# THEOREM 1.1

Let $R$ be a ring in which $2x = 0$ implies $x = 0$, and suppose further that $R$ has no non-zero nilpotent ideals. Then any non-zero Jordan ideal of $R$ contains a non-zero (associative) ideal of $R$.

### Proof

Let $U \neq 0$ be a Jordan ideal of $R$, and suppose that $a, b \in U$. By Lemma 1 and Lemma 2, for any $x \in R$, we have $x c - c x \in U$, where $c = a b + b a$. Since $c \in U$, it follows that $x c + c x \in U$. Adding these, we obtain $2 x c \in U$ for all $x \in R$. Hence, for any $y \in R$, we have $(2 x c) y + y (2 x c) \in U$. Therefore, $2 x c y \in U$, which implies $2 RcR \subseteq U$.

Now, $2 RcR$ is an ideal of $R$. If $2 RcR \neq \{0\}$, we are done. Otherwise, if $2 RcR = \{0\}$, then by our assumptions, $RcR = \{0\}$, and so $(Rc)^2 = \{0\}$. Since $R$ has no nilpotent ideals, this forces $c = 0$. Thus, given $a, b \in U$, we have $a b + b a = 0$.

Let $0 \neq a \in U$. For any $x \in R$, let $b = a x + x a \in U$. Then, we have:
\[
a (a x + x a) + (a x + x a) a = 0.
\]
Expanding this, we get:
\[
a^2 x + x a^2 + 2 a x a = 0.
\]
Now, for $a \in U$, we also have:
\[
a a + a a = 2 a^2,
\]
which simplifies to $a^2 = 0$. Substituting this into the previous relation, we find:
\[
2 a x a = 0 \quad \text{for all } x \in R.
\]
Thus, $a R a = \{0\}$. However, since $a R \neq \{0\}$, this implies that $a R$ is a nilpotent right ideal of $R$, which contradicts our assumption that $R$ has no nilpotent ideals.

In conclusion, we have shown that $U$ contains a non-zero ideal of $R$.

### Corollary

If $R$ is a simple ring of characteristic $\neq 2$, then $R$ is simple as a Jordan ring.

We now turn to the case of the Lie ideals of $R$.

### Definition
If $\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{B}$ are subsets of $R$, then $\{\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{B}\}$ is the additive subgroup of $R$ generated by all $ab - ba$ with $a \in \mathrm{A}$, $b \in \mathrm{B}$.

### Lemma 1.3
Let $R$ be a ring with no non-zero nilpotent ideals (ideals $I$ such that $I^n = 0$ for some $n > 0$) in which $2x = 0$ implies $x = 0$. Suppose that $\mathrm{U} \neq 0$ is both a Lie ideal and a subring of $R$. Then either $\mathrm{U} \subseteq Z$, the center of $R$, or $\mathrm{U}$ contains a non-zero ideal of $R$.

### Proof
Let us first suppose that $\mathrm{U}$, as a ring, is not commutative. Then for some $x, y \in \mathrm{U}$, $xy - yx \neq 0$. For any $r \in R$, $x(yr) - (yr)x$ is in $\mathrm{U}$, that is, $(xy - yx)r + y(xr - rx)$ is in $\mathrm{U}$. The second term of this is in $\mathrm{U}$ since both $y$ and $xr - rx$ are in $\mathrm{U}$ (since $\mathrm{U}$ is both a Lie ideal and a subring). The net result of all this is that $(xy - yx)R \subseteq \mathrm{U}$.

But then for $r_1, s \in R$, $((xy - yx)r)s - s((xy - yx)r) \in \mathrm{U}$, implying that $R(xy - yx)R \subseteq \mathrm{U}$. We have now shown that the ideal $R(xy - yx)R$ is in $\mathrm{U}$. If $R(xy - yx)R = 0$, then $(R(xy - yx))^2 = 0$, which contradicts the assumption.

We have shown that the result is correct if $\mathrm{U}$, as a subring of $R$, is not commutative.

So, suppose that $U$ is commutative; we want to show that it lies in the center of $R$. Given $a \in U$, $x \in R$, then $a x - x a \in U$, so it commutes with $a$. Now, for $x, y \in R$, 

\[
a \big((x y) - (x y) a\big) = \big(a (x y) - (x y) a\big) a.
\]

Expanding $a (x y) - (x y) a$ as 

\[
(a x - x a) y + x (a y - y a),
\]

and using that $a$ commutes with this, with $a x - x a$, and with $a y - y a$, yields 

\[
2 (a x - x a)(a y - y a) = 0
\]

for all $x, y \in R$. Since $2r = 0$ forces $r = 0$, we obtain 

\[
(a x - x a)(a y - y a) = 0.
\]

In this, put $y = a x_{2}$—this results in 

\[
(a x - x a) R (a x - x a) = 0.
\]

Since $R$ has no nilpotent ideals, we conclude that $a x - x a = 0$, and so, $a$ must be in the center of $R$.

Note that in the latter part of the proof of Lemma 1.3, we have also proved the following:

**Sublemma.** Let $R$ be a ring having no non-zero nilpotent ideals in which $2x = 0$ implies that $x = 0$. If $a \in R$ commutes with all $a x - x a$, $x \in R$, then $a$ is in the center of $R$.

**Lemma 1.3** immediately implies:

**Theorem 1.2.** Let $R$ be a simple ring of characteristic $\neq 2$. Then any Lie ideal of $R$ which is also a subring of $R$ must either be $R$ itself or be contained in the center of $R$.

**Definition.** If $U$ is a Lie ideal of $R$, let 

\[
T(U) = \{x \in R \mid [x, R] \subset U\}.
\]

**Lemma 1.4.** For any ring $R$, if $U$ is a Lie ideal of $R$, then $T(U)$ is both a subring and a Lie ideal of $R$; moreover, $U \subset T(U)$.

Proof. Since $U$ is a Lie ideal of $R$, $U \subset T(U)$; since $[\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{R}] \subset \mathrm{U} \subset \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$, $\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$ must certainly be a Lie ideal of $R$.

Now suppose that $a, b \in T(U)$, $r \in R$. Then

$$
(ab)r - r(ab) = \{a(br) - (br)a\} + \{b(ra) - (ra)b\},
$$

so, since $a, b \in \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$, the right side is in $U$. Therefore $[\mathrm{ab}, \mathrm{R}] \subset \mathrm{U}$, that is $\mathrm{ab} \in \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$.

We now prove the following:

**THEOREM 1.3.**  
Let $R$ be a simple ring of characteristic $\neq 2$ and let $U$ be a Lie ideal of $R$. Then either $U \subset Z$, the center of $R$, or $U \supset [\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$.

**Proof.**  
By Theorem 1.2 and Lemma 1.4, since $T(U)$ is both a subring and a Lie ideal of $R$, either $T(U) \subset Z$ or $T(U) = R$. If $\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}) = \mathrm{R}$, then by its very definition $[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}] \subset \mathrm{U}$; if $\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}) \subset \mathrm{Z}$, since $U \subset T(U)$, we obtain $U \subset Z$.

**COROLLARY.**  
If $R$ is a non-commutative simple ring of characteristic $\neq 2$, then the subring generated by $[R, R]$ is $R$.

**Proof.** 

Any additive subgroup containing $[R, R]$ is, trivially, a Lie ideal of $R$. Hence, the subring generated by $[R, R]$ is a Lie ideal. Thus, by Theorem 1.2, it equals $R$ or is in $Z$. If it is in $Z$, then $[R, R] \subset Z$. Thus, for $a \in R$, $a$ commutes with all $a x - x a$, $x \in R$; by the sublemma, we get that $a \in Z$, that is, $R \subseteq Z$. Since we assumed $R$ to be non-commutative, this is ruled out; hence the corollary.

We now should like to settle the problem even when $R$ has characteristic 2. Note that the characteristic of $R$ has not entered into the discussion in the passage from Theorem 1.2 on. So we ask: when, in characteristic 2, does Theorem 1.2 fail? It certainly fails in $F_{2}$, the ring of all $2 \times 2$ matrices over $F$, a field of characteristic 2, for 

\[
U = \left\{ \left. \begin{pmatrix} a & b \\ b & a \end{pmatrix} \, \right| a, b \in F \right\}
\]

is a Lie ideal and subring of $R$ which is neither in $Z$ nor does it equal $R$. We aim to show that this is, effectively, the only counterexample.

Suppose that $R$ is a simple ring of characteristic 2 and that $U$ is a Lie ideal and subring of $R$, $U \neq R$, and $U \not\subseteq Z$. As in the proof of Lemma 1.3, we obtain that $U$, as a subring of $R$, must be commutative. That is, given $u, v \in U$, then $u v + v u = 0$.

Let $a \in U$; then $as + sa \in U$ for all $s \in R$, hence $a(as + sa) = (as + sa)a$. This says that $a^{2} \in Z$. Since for any $r \in R_{1}$, $ar + ra \in U$, we also have that $(ar + ra)^{2} \in Z$.

If $Z = (0)$, then $a^{2} = 0$, $(ar + ra)^{2} = 0$, from which we get $(ar)^{3} = 0$. But then $aR$ is a right ideal of $R$ in which the cube of every element is $0$; by Lemma 1.1, $R$ would have a nilpotent ideal, that is, $R$ would be nilpotent, which is impossible for a simple ring.

Therefore, we may assume that $Z \neq (0)$ and that there is an element $a \in U$, $a \in Z$, such that $a^{2} \neq 0$, $a^{2} \in Z$, and $(ar + ra)^{2} \in Z$ for all $r \in R$.

To answer completely what the structure of $R$ must be, we prove a subsidiary:

### Theorem 1.4
Let $R$ be a simple ring of characteristic $2$ and suppose that there exists an $a \in R$, $a \in Z$, such that $a^{2} \in Z$ and $(ax + xa)^{4} \in Z$ for all $x \in R$. Then $R$ is $4\text{-dimensional}$ over $Z$.

Before proving the theorem, we should like to point out that a more general theorem actually holds, namely: if $R$ is a simple ring with an element $a \in Z$ such that $(ax - xa)^{n} \in Z$ for all $x \in R$, then $R$ is $4\text{-dimensional}$ over $Z$.

# Proof of Theorem 1.4

If $z = \{0\}$, then both $a^2 = 0$ and $(a x + x a)^4 = 0$, hence $(a x)^5 = a (a x + x a)^4 x = 0$ for all $x \in R$. But then the right ideal $a R$ satisfies $u^5 = 0$ for all elements $u \in a R$; by Lemma 1.1, this is not possible in a simple ring.

Suppose, then, that $Z \neq \{0\}$, hence $Z < R$. If $a^2 = 0$, then $b = a + 1$ satisfies $b^2 = 1$ and $(b x + x b)^4 \in Z$ for all $x \in R$. Therefore, we may assume that $a^2 = \alpha \neq 0$ in $Z$. Let $Z^{\prime} = Z(\sqrt{\alpha})$; then $R^{\prime} = R \otimes_Z Z^{\prime}$ is simple. Moreover, in $R^{\prime}$ we have $\left(a x^{\prime} + x^{\prime} a\right)^4 \in Z^{\prime}$ for all $x^{\prime} \in R^{\prime}$. Since $\operatorname{dim}(R^{\prime} / Z^{\prime}) = \operatorname{dim}(R / Z)$, to prove the theorem it is enough to do so in $R^{\prime}$. Also, $b = a / \beta$ where $\beta \in Z^{\prime}$, $\beta^2 = \alpha$, satisfies $b^2 = 1$ and $\left(b x^{\prime} + x^{\prime} b\right)^4 \in Z^{\prime}$. Hence, without loss of generality, we may suppose that $a \in R$, $a^2 = 1$, and $(a x + x a)^4 \in Z$ for all $x \in R$.

Now $R$ is a dense ring of linear transformations on a vector space $V$ over a division ring $D$ (since $Z \neq \{0\}$ and $R$ is simple). Since $(a + 1)^2 = 0$ and $a + 1 \neq 0$, $V$ must be more than 1-dimensional over $D$. Since $a \neq 1$, it is immediate that there is $v \in V$ such that $v$ and $v a$ are linearly independent over $D$.

If for some $w \in V$, $v$, $va$, and $w(1+a)$ are linearly independent over $D$, then the subspace $V_{0}$ spanned by these is invariant under $a$, and $a$ induces the linear transformations

$$
\begin{pmatrix}
0 & 1 & 0 \\
1 & 0 & 0 \\
0 & 0 & 1
\end{pmatrix}
$$

on $V_{0}$. By density of $R$ on $V$, there is an $x \in R$ which induces

$$
\begin{pmatrix}
0 & 1 & 0 \\
0 & 0 & 0 \\
0 & 0 & 0
\end{pmatrix}
$$

on $V_{0}$. Hence $a x + x a$ induces

$$
\begin{pmatrix}
0 & 1 & 0 \\
1 & 0 & 0 \\
0 & 0 & 0
\end{pmatrix}
$$

on $V_{0}$. However, $(a x + x a)^{4}$ does not induce a scalar on $V_{0}$ since it induces

$$
\begin{pmatrix}
1 & 0 & 0 \\
0 & 1 & 0 \\
0 & 0 & 0
\end{pmatrix}
$$

on $V_{0}$. Thus, we have that for all $w \in V$, $v$, $va$, and $w(1+a)$ are linearly dependent over $D$.

If $V$ is more than 2-dimensional over $D$, there is a $w \in V$ such that $v$, $va$, and $w$ are linearly independent over $D$. By the above, $wa$ is in the subspace $V_{1}$ they span. The matrix of $a$ on $V_{1}$ is

$$
\begin{pmatrix}
0 & 1 & 0 \\
1 & 0 & 0 \\
\alpha & \beta & \gamma
\end{pmatrix}
$$

By density, there is an $x \in R$ which induces 

$$
\begin{pmatrix}
0 & 1 & 0 \\
0 & 0 & 0 \\
0 & 0 & 0
\end{pmatrix}
$$

on $V_{1}$; but then $a x + x a$ induces

$$
\begin{pmatrix}
1 & 0 & 0 \\
0 & 1 & 0 \\
0 & \alpha & 0
\end{pmatrix}
$$

on $V_{1}$.

### Definitions and Clarifications

- **$a$**: A linear operator on the vector space $V$.
- **$x$**: An element of the ring $R$ that acts on $V$.
- **$R$**: A dense subring acting on $V$.
- **$D$**: The division ring over which $V$ is defined.
- **$V$**: A vector space over $D$.
- **$V_{0}$**: A subspace of $V$ spanned by $v$, $va$, and $w(1+a)$.
- **$V_{1}$**: A subspace of $V$ spanned by $v$, $va$, and $w$.
- **$\alpha, \beta, \gamma$**: Scalars in $D$ representing entries in the matrix of $a$ on $V_{1}$.

# Proof of Commutativity of $D$

Whose fourth power is not a scalar.  
Thus, we must have that $V$ is 2-dimensional over $D$. All that remains is to show that $D$ is commutative.

Let 

$$
a = \begin{pmatrix} \alpha & \beta \\ \gamma & \delta \end{pmatrix};
$$ 

then $a^2 = 1$ yields $\alpha^2 + \beta^2 = 1$, $\alpha \beta + \beta \delta = 0$, $\gamma \beta + \delta^2 = 1$. In particular, not both $\alpha = 0$ and $\gamma = 0$. If $\sigma \in D$, then using 

$$
x = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & \sigma \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}
$$ 

and 

$$
(ax + xa)^4 \in Z
$$ 

yields

$$
\begin{pmatrix}
\sigma \alpha & \alpha \sigma + \sigma \beta \\
0 & \delta \sigma
\end{pmatrix}^4 \in Z.
$$

This gives for all $\sigma \in D$, $(\sigma \alpha)^4 \in Z$ and $(\gamma \sigma)^4 \in Z$. If $\alpha \neq 0$, then $\sigma \alpha$ runs through $D$ as $\sigma$ does, so every $x \in D$ would satisfy $x^4 \in Z$. But a non-commutative division ring cannot be purely inseparable over its center (see (N), Lemma 2.16). Thus, $\alpha \neq 0$ implies $D$ is commutative. Similarly, $\gamma \neq 0$ implies that $D$ is commutative. Since one of these must hold, we get that $D$ is commutative, and so $R$ is 4-dimensional over $Z$.

Since the hypothesis of Theorem 1.4 is precisely the one led to by the assumption that Theorem 1.2 (and so Theorem 1.3) was false, we obtain:

**THEOREM 1.5.** If $R$ is a simple ring and if $U$ is a Lie Ideal of $R$, then either $\mathrm{U} \subset \mathrm{Z}$ or $\mathrm{U} \supset [\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$, except if $R$ is of characteristic 2 and is 4-dimensional over its center.

The theorem has as an immediate corollary:

**COROLLARY.** If $R$ is a simple non-commutative ring, then the subring generated by $[R, R]$ is $R$.

As a consequence of these results, we prove a theorem about simple rings with involution. Let $R$ be a simple ring of characteristic not 2 and suppose that on $R$ there is defined an involution ${ }^{*}$, that is, a map of $R$ into itself satisfying $a^{**} = a$, $(a+b)^{*} = a^{*} + b^{*}$, $(ab)^{*} = b^{*}a^{*}$. Let  
$S = \{a \in R \mid a^{*} = a\}$ and $K = \{a \in R \mid a^{*} = -a\}$.  

$S$ is the set of symmetric elements, $K$ the set of skew-symmetric elements of $R$. Note that, since the characteristic of $R$ is not $2$, $R = S + K$, $S \cap K = \{0\}$, and that $S$ is a Jordan subring of $R$ and $K$ a Lie subring of $R$. Also, if $s \in S$ and $k \in K$, then $sk - ks \in S$. We shall make an intensive study of $S$ and $K$ in the next chapter. But now we content ourselves with proving the following:

**THEOREM 1.6.** Let $R$ be a simple ring with involution of characteristic not 2, and let $S$ be the set of symmetric elements of $R$. Then $\overline{\mathrm{S}}$, the subring of $R$ generated by $S$, is $R$ unless $R$ is of dimension 4 or less (thus 4 or 1-dimensional) over its center.

**Proof.**  
We claim that $\overline{S}$ is a Lie ideal of $R$. To see this, note first that trivially $[\overline{S}, S] \subset \overline{S}$. If $k \in K$ and $\overline{s} \in \overline{S}$, we want to show that $[\overline{s}, k] \in \overline{S}$. To do so, since $\overline{s}$ is a sum of monomials from $S$, we need merely verify it for monomials $\overline{s} = s_{1}s_{2} \cdots s_{n}$, where $s_{i} \in S$.  

But then:  
\[
\left[s_{1}s_{2} \cdots s_{n}, k\right] = \left[s_{1}, k\right]s_{2} \cdots s_{n} + \cdots + s_{1} \cdots s_{i-1}\left[s_{i}, k\right]s_{i+1} \cdots s_{n} + \cdots + s_{1}s_{2} \cdots s_{n-1}\left[s_{n}, k\right],
\]  
which is certainly in $\overline{S}$. Thus,  
\[
[\overline{S}, R] = [\overline{S}, S+K] = [\overline{S}, S] + [\overline{S}, K] \subset \overline{S},
\]  
and so $\overline{S}$ is a Lie ideal of $R$. By definition, it is a subring of $R$. Therefore, by Theorem 1.2, we conclude that either $\overline{S} = R$ or $\overline{S} \subset Z$.  

We consider the second possibility, namely, $\overline{S} \subset Z$. But then $S \subset Z$. Given $a \in R$, we have $a = s + k$, where $s \in S \subseteq Z$ and $k \in K$. Hence,  
\[
(a - s)^{2} = k^{2} \in S \subset Z.
\]  
Consequently,  
\[
a^{2} - 2sa + s^{2} - k^{2} = 0,
\]  
which is to say, $R$ is quadratic over $Z$. By Theorem 2.38 of $N$, we get that $R$ is at most 4-dimensional over $Z$.  

Related to this theorem is the following remark, which holds for simple rings of any characteristic that have involutions.

# Theorem 1.7

Let $R$ be a simple ring with involution whose center $Z = (0)$ or for which $\operatorname{dim}(R / Z) > 4$. Then the only elements commuting with all the symmetric elements lie in $Z$.

## Proof

Let $a \in R$ commute with all $s \in S$. If the characteristic of $R$ is not $2$, by Theorem $1.6$, $\overline{\mathrm{S}} = \mathrm{R}$, hence $a \in \mathrm{Z}$ follows. Thus, we may suppose that $R$ is of characteristic $2$.

Let 
\[
T = \{r \in R \mid r s = s r, \, \forall s \in S\}.
\]
$T$ is clearly a subring of $R$. Given $x \in R$, $a \in T$, then 
\[
a \left(x + x^{*}\right) = \left(x + x^{*}\right) a,
\]
hence 
\[
a x + x a = a x^{*} + x^{*} a.
\]
We want to show that $T$ is a Lie ideal of $R$. Given $a \in T$, $y \in R$, $s \in S$, then 
\[
(a y + y a) s = a y s + y a s = a \left(y s + s y^{*}\right) + s a y^{*} + y s a = \left(y s + s y^{*}\right) a + s a y^{*} + y s a = s y^{*} a + s a y^{*} = s \left(y^{*} a + a y^{*}\right) = s (y a + a y),
\]
as we have just shown. In other words, $T$ is both a Lie ideal and a subring of $R$. By our assumption on $\operatorname{dim}(R / Z)$, we get from Theorem $1.5$ and its corollary that $T \subset Z$ or $T = R$. If $T = R$, then $S \subset Z$, which we have seen forces $\operatorname{dim}(R / Z) \leq 4$. Thus, $T \subset Z$, which is the assertion of the theorem.

We now turn to a study of the Lie structure of $[R, R]$ as a Lie subring of $R$.

We begin with an easy result,

**Lemma 1.5.** Let $\mathrm{R}$ be a ring without any non-zero nilpotent ideals. Then any element of $\mathrm{R}$ which commutes with all elements of $[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$ must lie in the center of $\mathrm{R}$.

**Proof.** Let $a$ commute with all elements of $[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$. Given $x, y \in \mathrm{R}$, then $a$ commutes with $xy - yx$ and with $x(xy) - (xy)x = x(xy - yx)$. The upshot of this is that $(ax - xa)(xy - yx) = 0$ for all $x, y \in \mathrm{R}$. In this, replace $y$ by $ya$. Since $x(ya) - (ya)x = (xy - yx)a + y(xa - ax)$, we get 
\[
0 = (xa - ax)(x(ya) - (ya)x) = (xa - ax)(xy - yx)a + (xa - ax)y(xa - ax).
\]
This results in 
\[
(xa - ax)\mathrm{R}(xa - ax) = \{0\};
\]
but then $(xa - ax)\mathrm{R}$ is a nilpotent right ideal, so it is $\{0\}$. Since $\mathrm{R}$ has no nilpotent ideals, we get from this $xa - ax = 0$, that is, $a$ must be in the center of $\mathrm{R}$.

**Theorem 1.6.** If $\mathrm{R}$ is a simple ring and is not 4-dimensional over a field of characteristic 2, then $[[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}], [\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]] = [\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$.

**Proof.** If $\mathrm{R}$ is commutative, the result is clearly correct; so suppose $\mathrm{R}$ is not commutative. Now, $[[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}], [\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]]$ is a Lie ideal of $\mathrm{R}$; hence, by Lemma 1.5, either $[[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}], [\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]] \supseteq [\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$ (the desired conclusion) or $[[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}], [\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]] \subseteq \mathrm{Z}$. Suppose, then, that $[[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}], [\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]] \subseteq \mathrm{Z}$.

If $[R, R] \subset Z$, then by the corollary to Theorem 1.5, $R$ is commutative. So $[R, R] \not\subset Z$. Let $a \in [R, R]$, $a \notin Z$, and let $x \in R$. Thus $\alpha = [a, [a, x]]$ and $\beta = [a, [a, ax]]$ are in $[[R, R], [R, R]]$, hence in $Z$. Since $\beta = \alpha a$ and $Z$ is a field, if $\alpha \neq 0$ then it follows that $a \in Z$. So $a$ must be $0$. Thus $[a, [a, x]] = 0$ for all $x \in R$.

If the characteristic of $R$ is not $2$, this forces $a \in Z$ on invoking the sublemma. If the characteristic of $R$ is $2$, then $[a, [a, x]] = 0$ implies $a^{2} \in Z$; hence $a^{2} \in Z$ for all $a \in [R, R]$. But for $x \in R$, $ax - xa \in [R, R]$, so $(ax - xa)^{2} \in Z$ and $a^{2} \in Z$ and $a \in Z$. By Theorem 1.4, we get that $\operatorname{dim} R / Z = 4$, contrary to hypothesis. The theorem is now proved.

Let $U$ be a Lie ideal of $[R, R]$ and set $T(U) = \{x \in R \mid [x, R] \subset U\}$. It is immediate that $T(U)$ is a subring of $R$. We investigate the interrelationship of $T(U)$ and $U$ in

**LEMMA 1.6.** If $U$ is a Lie ideal of $[R, R]$, then:

1. $[U, U] \subset T(U)$  
2. $[U, T(U)] \subset T(U)$  
3. $[[R, T(U)], T(U)] \subset T(U)$  
4. $[[T(U), T(U)], R] \subset T(U)$.

Proof. The proofs are all of the same spirit, making use of the Jacobi identity.

Now 
\[
[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}], \mathrm{R}] \subset [[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{R}], \mathrm{U}] + [\mathrm{U}, [\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{R}]] + [\mathrm{U}, [\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]] \subset \mathrm{C} \mathrm{U}
\]
hence 
\[
[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}] \subset \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}).
\]
Also 
\[
[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})], \mathrm{R}] \subset [[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{R}], \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})] + [\mathrm{U}, [\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})]] \subset \mathrm{U}
\]
by definition of \(\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})\), so 
\[
[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})] \subset \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}).
\]
Now 
\[
[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})] \subset \mathrm{U}
\]
so (3) follows from (2). Finally, 
\[
[[\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})], \mathrm{R}] \subset [\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), [\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{R}]] \subset [\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{U}] \subset \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})
\]
by (2). This proves the lemma.

Let 
\[
A = [\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})].
\]
We prove:

**LEMMA 1.7.**  
\[
\mathrm{R}[\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{A}] \mathrm{R} \subset \mathrm{U} + \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}).
\]

**Proof.**  
Let \(\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b} \in \mathrm{A}\); then for \(\mathrm{r} \in \mathrm{R}\),
\[
[\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{b}] \mathrm{r} = [\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{br}] - \mathrm{b}[\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{r}].
\]
The first term is in \(\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})\) by part 4 of Lemma 1.6, as is \([\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{r}]\). Since \(\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})\) is a subring of \(\mathrm{R}\), \(\mathrm{b}[\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{r}] \in \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})\), hence 
\[
[\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{A}] \mathrm{R} \subset \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}).
\]
Similarly, 
\[
\mathrm{R}[\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{A}] \subset \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}).
\]
Now 
\[
\mathrm{R}[\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{A}] \mathrm{R} \subset [\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{R}] + [\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{A}] \mathrm{R} \subset \mathrm{U} + \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}).
\]

**THEOREM 1.9.** Let $R$ be a simple ring and let $\mathrm{U} \neq \{\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}\}$ be a Lie ideal of $\{\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}\}$. Suppose $R$ is not four-dimensional over a field of characteristic 2. Then

$$
[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}], [\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}]] = \{0\}.
$$

**Proof.** Since $\mathrm{U} \neq \{\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}\}$, $\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}) \subseteq Z(\{\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}\})$, for otherwise $\mathrm{U} \supseteq [\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{R}] \supseteq [[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}], \mathrm{R}] = \{\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}\}$ by Theorem 1.8. Now

$$
[\mathrm{U} + \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{U} + \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})] \subseteq [\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}] + [\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})] + [\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})] \subseteq \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}),
$$

by Lemma 1.6. Since $\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}) \subsetneq \{\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}\}$, we must have that $\mathrm{U} + \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}) \neq \mathrm{R}$. However, by the previous lemma, $\mathrm{R}[A, A] \subseteq \mathrm{U} + \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$ and is an ideal of $\mathrm{R}$; since $\mathrm{U} + \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}) \neq \mathrm{R}$, we get that $\mathrm{R}[A, A] = \{0\}$, hence $[A, A] = \{0\}$. Since $[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}] \subseteq \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})$ and $A = [\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U}), \mathrm{T}(\mathrm{U})]$, we get the theorem.

---

**LEMMA 1.8.** If $R$ is a simple ring, $\lambda \neq \{0\}$ a left ideal of $R$, then $\lambda + \{\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}\} = \mathrm{R}$.

**Proof.** Clearly $\lambda + \{\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}\} \subseteq \lambda + [\lambda, \mathrm{R}]$; if $a \in \lambda$, $r \in \mathrm{R}$, then $\mathrm{ar} = (\mathrm{ar} - \mathrm{ra}) + \mathrm{ra} \in \lambda + [\lambda, \mathrm{R}]$, that is $\mathrm{R} = \lambda + [\lambda, \mathrm{R}]$.

---

**LEMMA 1.9.** If $\mathrm{U} \neq \{0\}$ is a Lie ideal of $\{\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}\}$, $R$ simple, then $\mathrm{xU} = \{0\}$ implies $\mathrm{x} = 0$.

**Proof.** Let $\lambda = \{x \in R \mid x \cdot U = \{0\}\}$; clearly, $\lambda$ is a left ideal of $R$. If $\lambda \neq \{0\}$, then by the previous lemma $R = \lambda + [R, R]$. Let $x \in \lambda$, $a \in [R, R]$, $u \in U$; then $(x a - a x) \cdot u = x \cdot a \cdot u = x \cdot (a \cdot u - u \cdot a) = 0$, that is, $[\lambda, [R, R]] \subseteq \lambda$. Since $\lambda$ is a left ideal of $R$, this implies that $\lambda \cdot [R, R] \subseteq \lambda$, hence $R = \lambda \cdot R = \lambda \cdot (\lambda + [R, R]) \subseteq \lambda$, forcing $R \cdot U = \{0\}$. This contradicts that $U \neq \{0\}$, so we must conclude that $\lambda = \{0\}$.

We now prove the basic **Theorem 1.10**.

**Theorem 1.10.** Let $R$ be a simple ring and $U$ a Lie ideal of $[R, R]$ such that $[U, U] = \{0\}$. Then:

1. If the characteristic of $R$ is not $2$, $U \subset Z$, where $Z$ is the center of $R.
2. If the characteristic of $R$ is $2$, then for every $u \in U$, $u^{2} \in Z$.

**Proof.** Let $u \neq 0 \in U$ and let $d(x)$ be defined by $d(x) = u \cdot x - x \cdot u$ for $x \in R$.

By assumption, if $x, y \in R$, then since $[u, [x, y]] \in U$, $d^{2}([x, y]) = 0$. In this last relation, replace $y$ by $z \cdot v$ with $v \in U$. Using Leibniz's rule, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}
0 = & \ d^{2}([x, z \cdot v]) = d^{2}([x, z]) \cdot v + z \cdot d^{2}([x, v]) \\
= & \ d^{2}(z) \cdot [x, v] + 2 \cdot d(z) \cdot d([x, v]) + z \cdot d^{2}([x, v]) \\
= & \ d^{2}(z) \cdot [x, v] + 2 \cdot d(z) \cdot d([x, v]).
\end{aligned}
$$

If $x \in [R, R]$, then $[x, v] \in U$, hence $d([x, v]) = 0$. Thus, $d^{2}(z) \cdot [U, [R, R]] = 0$ for all $z \in R$. Since $[U, [R, R]]$ is a Lie ideal of $[R, R]$, if it were not equal to $(0)$, we would conclude by Lemma 1.9 that $d^{2}(z) = 0$ for all $z \in R$ because $d^{2}$ annihilates Lie ideals.

On the other hand, if $[U, [R, R]] = (0)$, by Lemma 1.5, $U$ would be contained in the center $Z$. So we may suppose that $d^{2}(z) = 0$ for all $z \in R$. That is, $u \in U$ commutes with all $u x - x u$. By the sublemma following Lemma 1.3, we get that if the characteristic of $R$ is not 2, then $U \subset Z$. If the characteristic is 2, then from $u x + x u = (u x + x u) u$, we get $u^{2} x = x u^{2}$ for all $x \in R$, and so $u^{2} \in Z$. This proves the theorem.

We sharpen the last theorem to:

### Theorem 1.11
If $R$ is simple of characteristic not 2 and if $U$ is a Lie ideal of $[R, R]$ such that $[U, U] \subset Z$, then $U \subset Z$.

#### Proof
If $[U, U] = (0)$, then by Theorem 1.10, we are done. So suppose that there are $u, v \in U$ such that $\alpha = [u, v] \neq 0$. By assumption, $\alpha \in Z$. Let $d(x) = u x - x u$ for $x \in R$. By assumption, $d(v) = a$, where $a \neq 0$ and $a \in Z$. Since $d^{2}(z) = 0$ for all $z \in R$, it follows that $d$ is a derivation. Thus, $d$ annihilates $[R, R]$, and $a$ commutes with all elements of $R$. This implies $a \in Z$, completing the proof.

Let $R$ be a ring, $Z$ its center, and $U$ a Lie ideal of $[R, R]$. Suppose $d$ is a derivation on $R$, satisfying the properties of linearity and the Leibniz rule: for all $x, y \in R$, $d(x + y) = d(x) + d(y)$ and $d(xy) = d(x)y + xd(y)$. Assume further that $d^2(x) = 0$ for all $x \in R$. Then $d^2(xv) = 0$. Expanding, using $d^2(v) = 0$, we get 
\[
0 = d^2(x)v + 2d(x)d(v) = 2\alpha d(x),
\]
where $\alpha = d(v)$. Since $2\alpha \neq 0$ and $\alpha \in Z$, it follows that $d(x) = 0$ for all $x \in R$. This contradicts the assumption that $d(v) = \alpha \neq 0$. 

We claim that $d^2(x) \notin Z$ for all $x \in R$ is also impossible. Suppose $d^2(y) = \beta \neq 0$ with $\beta \in Z$. Then 
\[
d^2(uy) = u d^2(y) = u\beta \in Z,
\]
which implies $u \in Z$.

Now, pick $x \in R$ such that $d^2(x) \notin Z$. Since $U$ is a Lie ideal of $[R, R]$, $d^2(x) \in U$. Hence 
\[
[u, d^2(x)] = y \in [U, U].
\]
Similarly, 
\[
[v, d^2(ux)] = \delta \in Z.
\]
Expanding, we have 
\[
\delta = [v, d^2(ux)] = [v, u d^2(x)] = [v, u] d^2(x) + u[v, d^2(x)] = -\alpha d^2(x) + u\delta.
\]
Since $\alpha \neq 0$, we deduce that 
\[
d^2(x) = \mu + \lambda u,
\]
where $\mu, \lambda \in Z$ and $d^2(x) \notin \langle z \rangle$, with $z \in Z$ and $\lambda \neq 0$. Similarly, 
\[
d^2(ux) = \mu_1 + \lambda_1 u_1, \quad \mu_1, \lambda_1 \in Z.
\]
Expanding further, 
\[
\mu_1 + \lambda_1 u = d^2(ux) = u d^2(x) = u(\mu + \lambda u) = \lambda u^2 + \mu u.
\]
Since $\lambda \neq 0$, we obtain the quadratic equation 
\[
u^2 + \sigma u + \tau = 0,
\]
for some $\sigma, \tau \in Z$.

Thus, 
\[
0 = [u^2 + \sigma u + \tau, v] = \{u^2, v\} + \sigma[u, v] = u(uv - vu) + (uv - vu)u + \alpha\sigma z + \alpha\sigma.
\]
Since $2\alpha \neq 0$, this leads to the contradiction $u \in Z$. 

Thus, the theorem is proved, and $U \subset Z$.

We are now in a position to prove one of the most fundamental theorems in this area of results, namely:

**THEOREM 1.12.** Let \( R \) be a simple ring of characteristic not 2; then any Lie ideal of \([R, R]\) is either \([R, R]\) or is contained in the center of \( R \).

**Proof.** Let \( U \neq [R, R] \) be a Lie ideal of \([R, R]\). According to Theorem 1.9,  
\[
[[[U, U], [U, U]], [U, U], [U, U]] = (0),
\]  
hence by Theorem 1.10,  
\[
[[U, U], [U, U]] \subset Z.
\]  
By Theorem 1.11,  
\[
[U, U] \subset Z,
\]  
and so again by use of the same theorem,  
\[
U \subset Z.
\]

We now consider the case of characteristic 2. Suppose that \( U \) is a Lie ideal of \([R, R]\) such that \([U, U] \subset Z\). Given \( u \in U \), \( x \in [R, R] \), then  
\[
u(ux + xu) + (ux + xu)u \in Z,
\]  
that is,  
\[
u^2x + xu^2 \in Z.
\]  
Let  
\[
W = \{a \in R \mid [a, [R, R]] \subset Z\};
\]  
\( W \) is a Lie ideal of \( R \), so if  
\[
\operatorname{dim}(R / Z) > 4,
\]  
by Theorem 1.5, \( W \subset Z \) or \( W \supset [R, R] \). However, if \( W \supset [R, R] \), then  
\[
[[R, R], [R, R]] \subset Z,
\]  
so by Theorem 1.8,  
\[
[R, R] \subset Z.
\]  
Thus, since \( U \subset [R, R] \), we have \( U \subset Z \). If \( W \subset Z \), then \( a \in U \) implies \( a^2 \in Z \) since \( a^2 \in W \).

If $a \in U$ and $x \in R$ then $a(x(ax)+(ax)x)+(x(ax)+(ax)x)a \in U$, that is, $(ax)^{2}+a^{2}x^{2}+(xa)^{2}+ax^{2}a \in U$. But since $a^{2} \in Z$, $(ax)^{2}+a^{2}x^{2}+(xa)^{2}+ax^{2}a=(ax+xa)^{2}$; since $(ax+xa)^{2} \in U$ then $(ax+xa)^{4} \in Z$ for all $x \in R$. By Theorem 1.4, if $\operatorname{dim} R / Z > 4$, this forces $a \in Z$, that is $U \subseteq Z$. We have sharpened Theorem 1.12 to its definitive form.

**THEOREM 1.13.** If $R$ is a simple ring and if $U$ is a Lie ideal of $[R, R]$, then $U = [R, R]$ or $U \subseteq Z$, except if $R$ is of characteristic 2 and 4-dimensional over $Z$.

If $T$ is an additive subgroup of $R$ such that $[T,[R, R]] \subseteq T$, even if $T \not\subseteq [R, R]$, we can still pin it down tightly as we see in:

**THEOREM 1.14.** If $R$ is a simple ring and if $[T,[R, R]] \subseteq T$, then either $T \subseteq Z$ or $T \supseteq [R, R]$, except if $R$ is of characteristic 2 and 4-dimensional over $Z$.

**Proof.** Let $S = [T,[R, R]]$; from the assumption on $T$, $S$ is a Lie ideal of $[R, R]$. Therefore, by Theorem 1.13, either $S \subseteq Z$ or $S \supseteq [R, R]$. In the second possibility, since $T \supseteq S$, we get that $T \supseteq [R, R]$.

If $[T,[R, R]]=S \subset Z$ let $W=\{x \in R \mid [x,[R, R]] \subset Z\}$. As is easily verified, $W$ is a Lie ideal of $R$, hence $W \subset Z$ or $W \supset [R, R]$. Since, as we have seen several times, $[R, R] \subset \frac{R}{Z}$ and since $[[R, R],[R, R]]=[R, R]$, we obtain that $W \supset [R, R]$. Thus we are forced to conclude that $W \subset Z$. Since $T \subset W$, we get the desired conclusion $T \subset Z$.

We now show a general construction of a Lie ideal in any ring. We shall use this to obtain a theorem of Amitsur about subspaces of a simple ring invariant with respect to a suitable set of automorphisms.

**LEMMA 1.10.** In any ring $R$, the additive subgroup $E$ of $R$ generated by the idempotents of $R$ is a Lie ideal of $R$.

**Proof.** Let $e$ be any idempotent in $R$ and $x$ any element of $R$. Then, as is quickly checked, $f_{1}=e+x e-e x e$ and $f_{2}=e+e x-e x e$ are idempotents. Hence $x e - e x = f_{1} - f_{2}$ is in the additive group $E$ generated by the idempotents. This proves the lemma.

**COROLLARY.** Let $R$ be a simple ring having an idempotent $e \neq 0, 1$. Then $E \supset [R, R]$.

### Proof

Since the center, $Z$, of $R$ is $(0)$ or a field, $e \notin Z$, hence $E \notin Z$. But by Lemma $1.10$, $E$ is a Lie ideal of $R$, hence $E \supseteq [R, R]$ unless $R$ is 4-dimensional over $Z$ and $Z$ is of characteristic 2 (Theorem 1.13). If $\operatorname{dim}(R / Z) = 4$ and $Z$ is of characteristic 2, since $R$ is simple and has a non-trivial idempotent, by Wedderburn's theorem $R \cong M_2(Z)$. But then $[R, R]$ consists of the matrices of trace 0, that is, matrices of the form 

$$
\begin{pmatrix}
\alpha & \beta \\
\gamma & -\alpha
\end{pmatrix}, \quad \alpha, \beta, \gamma \in Z.
$$

Moreover, 

$$
\begin{pmatrix}
\alpha & \beta \\
\gamma & -\alpha
\end{pmatrix} = e_{1} + e_{2} + e_{3} + e_{4},
$$ 

where the $e_{i}$ are the following idempotents:

$$
\begin{aligned}
e_{1} &= 
\begin{pmatrix}
1 & 0 \\
0 & 0
\end{pmatrix}, \quad 
e_{2} = 
\begin{pmatrix}
0 & 1 \\
0 & 0
\end{pmatrix}, \\
e_{3} &= 
\begin{pmatrix}
0 & 0 \\
1 & 0
\end{pmatrix}, \quad 
e_{4} = 
\begin{pmatrix}
0 & 0 \\
0 & 1
\end{pmatrix}.
\end{aligned}
$$

This establishes the corollary.

---

### Corollary

Let $R$ be a simple ring having an idempotent $e \neq 0, 1$. Then $[E, R] \supseteq [R, R]$.

Proof. If $R$ is not 4-dimensional over a field of characteristic 2, since $E \supset [R, R]$, $[E, R] \supset [[R, R], R]$ and $[[R, R], R] = [R, R]$ by Theorem 1.8. If $R$ is 4-dimensional over a field of characteristic 2, since $R = Z_2$ and, by a direct computation, $[[R, R], R] = [R, R]$, the fact that $E \supset [R, R]$ implies $[E, R] \geq [R, R]$.

Given $a \in R$, $a^2 = 0$, the mapping

$$
x \mapsto (1 + a)x(1 - a) = x + ax - xa - axa
$$

is an automorphism of $R$. We call such an automorphism a special inner automorphism of $R$.

If $R$ is a simple ring, it is an algebra over its centroid, which is a field; if, in addition, $R$ has a unit element, its centroid coincides with $Z$ (see (N), p.53-54). By a subspace of $R$, we shall mean a subset of $R$ which is a vector space over the centroid of $R$.

We now prove:

**THEOREM 1.15.** Let $R$ be a simple ring whose centroid $F$ is not $GF(2)$; suppose further that $R$ has an idempotent $e \neq 0, 1$. Then any subspace of $R$ invariant under all special inner automorphisms of $R$ must be $(0)$, $Z$, or contain $[R, R]$. The only subalgebras of $R$ invariant under all special inner automorphisms of $R$ are $(0)$, $Z$, and $R$.

Proof: Let $W$ be a subspace invariant under all special inner automorphisms of $R$. Given $a \in R$ with $a^{2} = 0$ and $w \in W$, then $(1 + a)w(1 - a) = w + aw - wa - awa \in W$, hence $aw - wa - awa \in W$. Let $\alpha \in F$ with $\alpha^{2} \neq \alpha$ (where $F$ is a field); since $(\alpha a)^{2} = 0$, we get $\alpha aw - w(\alpha a) - (\alpha aw(\alpha a)) \in W$, that is, $\alpha(aw - wa) - \alpha^{2} awa \in W$. Eliminating with the above yields $(\alpha^{2} - \alpha)(aw - wa) \in W$, and since $\alpha^{2} - \alpha \neq 0$ in $F$, we end up with $aw - wa \in W$.

If $e$ is an idempotent in $R$ (i.e., $e^{2} = e$), then $a = xe - exe$ satisfies $a^{2} = 0$ for all $x \in R$, so $(xe - exe)w - w(xe - exe) \in W$. Similarly, $(ex - exe)w - w(ex - exe) \in W$. The net result of these is that $(ex - xe)w - w(ex - xe) \in W$, which is to say, $[[E, R], W] \subset W$, where $E$ is the set of idempotents in $R$. Since $[E, R] \supset [R, R]$ by the corollary to Lemma 1.10 (which states that $[E, R]$ contains all commutators of $R$), we get $[[R, R], W] \subset W$. Hence, if $R$ is not 4-dimensional over a field of characteristic 2, by Theorem 1.14 (which establishes the structure of $R$ in this case), we get $W \subset Z$ or $W \subset [R, R]$. Since $W$ is a subspace over the centroid (the center of $R$), $W \subset Z$ implies $W = \{0\}$ or $W = Z$.

So if $R$ is not 4-dimensional over $\mathbb{Z}$ of characteristic 2, we are done. Suppose that $R$ is 4-dimensional over $\mathbb{Z}$; since $R$ has a non-trivial idempotent, $R$ is not a division ring. In this case, $R \approx M_2(\mathbb{Z})$, the ring of $2 \times 2$ matrices over $\mathbb{Z}$. Note that $\mathbb{Z}$ is the ring of integers, which is distinct from $\mathrm{GF}(2)$, the finite field with two elements. Here, $F = \mathbb{Z}$, and $\mathrm{WC}(M_2(\mathbb{Z}))$ is invariant under all inner automorphisms of $M_2(\mathbb{Z})$. 

Let $W$ be a subset of $M_2(\mathbb{Z})$. Given 

\[
a = \begin{pmatrix} \alpha & \beta \\ \gamma & \delta \end{pmatrix} \in W,
\]

we consider the standard basis elements of $M_2(\mathbb{Z})$: 

\[
e_{11} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}, \, e_{12} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}, \, e_{21} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}, \, e_{22} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.
\]

Then, $1 + a e_{12} \in W$ and 

\[
a + e_{12} a + a e_{12} + e_{12} a e_{12} \in W,
\]

so that 

\[
e_{12}^{a e_{12}} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & y \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix} \in W.
\]

If $y \neq 0$, we get $e_{12} \in W$, from which $e_{21} \in W$ follows by applying the inner automorphism induced by 

\[
\begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}.
\]

Consequently, 

\[
e_{11} + e_{22} = e_{12} e_{21} + e_{21} e_{12} \in W.
\]

But then $W \supset [R, R]$. 

If $W \neq [R, R]$, we must assume 

\[
\begin{pmatrix} \alpha & \beta \\ \gamma & \delta \end{pmatrix} \in W
\]

forces $\gamma = 0$, and similarly $\beta = 0$. That is, $W$ consists of diagonal matrices. If 

\[
\begin{pmatrix} \alpha & 0 \\ 0 & \delta \end{pmatrix} \in W,
\]

with $\alpha \neq \delta$, then

$$
\left(\begin{array}{cc}
1 & 1 \\
0 & 1
\end{array}\right)
\left(\begin{array}{cc}
a & 0 \\
0 & 6
\end{array}\right)
\left(\begin{array}{cc}
1 & 1 \\
0 & 1
\end{array}\right)
=
\left(\begin{array}{cc}
\alpha & 6 \\
0 & \delta
\end{array}\right)
\left(\begin{array}{cc}
1 & 1 \\
0 & 1
\end{array}\right)
=
\left(\begin{array}{cc}
\alpha & \alpha+\delta \\
0 & \delta
\end{array}\right)
$$

Here, \( \alpha \) and \( \delta \) are elements of the ring \( R \), and the matrices represent transformations in \( R \). This equation is in \( W \), forcing \( \alpha + \delta = 0 \), that is, \( \alpha = -\delta \). Thus, if \( W \subseteq [R, R] \), then \( W = Z \), where \( Z \) is the center of \( R \).

If \( W \) is, in addition, a subring of \( R \) being \( (0) \), \( Z \), or containing \( [R, R] \), then in light of the corollary to Theorem 1.5, \( W \) is \( (0) \), \( Z \), or \( R \).

This theorem has many applications of a certain form; for instance, let \( M \) be the additive subgroup generated by all elements of square 0. Then \( M \) is invariant under all automorphisms, so in the presence of an idempotent, \( M \subseteq [R, R] \). Similarly, this holds for sums of invertible elements, quasi-regular elements, and other similar subsets.

If one wanted to study the existence or nature of a simple nil ring—and in light of Golod's recent results, this looks likely, at least when this simple ring has an infinite centroid—one can easily show that the subring generated by the elements of square 0 is a Lie ideal of \( R \), so it must be \( R \) itself. Thus, to investigate the local finiteness of \( R \), one could content oneself with studying the subring generated by a finite number of elements whose square is 0.

# Theorem 1.15 Revisited

Let's return to Theorem 1.15 for a moment. If $R$ is simple without a non-trivial idempotent, the result may be false as we show by an example.

## Example

Let $F$ be a field of characteristic 0 and $I$ a non-trivial derivation of $F$ into itself, that is:  
$$(\alpha+\beta)' = \alpha' + \beta',$$  
$$(\alpha \beta)' = \alpha' \beta + \alpha \beta'.$$  

Consider all polynomials $F[t]$ in $t$, where we suppose that for $\alpha \in F$, $\alpha t - t \alpha = \alpha^f$. It is easy to show that $F[t]$ is a simple, non-commutative integral domain. Now, the only invertible elements in $F[t]$ are precisely the non-zero elements of $F$, so that $F$ is invariant under all automorphisms of $F[t]$. Yet, $F \not\subset Z$, nor does $F = F[t]$.

## Theorem Discussion

One might ask: can we weaken the hypothesis of Theorem 1.15 as follows? Let $R$ be a simple ring with zero divisors. Then, is a subring invariant under all automorphisms in the center or all of the ring? 

Sasiada, in an unpublished result, has shown that simple rings with zero divisors can have proper subrings, not in the center, invariant under all automorphisms. In fact, in his example, such a subring can be chosen to consist of all the nilpotent elements in the ring.

## Bibliography

*No references provided.*

1. S. A. Amitsur, "Derivations in simple rings," *Proc. London Math. Soc.* **7** (1957), pp. 87-112.  
2. S. A. Amitsur, "Invariant submodules of simple rings," *Proc. Amer. Math. Soc.* **7** (1958), pp. 987-989.  
3. W. Baxter, "Lie simplicity of a special class of associative rings," *Proc. Amer. Math. Soc.* **7** (1958), pp. 855-863.  
4. L. N. Herstein, "On the Lie and Jordan rings of a simple associative ring," *Amer. Journal of Math.* **77** (1955), pp. 279-285.  
5. L. N. Herstein, "The Lie ring of a simple associative ring," *Duke Math. Journal* **22** (1955), pp. 471-476.  
6. L. N. Herstein, "Lie and Jordan structures in simple associative rings," *Bull. Amer. Math. Soc.* **67** (1961), pp. 517-531.  

**DEFINITION.**  
The involution is of the first kind if it preserves the center and is symmetric; the involution is of the second kind if it reverses the center and is symmetric.  

A study of the Lie and Jordan structure of simple rings with involutions of the second kind is easy, and we shall dispose of these soon. Involutions of the first kind offer much deeper difficulties.  

Let us recall at this point that in Theorem 1.6 we showed that in a simple ring with involution whose dimension over its center exceeds 4, the subring generated by the symmetric elements is the ring itself.  

**Notation.**  
If $A$ is a subset of $R$, then $\overline{A}$ will denote the subring of $R$ generated by $A$.

Let $R$ have an involution of the second kind (we do not restrict the characteristic of $R$), and let $\mathrm{F} = \{a \in Z \mid a^{*} = a\}$. Then $[Z : \mathrm{F}] = 2$, and it is easy to verify that $R \otimes_{\mathrm{F}} Z$ is a direct sum of two simple rings and that the involution on $R \otimes_{\mathrm{F}} Z$ interchanges these simple rings. The Lie and Jordan results in $S$ and $K$ can be obtained from the results developed in Chapter 1 on simple rings without involution. However, we choose to follow a different path, working in $R$ itself.

Let $R$ be simple, of arbitrary characteristic, having an involution of the second kind. Let $Z_S = Z \cap S$. Then it follows that $[Z : Z_S] = 2$ and $Z = Z_S(\alpha)$. From this, it is immediate that $R = S + \alpha S$.

If the characteristic of $R$ is not 2, let $U$ be a Jordan ideal of $S$; then we claim that $U + ZU$ is a Jordan ideal of $R$, as can immediately be verified. So, if $U \neq \{0\}$, then $U + ZU = R$. But then $Z_S U = S$, on comparing $R$ with $S + Z_S$, hence $U \supset S \circ U = \left(Z_S S\right) \circ U = S \circ \left(Z_S U\right) = S \circ S$, that is, $U \supset S \circ S$. Since $R = R \circ R$ ($R$ is simple as a Jordan ring), and $S \circ S + \alpha S \circ S = R \circ R = R$, where $\alpha$ is a scalar, we get $U = S$.

If, in the case of arbitrary characteristic for $R$, $V$ is a Lie ideal of $[K, K]$, then, as can be verified, since $[R, R] = [K, K] + \alpha [K, K]$, and since $V + ZV$ is a Lie ideal of $[R, R]$, by Theorem 1.14 (which states that every Lie ideal of $[R, R]$ is either contained in $Z$ or equals $[R, R]$), if $R$ is not 4-dimensional over $Z$ (where $Z$ has characteristic 2), then either $V + ZV \subset Z$ or $V + ZV = [R, R]$.

The first possibility implies that $VCZ$, the second that $\mathrm{V}+\alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}=[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$. Since $[[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}], [\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]]=[\mathrm{R}, \mathrm{R}]$, it follows that $[[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}], [\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]]=[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$ and so 

$$
\left[\mathrm{V}+\alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}, \mathrm{V}+\alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}\right]=[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}].
$$

Thus, 

$$
[\mathrm{V}, \mathrm{V}]+\left[\mathrm{V}, \alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}\right]+\left[\alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}, \alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}\right] = [\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}],
$$ 

and so 

$$
\mathrm{V} \supset [\mathrm{V}, [\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]] \supset [\mathrm{V}, \mathrm{V}]+\left[\mathrm{V}, \alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}\right]+\left[\mathrm{V}, \alpha^{2} \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}\right] \supset [\mathrm{V}, \mathrm{V}]+\left[\mathrm{V}, \alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}\right] +\left[\alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}, \alpha \mathrm{Z}_{\mathrm{S}} \mathrm{V}\right] = [\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}].
$$

We have proved:

**THEOREM 2.1.** Let $R$ be a simple ring having an involution of the second kind. Then

1. If the characteristic of $R$ is not 2, then $S$ is a simple Jordan ring.

2. Any Lie ideal of $[K, K]$ is either in $Z$ or is $[K, K]$, except if $R$ is of characteristic 2 and is 4-dimensional over its center.

3. Any Lie ideal of $K$ either is in $Z$ or contains $[K, K]$, except if $R$ is of characteristic 2 and is 4-dimensional over its center.

We leave the proof of part 3 to the reader.

With this theorem out of the way, we can focus our attention on the case of involutions of the first kind. While many of the results to follow hold irrespective of the nature of the involution, we henceforth assume in this chapter that $R$ is a simple ring of characteristic not 2 having an involution of the first kind.

We have already seen in Theorem 1.6 that $\overline{\mathrm{S}} = \mathrm{R}$ for most simple rings. We now wish to establish its companion theorem, namely, that $\overline{\mathrm{K}} = \mathrm{R}$ in general. To do so, we first show another construction, in most rings with involution, of a Lie ideal of the ring.

**DEFINITION**: $K^{2}$ is the additive group generated by all $k_{1} k_{2}$, with $k_{1}, k_{2} \in K$.

**LEMMA 2.1**: Let $R$ be any ring with involution such that $R = S + K$. Then $K^{2}$ is a Lie ideal of $R$.

Proof. Let $k_{1}, k_{2} \in K$ and $k \in K$. Then $\left(k_{1} k_{2}\right) k - k\left(k_{1} k_{2}\right) = \left(k_{1} k - k k_{1}\right) k_{2} + k_{1}\left(k_{2} k - k k_{2}\right) \in K^{2}$, so $\left[K^{2}, K\right] \subset K^{2}$. On the other hand, if $s \in S$ then $\left(k_{1} k_{2}\right) s - s\left(k_{1} k_{2}\right) = k_{1}\left(k_{2} s - s k_{2}\right) - \left(k_{1} s - s k_{1}\right) k_{2}$, which is in $K^{2}$ since $K \bullet S \subset K$. Thus $\left[K^{2}, S\right] \subset K^{2}$. Since $R = S + K$, we have $\left[K^{2}, R\right] \subset K^{2}$, whence $K^{2}$ is a Lie ideal of $R$.

The lemma enables us to prove:

**THEOREM 2.2.** If $R$ is a simple ring with involution, of characteristic not $2$, then $\bar{K} = R$ provided $\operatorname{dim} R / Z > 4$.

**Proof.** The conditions of Lemma 2.1 hold in $R$, hence $K^{2}$, as a Lie ideal of $R$, by Theorem 1.3, must either contain $[R, R]$ or $K^{2} \subset Z$.

Now, if $K^{2} \supset [R, R]$, then $\bar{K} \supset \overline{K^{2}} \supset [\overline{R}, R] = R$ by the corollary to Theorem 1.3. Suppose then that $K^{2} \subset Z$.

If $a \in K$ is not invertible, then since $aK \subset Z$ and all the nonzero elements of $Z$ are invertible, we must have $aK = \{0\}$. In particular, $a^2 = 0$. If $s \in S$, then $as + sa \in K$, hence $0 = a(as + sa) = a^2s + asa = asa$. Therefore, $aSa = \{0\}$. Together with $aK = \{0\}$, we get $aRa = \{0\}$; consequently, $Ra$ is a nilpotent left ideal, and so $a = 0$. Thus, $a \neq 0$ in $K$ forces $a$ to be invertible. If $b \in K$, then since $ab \in Z$, we get $b \in Za^{-1} = Za$, which is to say $K \subset Za$.

If $s \in S$ commutes with $a$, then $sa \in K \cap Za$, which implies $s \in Z$. Now, if $s \in S$, then $sa + as = \lambda a$, where $\lambda \in Z$. In fact, $\lambda \in Z \cap S$, as $\lambda$ must belong to both $Z$ and $S$ due to the structure of the elements involved. Thus, $\left(s^2 - \lambda s\right)a = a\left(s^2 - \lambda s\right)$; but since $s^2 - \lambda s \in S$ and commutes with $a$, by the above remark, $s^2 - \lambda s \in Z$.

Given $x \in \mathbb{R}, x = s + \alpha a, s \in \mathbb{S}, \alpha \in \mathbb{Z}$. Hence 

$$
x^{2} = s^{2} + \alpha(s a + a s) + \alpha^{2} a^{2} = s^{2} + \lambda \alpha a + \alpha^{2} \sigma
$$ 

where $a s + s a = \lambda a$. Thus 

$$
x^{2} - \lambda x = s^{2} - \lambda s + \alpha^{2} \sigma
$$ 

and since, as we have seen, $s^{2} - \lambda s \in \mathbb{Z}$, we must have $x^{2} - \lambda x \in \mathbb{Z}$. In this way, $\mathbb{R}$ has been shown to be quadratic over $\mathbb{Z}$. By Theorem 2.38 of (N), $\mathbb{R}$ must be at most 4-dimensional over $\mathbb{Z}$. This proves the theorem.

We sharpen this result to get an extension of a result of Baxter.

---

### THEOREM 2.3
Let $\mathbb{R}$ be a ring with involution such that $2 \mathbb{R} = \mathbb{R}$ in which $\overline{\mathbb{K}} = \mathbb{R}$. Then 

$$
\mathbb{S} = \mathbb{K} \circ \mathbb{K};
$$ 

that is, given $s \in \mathbb{S}$, then 

$$
s = \sum a_{i}^{2} - \sum b_{i}^{2}
$$ 

where $a_{i}, b_{i} \in \mathbb{K}$.

---

### Proof
The hypothesis implies that 

$$
2 \mathbb{S} = \mathbb{S}, \quad 2 \mathbb{K} = \mathbb{K}, \quad \text{and} \quad \mathbb{R} = \mathbb{S} \oplus \mathbb{K}.
$$

Let $k_{1}, k_{2} \in \mathbb{K}$; then 

$$
k_{1} k_{2} - k_{2} k_{1} \in \mathbb{K} \quad \text{and} \quad k_{1} k_{2} + k_{2} k_{1} \in \mathbb{K} \circ \mathbb{K},
$$ 

so 

$$
2 k_{1} k_{2} = \left(k_{1} k_{2} - k_{2} k_{1}\right) + \left(k_{1} k_{2} + k_{2} k_{1}\right) \subset \mathbb{K} + \mathbb{K} \circ \mathbb{K}.
$$ 

This gives 

$$
\mathbb{K}^{2} \subset \mathbb{K} + \mathbb{K} \circ \mathbb{K}.
$$ 

We want to show that 

$$
\mathbb{K}^{n} \subset \mathbb{K} + \mathbb{K} \circ \mathbb{K}
$$ 

for any positive integer $n$, so we go by induction on $n$, assuming that 

$$
\mathbb{K}^{n-1} \subset \mathbb{K} + \mathbb{K} \circ \mathbb{K}
$$ 

for $n \geq 3$.

Given $k_{1}, \ldots, k_{n}, K$, then $\left(k_{1} k_{2} - k_{2} k_{1}\right) k_{3} \ldots k_{n}, K^{n-1} \subseteq K + K \cdot K$, as is $k_{2}\left(k_{1} k_{3} - k_{3} k_{1}\right) k_{4} \ldots k_{n}, K + K \cdot K$. Adding, we have 
\[
\left(k_{1} k_{2} k_{3} - k_{2} k_{3} k_{1}\right) k_{4} \ldots k_{n} \in K + K \cdot K.
\]

However, since $k_{1} k_{3} k_{2} + k_{2} k_{3} k_{1} \in K$, 
\[
\left(k_{1} k_{3} k_{2} + k_{2} k_{3} k_{1}\right) k_{4} \ldots k_{n} \in K^{n-2} \cdot K + K \cdot K;
\]
adding this to $\left(k_{1} k_{2} k_{3} - k_{2} k_{3} k_{1}\right) k_{4} \ldots k_{n}$, which we have seen is in $K + K \cdot K$, we obtain 
\[
k_{1}\left(k_{2} k_{3} + k_{3} k_{2}\right) k_{4} \cdots k_{n} \in K + K \cdot K.
\]
Since $k_{1}\left(k_{2} k_{3} - k_{3} k_{2}\right) k_{4} \ldots k_{n} \in K^{n-1} \subseteq K + K \cdot K$, we get 
\[
2 k_{1} k_{2} \ldots k_{n} \in K + K \cdot K,
\]
so that $K^{n} \subseteq K + K \cdot K$, completing the induction.

Since $\sum K^{n} = \bar{K}$ and $\bar{K} = R$, we get $R = K + K \cdot K$. Clearly, the term $K \cdot K(S)$ is incomplete and requires clarification. Assuming $R = K + S$ and $K \cap S = \{0\}$, we conclude that $S = K \cdot K$.

Given $s$ and $\varepsilon = \sum_{i}\left(a_{i} b_{i} + b_{i} a_{i}\right)$ with $a_{i}, b_{i} \in K$, we have 
\[
s = \sum_{i}\left(a_{i} + b_{i}\right)^{2} - \sum_{i} a_{i}^{2} - \sum_{i} b_{i}^{2}.
\]
This proves the theorem completely.

We return to simple rings; for these, this theorem has a very interesting consequence, namely:

**THEOREM 2.4.** If $K$ has only a finite number of elements linearly independent over $\mathbb{Z}$, then $R$ is finite-dimensional over $\mathbb{Z}$; in fact, $\dim_{\mathbb{Z}}(R) \leq n^{2} + n$, where $n$ is the maximal number of independent elements in $K$.

**Proof:** If $k_{1}, \ldots, k_{n}$ is a maximally independent set of elements in $K$ over $\mathbb{Z}$, then since $\mathrm{S} = K \cdot K$, we have that the elements $k_{i} k_{j} + k_{j} k_{i}$ form a spanning set for $\mathrm{S}$ over $\mathbb{Z}$. Thus, $\dim_{\mathbb{Z}}(R) \leq n^{2} + n$.

We now prepare to study the Jordan structure of $\mathrm{S}$. We begin with:

**THEOREM 2.5.** If $\mathrm{U} \neq \{0\}$ is a Jordan ideal of $\mathrm{S}$, then for $u \in \mathrm{U}$, $r, s \in R$, we have $r u^{4} s + s u^{4} r \in \mathrm{U}$.

**Proof:** The proof will consist of breaking $\tau$ and $a$ into their symmetric and skew parts and verifying that in these special instances the theorem holds. We do this in the sequence of three lemmas.

**LEMMA 2.2.** If $x, y \in \mathrm{S}$ and $u \in \mathrm{U}$, then $x u y + y u x \in \mathrm{U}$.

**Proof:** $2 x u x = \{x(x u + u x) + (x u + u x) x\} - \left(x^{2} u + u x^{2}\right)$. Since $x^{2} \in \mathrm{S}$, $x^{2} u + u x^{2} \in \mathrm{U}$; since $x u + u x \in \mathrm{U}$, so is $x(x u + u x) + (x u + u x) x$. Thus, $2 x u x \in \mathrm{U}$. Since $\mathrm{S}$ is closed under scalar multiplication, we get $x u x \in \mathrm{U}$. Linearizing on $x$, we obtain the lemma.

**LEMMA 2.3.** If $s \in \mathrm{S}$, $k \in K$, and $u \in \mathrm{U}$, then $s u^{2} k - k u^{2} s \in \mathrm{U}$.

**Proof:** Since $u^{2} \in \mathrm{U}$ and $\mathrm{U}$ is closed under multiplication and addition, the expression $s u^{2} k - k u^{2} s$ is in $\mathrm{U}$. This follows from the closure properties of $\mathrm{U}$ and the distributive law applied to the elements of $\mathrm{S}$ and $K$.

**Proof.**  
We notice, first of all, that since $uk - ku$ is in $S$,  
$u^{2}k - ku^{2} = u(uk - ku) + (uk - ku)u$ is in $U$.  
Being a Jordan ideal of $S$,  
$s\left(u^{2}k - ku^{2}\right) + \left(u^{2}k - ku^{2}\right)s \in U$.  
That is  

$$
s\left(u^{2}k - ku^{2}\right) + \left(u^{2}k - ku^{2}\right)s = su^{2}k - ku^{2}s - sku^{2} + u^{2}ks \tag{1}
$$  

is in $U$.  

Consider  

$$
k\left(u^{2}s - su^{2}\right) + \left(u^{2}s - su^{2}\right)k = ku^{2}s - su^{2}k - ksu^{2} + u^{2}sk \tag{2}
$$  

Adding (1) and (2), the right sides add up to $u^{2}(ks + sk) - (ks + sk)u$, which, since $ks + sk \in K$, we have seen must be in $U$. Therefore, the sum of the left sides must be in $U$; since the left side of (1) is already in $U$, we get that of (2) must also be.  

Now subtract (1) from (2); doing so, we stay in $U$. The result on the right is $2\left(su^{2}k - ku^{2}s\right) \in U$. Since $2S = S$, this gives $su^{2}k - ku^{2}s \in U$ for all $s \in S$, $k \in K$, $u \in U$, which is the desired result.  

**Variable Definitions:**  
- $S$: A set or space defined in the context of the proof.  
- $U$: A subset or ideal related to $S$.  
- $K$: A subset or space related to $S$ and $U$.

**LEMMA 2.4.** If $a, b \in K$ and $u \in U$, then $a u^{4} b + b u^{4} a \in U$.

**Proof.** Since $b \in K$, we have already seen that $b u^{2} - u^{2} b \in U$, so that $2\left(b u^{2} - u^{2} b\right)^{2} \in U$. Since $2K = K$ (and so $4K = K$), this gives us $\left(b u^{2} - u^{2} b\right)^{2} \in U$. Expanding, we have:

$$
\left(b u^{2} - u^{2} b\right)^{2} = \left(b u^{2}\right) b u^{2} + u^{2}\left(b u^{2}\right) b - u^{2} b^{2} u^{2} - b u^{4} b.
$$

Now, the terms $\left(b u^{2}\right) b u^{2} + u^{2}\left(b u^{2}\right) b \in U$ since $b u^{2} b \in U$ and $2 u^{2} \in U$. By Lemma 2.2, since $2 u^{2} \in U$, it follows that $4 u^{2} b^{2} u^{2} \in U$, whence $u^{2} b^{2} u^{2} \in U$. The upshot of all this is that $b u^{4} b \in U$. Linearizing on $b$, we get the lemma.

---

**Proof of Theorem 2.5.** Given $u \in U$ and $r, s \in R$, then $r = r_{0} + r_{1}$ and $s = s_{0} + s_{1}$ with $r_{0}, s_{0} \in S$ and $r_{1}, s_{1} \in K$. Thus:

$$
\begin{aligned}
r u^{4} s + s^{*} u^{4} r^{*} & = \left(r_{0} + r_{1}\right) u^{4} \left(s_{0} + s_{1}\right) + \left(s_{0} - s_{1}\right) u^{4} \left(r_{0} - r_{1}\right) \\
& = \left(r_{0} u^{4} s_{0} + s_{0} u^{4} r_{0}\right) + \left(r_{0} u^{4} s_{1} - s_{1} u^{4} r_{0}\right) \\
& \quad + \left(r_{1} u^{4} s_{0} - s_{0} u^{4} r_{1}\right) + \left(r_{1} u^{4} s_{1} + s_{1} u^{4} r_{1}\right).
\end{aligned}
$$

Since $4 u^{4} \in U$ and since we have seen that the factor 4 can be eliminated, we obtain the desired theorem as a combination of the previous three lemmas.

We are in a position to prove the basic

**THEOREM 2.6.** The only Jordan ideals of $S$ are $(0)$ and $S$; that is, $S$ is a simple Jordan ring.

**Proof.** Let $U \neq (0)$ be a Jordan ideal of $S$. If $u \in U$, then we have seen that $r u^4 t + t^* u^4 r^* \in U$ for all $r, t \in R$. If $u^4 \neq 0$, then $R u^4 R = R$ and so, given $x \in R$, we have $x = \sum r_i u^4 t_i$. But then $x^* = \sum t_i^* u^4 r_i^*$, leading to $x + x^* = \sum \left(r_i u^4 t_i + t_i^* u^4 r_i^*\right)$, so $x + x^* \in U$. Since $x + x^*$ covers $S$ as $x$ runs over $R$, we get that $U = S$.

Thus if $\mathrm{U} \neq \mathrm{S}$ we must assume that $\mathrm{u}^{4}=0$ for all $\mathrm{u} \in \mathrm{U}$. Given $u \in U$, $r = r_{0} + r_{1}$, $r_{0} \in S$, $r_{1} \in K$, we have 
\[
2\left(u^{2} r + r^{*} u^{2}\right) = 2\left(u^{2} r_{0} + r_{0} u^{2}\right) + 2\left(u^{2} r_{1} - r_{1} u^{2}\right)
\]
is in $U$; but since $\left(u^{2}\right)^{2} = 0$, $4\left(u^{2} r + r^{*} u^{2}\right)^{4} = 0$, we get $\left(u^{2} r\right)^{5} = 0$ for all $u \in U$ and $r \in R$. By Lemma 1.1, we conclude that $u^{2} = 0$ for all $u \in U$. Linearizing, we get that $u v + v u = 0$ for all $u, v \in U$. 

Given $s \in S$, $v = u s + s u \in U$, hence 
\[
0 = u(u s + s u) + (u s + s u) u = 2 u s u
\]
since $u^{2} = 0$. Thus $u s u = 0$ for all $u \in U$, $s \in S$. Given $k \in K$, then $k u k \in S$, hence $u k u k u = 0$. 

For any $r \in R$, $r = r_{0} + r_{1}$ with $r_{0} \in S$, $r_{1} \in K$, whence 
\[
u r u r u = u\left(r_{0} + r_{1}\right) u\left(r_{0} + r_{1}\right) u = u r_{1} u r_{1} u = 0.
\]
That is $(u r)^{3} = 0$ for all $r \in R$. By Lemma 1.1, we conclude that $u = 0$. We have proved that $U = (0)$ or $U = S$; this establishes the theorem.

Having determined the Jordan structure of $S$, we now want to determine the Lie structure of $K$ and of $[K, K]$. We begin with the very easy:

### Lemma 2.5
If $U$ is a Lie ideal of $K$ and if $u \in U$, $s \in S$, then $u^{2} s - s u^{2} \in U$.

#### Proof
To see the result, merely note that $u s + s u \in K$ and 
\[
u^{2} s - s u^{2} = u(u s + s u) - (u s + s u) u.
\]

Motivated by what we did in Chapter 1, we make the

**DEFINITION**: If $U$ is a Lie ideal of $K$, then $T(U) = \{x \in K \mid [x, K] \subset U\}$.

Clearly, $T(U)$ is a Lie ideal of $K$ and contains $U$. We want a closer tie-in between $U$ and $T(U)$. This is given to us by

**LEMMA 2.6**: If $U$ is a Lie ideal of $K$, then $u, v, w \in U$ implies $uvu \in T(U)$ and $uvw + wvu \in T(U)$.

**Proof**: Consider $[uvu, K]$. For $k \in K$,  
$uvu k - k uvu = u(vuk + kuv) - (vuk + kuv)u + vuku - ukuv$.  
Since $vuk + kuv = vuk$, it is in $K$, so its commutator with $u$ is in $U$.  
Since $uku \in K$, $v(uku) - (uku)v \in U$.  
In all, we have shown that $[uvu, K] \in U$, and so $uvu \in T(U)$.  
Linearizing on $U$, we obtain $uvw + wvu \in T(U)$.

We proceed to prove

**THEOREM 2.7**: If $U$ is a Lie ideal of $K$, then for all $u, v \in U$,  
$$
\left(u^{2}v - vu^{2}\right)r - r^{*}\left(u^{2}v - vu^{2}\right) \in T(U) \quad \text{for all } r \in R.
$$

**Proof**: We write $r = s + k$ with $s \in S$, $k \in K$, and verify the theorem for $s$ and $k$.

1. For $s \in S$,  
$$
\left(u^{2}v - vu^{2}\right)s - s\left(u^{2}v - vu^{2}\right) = \left[\left(u^{2}s - su^{2}\right)v - v\left(u^{2}s - su^{2}\right)\right] 
+ \left[\left(u^{2}(vs - sv) - (vs - sv)u^{2}\right)\right].
$$

However, by Lemma 2.5, $u^{2}s - su^{2} \in U$, so the first term is in $U$. Also, since $vs - sv \in S$, by Lemma 2.5 again, the second term is in $U$. Being in $U$, these are certainly in $T(U)$.

2. $\left(u^{2}v - vu^{2}\right)k + k\left(u^{2}v - vu^{2}\right)$

$$
\begin{aligned}
&= \left\{u^{2}(vk + kv) - (vk + kv)u^{2}\right\} + v\left(ku^{2} - u^{2}k\right) + \left(ku^{2} - u^{2}k\right)v \\
&\equiv v\left(ku^{2} - u^{2}k\right) + \left(ku^{2} - u^{2}k\right)v \pmod{U} \quad \text{(by Lemma 2.5 since } vk + kv \in S\text{)} \\
&\equiv v\big((ku - uk)u + u(ku - uk)\big) + \big((ku - uk)u + u(ku - uk)\big)v \pmod{U} \\
&\equiv v\big((ku - uk)u - u(ku - uk)\big) - \big((ku - uk)u - u(ku - uk)\big)v \\
&\quad + 2\big(vu(ku - uk) + (ku - uk)uv\big) \pmod{U} \\
&\equiv 2\big(vu(ku - uk) + (ku - uk)uv\big) \pmod{U}.
\end{aligned}
$$

But by Lemma 2.5, since $ku - uk \in U$, $vu(ku - uk) + (ku - uk)uv$ is in $T(U)$. The upshot of all this is that $\left(u^{2}v - vu^{2}\right)k + k\left(u^{2}v - vu^{2}\right) \in T(U)$. Thus, we indeed have proved the assertion of the theorem.

This theorem enables us to establish the key fact contained in:

**THEOREM 2.8.** If $R$ is simple and $\operatorname{dim} R / Z > 4$ and if $U$ is a Lie ideal of $K$, then either $U \subseteq [K, K]$ or $u^{2}v = vu^{2}$ for all $u, v \in U$.

**Proof.** Let $a = u^{2}v - vu^{2}$ where $u, v \in U$. By the previous theorem, $az - za \in T(U)$ for all $z \in R$.

If $k_{1} \in K$ then $b=\left(a r-r^{*} a\right) k_{1}-k_{1}\left(a r-r^{*} a\right)$ and $U \subset T(U)$. Since 

$$
b=a\left(r k_{1}\right)-\left(r k_{1}\right)^{*} a-r^{*} a k_{1}-k_{1} a r,
$$ 

we end up with 

$$
k_{1} a r+r^{*} a k_{1} \in T(U)
$$ 

for all $r \in R$, $k_{1} \in K$.

We continue in this vein; let $k_{2} \in K$. Then

$$
\left(r^{*} a k_{1}+k_{1} a r\right) k_{2}-k_{2}\left(r^{*} a k_{1}+k_{1} a r\right) \in T(U),
$$

hence

$$
r^{*} a k_{1} k_{2}-\left(r^{*} a k_{1} k_{2}\right)^{*}+k_{1} a\left(r k_{2}\right)+\left(r k_{2}\right)^{*} a k_{1} \in T(U).
$$

And since 

$$
k_{1} a\left(r k_{2}\right)+\left(r k_{2}\right)^{*} a k_{1} \in T(U),
$$ 

we obtain 

$$
r^{*} a k_{1} k_{2}-\left(r^{*} a k_{1} k_{2}\right)^{*} \in T(U).
$$ 

Continuing, we get by induction that for all $\bar{k} \in \bar{K}$, 

$$
r^{*} a \bar{k}-\left(r^{*} a \bar{k}\right)^{*} \in T(U).
$$

Since $\operatorname{dim} \mathrm{R} / \mathrm{Z} > 4$, by Theorem 2.2, $\overline{\mathrm{K}} = \mathrm{R}$; the above then translates to: for all $r, t \in \mathrm{R}$, $r a t - (r a t)^{*} \in T(U)$. Now $\mathrm{RaR}$ is an ideal of $\mathrm{R}$, hence, if $\mathrm{a} \neq 0$, $\mathrm{RaR} = \mathrm{R}$. But then for any $\mathrm{x} \in \mathrm{R}$, $x = \Sigma r_{i} a t_{i}$, whence $x^{*} = \Sigma t_{i}^{*} a r_{i}^{*}$, and so  
$x - x^{*} = \Sigma \left(r_{i} a t_{i} - \left(r_{i} a t_{i}\right)^{*}\right) \in T(U)$. Since $x - x^{*}$ sweeps out $\mathrm{K}$, we have that if $a \neq 0$, then $T(U) \supset \mathrm{K}$. From the definition of $T(U)$, this says that $U \subseteq D[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$. The theorem has now been proved.

**THEOREM 2.9.**  
If $\mathrm{R}$ is simple, $\operatorname{dim} \mathrm{R} / \mathrm{Z} > 4$, and if $U$ is a Lie ideal of $\mathrm{K}$ such that $U \not\subseteq D[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$, then given $u \in U$, $u^{2} \in \mathrm{Z}$.

**Proof.**  
Since $u^{2} v = v u^{2}$ by the previous theorem, $u^{2}$ is in the center of $\bar{U}$, the subring generated by $U$. However, $u^{2} s - s u^{2} \in U$ for $s \in \mathrm{S}$, and $u^{2} k - k u^{2} = u(u k - k u) + (u k - k u) u \in \bar{U}$ for $k \in \mathrm{K}$. Thus, $u^{2}$ commutes with all $u^{2}(s + k) - (s + k) u^{2}, s \in \mathrm{S}, k \in \mathrm{K}$, that is, with all $u^{2} r - r u^{2}, r \in \mathrm{R}$. Since the characteristic is not 2, we have seen in the sublemma in Chapter 1 that this forces $u^{2}$ to be in $\mathrm{Z}$.

**COROLLARY.**  
If $U \subseteq D[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$, then $u v + v u \in \mathrm{Z}$ for all $u, v \in U$.

One could ask: we know $u^{2} \in \mathrm{Z}$ for all $u \in U$; is it possible that $u^{2}=0$ for these $u$'s? This is answered in the following:
