**THEOREM 2.10.** If $R$ is simple, $\operatorname{dim} R / \mathrm{Z} > 4$, and $U$ is a Lie ideal of $K$ such that $u^{2} \in U$ implies $u^{2}=0$, then $U=(0)$.

**Proof.** On linearizing $u^{2}=0$, we get $uv + vu = 0$ for all $u, v \in U$. Thus, $uvu = -vu^{2} = 0$ for any $u, v \in U$. Given $u \in U$, $k \in K$, then  
\[
2uku = (uk - ku)u - u(uk - ku) \quad \text{(since $u^{2}=0$)},
\]  
hence $uku \in U$; but then $uku = 0$. Since $uv = -vu$, we arrive at $uvKuv = (0)$. Now $(uv)^{*} = v^{*}u^{*} = vu = -uv$, that is, $uv \in K$. Thus, for $s \in S$, $suvs \in K$, and so $uvsuv = 0$. Given $r \in R$, $r = s + k$, $s \in S$, $k \in K$, whence $uvruv = 0$. The right ideal $uvR$ is such that every element in it has cube $0$. By Lemma 1.1, this forces $uv = 0$ for all $u, v \in U$. But then for $k \in K$, $u(uk - ku) = 0$, leaving us with $uKu = (0)$. As above, we then get $usu = 0$ for $s \in S$, and so $uR$ is a nil right ideal where every element has cube $0$. The outcome of this is that $u = 0$, that is, $U = (0)$.

Combining Theorems 2.9 and 2.10, we have:

**THEOREM 2.11.** If $R$ is simple and $\mathrm{Z} = (0)$, then any nonzero Lie ideal $U$ of $K$ must contain $[K, K]$.

We henceforth may assume that $Z \neq 0$. We recall the precise situation we are studying: $R$ is simple, of characteristic $\neq 2$, and $Z \neq 0$. $\dim(R / Z) > 4$ and $R$ has an involution of the first kind.

Let $U$ be a Lie ideal of $K$ such that $U \subseteq Z + [K, K]$. According to Theorems 2.9 and 2.10, every element $w \in U$ satisfies $w^{2} \in Z$ and, furthermore, there is an element $u \in U$ such that $u^{2} = \lambda_{1} \neq 0 \in Z$. Since $K$ generates $R$ and $K \cap Z = 0$, $u \neq 0$, so $u$ cannot commute with every element $k \in K$. We claim something more, namely that there is a $k \in K$ such that $(uk - ku)^{2} \neq 0$.

Suppose, then, that $(uk - ku)^{2} = 0$ for all $k \in K$. Linearizing, we obtain

\[
\left(uk_{1} - k_{1}u\right)\left(uk_{2} - k_{2}u\right) + \left(uk_{2} - k_{2}u\right)\left(uk_{1} - k_{1}u\right) = 0 \tag{1}
\]

for all $k_{1}, k_{2} \in K$.  
Since $u^{2} \in Z$, each $uk_{i} - k_{i}u$ anti-commutes with $u$. Hence, $\left(uk_{1} - k_{1}u\right)\left(uk_{2} - k_{2}u\right) - \left(uk_{2} - k_{2}u\right)\left(uk_{1} - k_{1}u\right)$ is both in $U$ and commutes with $u$. By (1), this element equals

\[
w = 2\left(uk_{1} - k_{1}u\right)\left(uk_{2} - k_{2}u\right).
\]

Since it commutes with $u$ and $uw + wu \in Z$, we get $Zuw \in Z$. However, $w$ is not invertible since $uk_{2} - k_{2}u$ is not, so $Zuw$ is not invertible, hence $Zuw = 0$. Because $2u$ is invertible, we end up with $w = 0$.

Let $v = u k_{1} - k_{1} u \neq 0$; then, by the above, $v(u k - k u) = 0$ for all $k \in K$, that is, $v u k = v k u$. Now $k \in K$, hence $v u k u k = v k u k u = v u k^{2} u$ from the above. Also, $v u k u k = v k u^{2} k = v k^{2} u^{2}$; comparing, we have $v k^{2} u^{2} = v u k^{2} u$. But $u$ is invertible, hence $v\left(k^{2} u - u k^{2}\right) = 0$. By Theorem 2.3, the $k^{2}$ additively span $S$, so $v(u s - s u) = 0$ for all $s \in S$. But then $v(u r - r u) = 0$ for all $r \in R$. Given $r, a \in R$, 

\[
0 = v(u r a - r a u) = v(u r - r u) a + v r(u a - a u) = v r(u a - a u),
\]

which is to say, $v R(u a - a u) = 0$. Since $R$ is prime and $v \neq 0$, we get $u a = a u$ for all $a \in R$, therefore $u \in Z$, contrary to $u \notin Z$. 

We have substantiated our claim that there is a $v = u k - k u$ so $v^{2} = \lambda_{2} \neq 0 \in Z$. Notice that $u v + v u = 0$ and $v \in U$. A calculation reveals that 

\[
(u v - v u)^{2} = -4 \lambda_{1} \lambda_{2} \neq 0.
\]

We claim that $u, v, u v - v u$ are linearly independent over $Z$, for if 

\[
w = \alpha_{0} u + \alpha_{1} v + \alpha_{2}(u v - v u) = 0, \quad \alpha_{i} \in Z,
\]

then 

\[
0 = u w + w u = 2 \alpha_{0} \lambda_{1}, \quad 0 = v w + w v = 2 \alpha_{1} \lambda_{2},
\]

so that $\alpha_{0} = \alpha_{1} = 0$, forcing $\alpha_{2} = 0$ in addition.

We further claim that $u, v, uv - vu$ form a basis of $U$ over $Z$. For if there is an $x \in U$ linearly independent of these, since $ux + xu = \alpha$, $vx + xv = \beta$, $\alpha, \beta \in Z$, letting $x^{\prime} = x + \sigma_{1} u + \sigma_{2} v$, $\sigma_{1}, \sigma_{2} \in Z$, a computation shows that  
$x^{\prime} u + u x^{\prime} = \alpha + 2 \sigma_{1} \lambda_{1}$,  
$x^{\prime} v + v x^{\prime} = \beta + 2 \sigma_{2} \lambda_{2}$.  

Since $\lambda_{1} \neq 0$, $\lambda_{2} \neq 0$, we can find $\sigma_{1}, \sigma_{2}$ such that  
$x^{\prime} u + u x^{\prime} = x^{\prime} v + v x^{\prime} = 0$;  
moreover, $x^{\prime}$ is linearly independent of $u, v, uv - vu$.  

We showed that, without loss of generality, there is an $x \in U$ linearly independent of $u, v, uv - vu$ which satisfies  
$x u + u x = x v + v x = 0$.  

Now $x(uv - vu) = (uv - vu)x$, which together with  
$x(uv - vu) + (uv - vu)x \in Z$ forces  
$2x(uv - vu) = Y \in Z$.  

Since $(uv - vu)^{2} = -4 \lambda_{1} \lambda_{2} \in Z$, we get  
$-8 \lambda_{1} \lambda_{2} x = Y(uv - vu)$, forcing $x = \delta(uv - vu)$ with $\delta \in Z$, a contradiction.  

We have shown that any Lie ideal $U \neq (0)$ of $K$ which does not contain $[K, K]$ must be 3-dimensional over $Z$; in fact, $U$ must contain a basis of the form $u, v, uv - vu$, where $u^{2} = \lambda_{1} \neq 0$, $v^{2} = \lambda_{2} \neq 0$, $uv + vu = 0$, and where $\lambda_{1}, \lambda_{2} \in Z$.

Let $W = \{x \in K \mid x u = u x, x v = v x\}$. $W$ clearly is $\{x \in K \mid [x, U] = 0\}$. The Jacobi identity reveals that $W$ is a Lie ideal of $K$. Moreover, since $u v - v u \in W$ and is in $[K, K]$, we must have $W \subseteq Z([K, K])$, where $Z$ denotes the center of the algebra $K$. From the above discussion, either $W = 0$ or $W$ itself is 3-dimensional over $Z$. Knowing the form of $U$, we readily check that $U \cap W = 0$.

Let $s \in S$; we claim that $a = (u s - s u) v + v (u s - s u)$ is in $W$. Clearly, at any rate, it is in $K$; moreover, since $v^{2} \in Z$, $a$ commutes with $v$. But $(u s - s u) v + v (u s - s u) = u (s v - v s) + (s v - v s) u$ since $u v = -v u$, hence $a$ also commutes with $u$. Consequently, $a \in W$.

If $W = 0$, we would have that $(u s - s u) v + v (u s - s u) = 0$ for all $s \in S$. Now $t = v s - s v$ and $S$, therefore $(u t - t u) v + v (u t - t u) = 0$. Since $t$ and $u$ anti-commute with $v$, $u - t u$ commutes with $v$, thus we get $2 (u t - t u) v = 0$. But $v$ is invertible, reducing this to $u t - t u = 0$. However, $u t + t u = u (v s - s v) + (v s - s v) u = 0$, thus $u t = 0$ and so, since $u$ is invertible, $t = 0$. That is, $v \in U$ centralizes all of $S$. But $\bar{S} = R$ (Theorem 1.6), hence $v \in Z$, a contradiction since $u v - v u \neq 0$. Thus $W \neq 0$. By the argument already given, $W$ must be 3-dimensional over $Z$.

We claim that $W \subseteq C[K, K]$. Clearly, $[W, K] \subseteq W$ is contained properly in $[K, K]$ and is a Lie ideal, so it is 3-dimensional over $Z$ or is $(0)$. If $(0)$, then $W$ centralizes $\bar{K} = R$, so $W$ is in $Z$, contrary to $W \cap K = (0)$. Thus, $[W, K]$ is 3-dimensional, so it must be all of $W$.

Now $W \cap U = (0)$ and $W + U \subseteq [K, K]$ is a 6-dimensional Lie ideal of $K$. By the above, not being 3-dimensional, $W + U = [K, K]$.

We claim that $[K, K] = K$. If not, there is an $s \in S$ such that $us + su \notin [K, K]$. For if $us + su \in [K, K]$ for all $s \in S$, since $[u, K] \subset [K, K]$, we would have $ur - (ru)^* = ur + r^*u \in [K, K]$ for all $r \in R$. However, we said earlier that this forces $x - x^* \in [K, K]$ for all $x \in R$, that is, $K = [K, K]$. Hence, there is an $s_0 \in S$ such that $x_1 = us_0 + s_0u \notin [K, K]$. Clearly, since $u^2 \in Z$, $x_1$ commutes with $u$. Thus, $vx_1 - x_1v$ anti-commutes with $u$. Since $v^2 \in Z$, $vx_1 - x_1v$ anti-commutes with $v$. But $vx_1 - x_1v \in U$, so $vx_1 - x_1v = \alpha u + \beta v + y(uv - vu)$ with $\alpha, \beta, y \in Z$. Since it anti-commutes with both $u$ and $v$, we readily see that $\alpha = \beta = 0$. Thus, $vx_1 - x_1v = y(uv - vu)$, and so $x_1 + yu$ commutes with $v$. It commutes with $u$ since $x_1$ does, hence $x_1 + \gamma u \in WC[K, K]$. Therefore, since $u \in [K, K]$, $x_1 \in [K, K]$, contrary to assumption. We are thereby forced to conclude that $[K, K] = K$.

Since $K = [K, K] = U + W$, we have that $K$ is 6-dimensional over $Z$. We know that if $v_{1}, v_{2}, v_{3}$ is a basis of $U$ and $w_{1}, w_{2}, w_{3}$ is a basis of $W$, then $v_{i}v_{j} + v_{j}v_{i}$ and $w_{i}w_{j} + w_{j}w_{i}$ are in $Z$ for any $i, j$. Thus, $K$ is spanned by the 10 elements $1, v_{i}w_{j} + w_{j}v_{i}$ ($i, j = 1, 2, 3$), over $Z$. By Theorem 2.4, $\operatorname{dim}(K) = 5$, hence $S$ is at most 10-dimensional over $Z$. Since $K$ is 6-dimensional over $Z$ and

**Departamento de Matemáticas**

$R = S + K$, we get $\operatorname{dim}(R/Z) \leq 16$. In other words, if $\operatorname{dim}(R/Z) > 16$ and $R$ has an involution of the first kind, then we have proved that any Lie ideal of $K$ must contain $[K, K]$. Knowing the result for involutions of the second kind by Theorem 2.1, we have proved the fundamental:

**Theorem 2.12**  
If $R$ is a simple ring with involution of characteristic not 2 and if $U$ is a Lie ideal of $K$, then either $U \subset Z$ or $U \supset [K, K]$, provided $\operatorname{dim}(R/Z) > 16$.

We show that the result is false in $F_{4}$, where $F$ is a field, and $^T$ denotes the transpose in $F_{4}$. In fact, the set

$$
U = \left\{\begin{pmatrix}
0 & \alpha & \beta & \gamma \\
-\alpha & 0 & -\gamma & -\beta \\
-\beta & \gamma & 0 & \alpha \\
-\gamma & \beta & -\alpha & 0
\end{pmatrix} : \alpha, \beta, \gamma \in F \right\}
$$

is a 3-dimensional Lie ideal of $K$.

We conclude our study of the Lie structure of simple rings by considering the nature of $[K, K]$ as a Lie ring in a simple ring with involution.

In the course of proving Theorem 2.12, we actually proved: 

1. If $U$ is a Lie ideal of $K$ such that $u \in U$ implies $u^{2} \in Z$, then $U = (0)$ or is 3-dimensional over $Z$ with a basis $\{u, v, uv - vu\}$ where $u^{2} = \lambda_{1} \neq 0$, $v^{2} = \lambda_{2} \neq 0$, $\lambda_{1}, \lambda_{2} \in Z$, and $uv + vu = 0$. 

2. For $U$ as in (1), $W = \{x \in K \mid xa = ax \text{ for all } a \in U\}$ is not $(0)$. Since $[W, K] \neq (0)$ (otherwise $W$ would centralize $K$, hence $\overline{K} = R$, forcing $W(Z \cap K) = (0)$), there is an element in $[K, K]$ that centralizes $U$.

From these considerations, we prove:

**THEOREM 2.13.** If $\operatorname{dim} R / Z > 4$, then $[K, K] = R$.

Proof. Let $u \in [K, K]$; then $u^{2}s - su^{2} \in [K, K]$ and $u^{2}k - ku^{2} \in \overline{[K, K]}$ for $s \in S, k \in K$. Thus $u^{2}r - ru^{2} \in \overline{[K, K]}$ for all $r \in R$. But then for $k \in [K, K]$, $\overline{[K, K]} \supset u^{2}kr - kru^{2} = \left(u^{2}k - ku^{2}\right)r + k\left(u^{2}r - ru^{2}\right)$. Since $k\left(u^{2}r - ru^{2}\right) \in \overline{[K, K]}$, we get that $\left(u^{2}k - ku^{2}\right)R \subset \overline{[K, K]}$ for $k \in [K, K]$. Therefore, $\left[[K, K], \left(u^{2}K - Ku^{2}\right)R\right] \subset [K, \overline{[K, K]}] \subset \overline{[K, K]}$, leaving us with $K\left(u^{2}k - ku^{2}\right)R \subset \overline{[K, K]}$. 

Continuing, we get $K^{n}\left(u^{2}k - ku^{2}\right)R \subset \overline{[K, K]}$. Since $\overline{K} = R$ (Theorem 2.2) and $\overline{K} = \bigcup_{n \geq 1} K^{n}$, we obtain $R\left(u^{2}k - ku^{2}\right)R \subset \overline{[K, K]}$. If $\overline{[K, K]} \neq R$, we conclude that $u^{2}k = ku^{2}$ for $k \in [K, K]$, so $u^{2}$ is in the center of $\overline{[K, K]}$. Since $u^{2}r - ru^{2} \in \overline{[K, K]}$, $u^{2}$ must commute with all $r \in R$. By the sublemma in Chapter 1 (stated earlier), $u^{2}$ must be in $Z$. 

By the discussion we gave prior to this theorem, $[K, K]$ must be 3-dimensional over $Z$ and contain a non-zero element which centralizes it. Since we also have a specific basis $u, v, uv - vu$ of $[K, K]$ over $Z$, where $u^{2} = \lambda_{1} \neq 0 \in Z$, $v^{2} = \lambda_{2} \neq 0 \in Z$, and $uv + vu = 0$, we immediately check that there is no non-zero element in $[K, K]$ which centralizes it. Thus, we must conclude that $\overline{[K, K]} = R$.

We assume henceforth that $R$ is more than 16-dimensional over $\mathbb{Z}$.

**LEMMA 2.7.** If $U$ is a non-zero Lie ideal of $[K, K]$, then $\mathrm{xU} = (0)$ implies $\mathrm{x} = 0$.

**Proof.** Let $\mathrm{xU} = (0)$; for $\mathrm{u} \neq 0$ in $U$ and $\mathrm{k} \in [\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$, we have $0 = \mathrm{x}(\mathrm{u} \mathrm{k} - \mathrm{k} \mathrm{u}) = -\mathrm{x} \mathrm{k} \mathrm{u}$, hence $\mathrm{x} [\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}] \mathrm{u} = (0)$. Since $\mathrm{x} [\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}] \mathrm{U} = \langle 0 \rangle$, repeating the above argument yields $\mathrm{x} [\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]^{2} \mathrm{U} = (0), \ldots, \mathrm{x} [\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]^{n} \mathrm{U} = (0)$. Since $\overline{[K, K]} = R$, we get $\mathrm{xRU} = (0)$. Since $\mathrm{U} \neq (0)$ and $R$ is simple, this forces $\mathrm{x} = 0$.

**LEMMA 2.8.** If $\mathrm{U} \neq [\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$ is a Lie ideal of $[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$ for which $\mathrm{u}^{2} \in Z$ for all $\mathrm{u} \in \mathrm{U}$, then $\mathrm{U} = (0)$.

**Proof.** Suppose that $\mathrm{U} \neq (0)$; we claim that there is a $\mathrm{u} \in \mathrm{U}$ with $\mathrm{u}^{2} \neq 0$. If not, $\mathrm{u} \mathrm{v} + \mathrm{v} \mathrm{u} = 0$ for all $\mathrm{u}, \mathrm{v} \in \mathrm{U}$. Since $\mathrm{u} \mathrm{v} - \mathrm{v} \mathrm{u}$ is an element of $\mathrm{U}$, we get $2 \mathrm{u} \mathrm{v} \in \mathrm{U}$. If $\mathrm{w} \in \mathrm{U}$, then $2 (\mathrm{u} \mathrm{v} \mathrm{w} + \mathrm{w} \mathrm{u} \mathrm{v}) = 0$, hence $\mathrm{u} \mathrm{v} \mathrm{w} = -\mathrm{w} \mathrm{u} \mathrm{v}$. But $\mathrm{u}, \mathrm{v}$ anti-commute with $\mathrm{w}$; therefore, $\mathrm{u} \mathrm{v}$ commutes with $\mathrm{w}$; thus $\mathrm{u} \mathrm{v} \mathrm{w} = 0$. Since $\mathrm{u} \mathrm{v} \mathrm{U} = (0)$ by Lemma 2.7, $\mathrm{u} \mathrm{v} = 0$. Since $\mathrm{u} \mathrm{V} = (0)$, we get $\mathrm{u} = 0$, contradicting $\mathrm{U} \neq (0)$. Therefore, there is a $\mathrm{u} \in \mathrm{U}$ with $\mathrm{u}^{2} \neq 0$.

Now, for any $v, w \in U$, $vw + wv \in Z$. Also, for $k \in K$, $U \supset v(vk - kv) - (vk - kv)v$; since $v^{2} \in Z$, $vk - kv$ anti-commutes with $v$ and so $2v(vk - kv) \in U$. Putting $k = vb - bv$, $b \in K$, we get $v^{2}(vb - bv) \in U$. Since for some $u \in U$, $u^{2} \neq 0 \in Z$, then $u^{2}K = K$ and since $u^{2}(ub - bu) \in U$, we see that $ub - bu \in U$ for all $b \in K$. That is, for any $u \in U$ with $u^{2} \neq 0$, then $[u, K] \subset U$. If $u^{2} = 0$, pick $v \in U$ so that $v^{2} \neq 0$. Now, not both $(u + v)^{2} = 0$ and $(u - v)^{2} = 0$, for the two together would imply that $v^{2} = 0$; so one of these, say $u + v$, satisfies $(u + v)^{2} \neq 0$. But then $[u + v, K] \subset U$; since $v^{2} \neq 0$, $[v, K] \subset U$, resulting in $[u, K] \subset U$. All in all, we have shown that $[U, K] \subset U$, hence $U$ is a Lie ideal of $K$. By Theorem 2.12, $U = \{0\}$.

**Lemma 2.9.** If $U$ is a proper Lie ideal of $[K, K]$, then $[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}] = \{0\}$ or $\overline{\mathrm{U}} = \mathrm{R}$.

Proof. Let $a \in [U, U]$; it is immediate that $[a, K] \subset U$. Thus $\left[a^2, K\right] = a[a, K] + [a, K]a \subset \bar{U}$. Also, $\left[a^2, S\right] \subset U$; thus $\left[a^2, r\right] \subset \bar{U}$ for all $r \in R$. If $v \in U$, then $a^2 v r - v r a^2 \in \widetilde{U}$; but $a^2 v r - v r a^2 = \left(a^2 v - v a^2\right)r + v\left(a^2 r - r a^2\right)$. From $v\left(a^2 r - r a^2\right) \in \bar{U}$, we deduce that $\left(a^2 v - v a^2\right) R \subset \bar{U}$. As before, since $\overline{[K, K]} = R$, we get $R\left(a^2 v - v a^2\right) \subset \bar{U}$, so, if $\bar{U} \neq R$, then $a^2 v = v a^2$ for all $v \in U$, from which we see that $a^2$ centralizes $\bar{U}$. However, $a^2 r - r a^2 \in \bar{U}$ for all $r \in R$, so $a^2$ commutes with $r$. From this, $a^2 \in Z$ for all $a \in [U, U]$. By Lemma 2.8, $[U, U] = (0)$.

We dispose of the second possibility of Lemma 2.9 by proving:

**THEOREM 2.14.** If $U$ is a proper Lie ideal of $[K, K]$, then $[[U, U], [U, U]] = (0)$.

Proof. We first make the following remark: if $\overline{\mathrm{B}}=\mathrm{R}$, then $[B, R]=[R, R]$. We prove by induction that $\left[B^{n}, R\right] \subseteq [B, R]$. If $b_{1}, \ldots, b_{n} \in B$ and $r \in R$, where $b_{1}, \ldots, b_{n}$ are elements of $B$ and $r$ is an element of $R$, then:

$$
\begin{aligned}
b_{1} b_{2} \cdots b_{n} r &= b_{1}(b_{2} \cdots b_{n} r) - (b_{2} \cdots b_{n} r)b_{1} \\
&\quad + b_{2} \cdots b_{n}(r b_{1}) - (r b_{1})b_{2} \cdots b_{n} \in [B, R] + [B^{n-1}, R].
\end{aligned}
$$

is contained in $[B, R]$. Since $R = \Sigma B^n$, where $\Sigma$ and $B^n$ represent the summation over $n$ terms of $B$, we have $[B, R] = [R, R]$.

Since $\bar{K} = R$, $[K, R] = [R, R]$. From $R = K + S$, this gives us 
\[
[K, K] + [K, S] = [K, K] + [S, S] + [K, S],
\]
so that $[S, S] \subset [K, K]$, and $[R, R] = [K, K] + [K, S].
\]

Thus, if 
\[
[[U, U], [U, U]] \neq 0,
\]
by Lemma 2.9 (referenced in the text), $\overline{[U, U]} = R$. Hence, 
\[
[[U, U], R] = [R, R] = [K, K] + [K, S].
\]
But 
\[
[[U, U], K + S] = [[U, U], K] + [[U, U], S].
\]
Equating the skew-symmetric and symmetric parts, we see that 
\[
[[U, U], K] = [K, K].
\]
However, by the Jacobi identity (which states $[[X, Y], Z] + [[Y, Z], X] + [[Z, X], Y] = 0$), 
\[
[[U, U], K] \subset U,
\]
leading to the contradiction $U = [K, K]$. 

Therefore, we have proved 
\[
[[U, U], [U, U]] = 0.
\]

The problem of examining the Lie simplicity of $[K, K]$ has been reduced to proving that if $V$ is a Lie ideal of $[K, K]$ for which $[V, V] = (0)$, then $V = (0)$. Were this true, by Theorem 2.14, for any proper Lie ideal $U$ of $[K, K]$, $[[U, U], [U, U]] = (0)$; hence we would get $[U, U] = (0)$ and so $U = (0)$. This explains our concern with Lie ideals $V$ of $[K, K]$ such that $[V, V] = (0)$.

### Remark
**Remark:** If $a, K$ satisfies $aKa = (0)$, then $a = 0$.

**Proof.** Let $s \in S$ and $S$ be a subset of $R$; then $sas \in K$, hence $asasa = 0$. Given $r \in R$, $r = s + k$, $s \in S$ and $k \in K$, whence $arara = a(s + k)a(s + k)a = 0$. By Lemma 1.1, since in $aR$ every element has cube $0$, $a = 0$.

### Lemma 2.10
**Lemma 2.10.** Let $V$ be a Lie ideal of $[K, K]$ such that $[V, V] = (0)$. Then $V$ has no non-zero nilpotent elements.

**Proof.** Let $u \neq 0 \in V$. Then for any $k \in [K, K]$, $u(uk - ku) - (uk - ku)u = 0$, that is

\[
u^{2}k - 2uku + ku^{2} = 0. \tag{t}
\]

Thus we see that $u^{2} [K, K] \subset R u$. If $k = ub - bu$, $b \in K$, then $u^{2}(ub - bu) \subset R u$, hence $u^{3} \subset K \subset R u$. Finally, if $s \in S$, $u s + s u \subset K$ so that $u^{3}(u s + s u) \subset R u$ leading to $u^{4} S \subset R u$. But then $u^{4} R \subset R u$ so that $R u^{4} R \subset R u$; hence either $u^{4} = 0$ or $R u = R$. Similarly, if $u^{4} \neq 0$ we get $u R = R$; therefore $u \in V$ implies $u^{4} = 0$ or $u$ is invertible.

Suppose that $u \in V$ is not invertible; then $u^{4} = 0$, hence $u^{2} [K, K] u^{3} \subset R u u^{3} = 0$, so that $u^{2} [K, K] u^{3} = 0$. In particular, for $b \in K$, we have $u^{2}(ub - bu) u^{3} = 0$, that is $u^{3} K u^{3} = 0$. Since $u^{3} \subset K$, we have seen this implies that $u^{3} = 0$.

Let $N$ be the set of nilpotent elements in $V$. Since $[V, V] = 0$, clearly $N$ is closed under addition.

We claim that $\mathrm{N}$ is a Lie ideal of $[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$. If the characteristic of $R$ is $3$, then for $a \in V$, $[a, [a, [a, k]]] = 0$ implies $a^{3} k = k a^{3}$ for all $k \in \mathrm{K}$, hence $a^{3} \in \mathrm{Z} \cap \mathrm{K} = \{0\}$ in which case $\mathrm{N} = \mathrm{V}$, so $\mathrm{N}$ is a Lie ideal of $[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$. 

If the characteristic of $R$ is not $3$, then if $u \in \mathrm{N}$, since $u$ commutes with $u k - k u$, for $k \in [\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$, 
\[
u^{2} k - k u^{2} = 2 u (u k - k u),
\]
\[
0 = u^{3} k - k u^{3} = 3 u^{2} (u k - k u).
\]
If $u^{2} \neq 0$, then $u k - k u$ cannot be invertible since it annihilates $u^{2}$, so it is nilpotent. If $u^{2} = 0$, then $u (u k - k u) = 0$, so that again $u k - k u$ is nilpotent. Thus, $\mathrm{N}$ is a Lie ideal of $[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$.

Let $u, v \in \mathrm{N}$; then for $k \in [\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$, from (1), by multiplying by $u$ from the left and right, 
\[
u^{2} k u^{2} = 0,
\]
so 
\[
u^{2} (v b - b v) u^{2} = 0
\]
for all $b \in \mathrm{K}$. Thus, 
\[
u^{2} v b u^{2} = u^{2} b v u^{2},
\]
and so, since $u, v$ commute and $v^{3} = 0$, 
\[
u^{2} v^{2} \mathrm{K} u^{2} v^{2} = \{0\}.
\]
Also, for $s \in \mathrm{S}$, 
\[
v^{2} s - s v^{2} \in [\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}],
\]
so that 
\[
u^{2} \left(v^{2} s - s v^{2}\right) u^{2} = 0,
\]
leading to 
\[
u^{2} v^{2} \mathrm{S} u^{2} v^{2} = \{0\}.
\]
This gives 
\[
u^{2} v^{2} \mathrm{R} u^{2} v^{2} = \{0\},
\]
hence $u^{2} v^{2} = 0$. 

Linearizing on $u$, 
\[
(u w + w u) v^{2} = 0,
\]
that is, 
\[
u w v^{2} = 0.
\]
Linearizing on $v$, we get 
\[
u w v x = 0
\]
for $u, w, v, x \in \mathrm{N}$. But this says 
\[
u v w \mathrm{N} = \{0\},
\]
whence by Lemma 2.7, 
\[
u v w = 0,
\]
hence $u v = 0$, hence $u = 0$. (Again using Lemma 2.7.) Thus, $\mathrm{N} = \{0\}$.

Note that in the course of proving the result, we showed that if the characteristic of $R$ is 3, then $[\mathrm{V}, \mathrm{V}] = (0)$ implies $\mathrm{V} = (0)$ for $\mathrm{V}$ a Lie ideal of $[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$. So in what follows, we may assume that $R$ is of characteristic not 3.

### Lemma 2.11
If $\mathrm{V}$ is a Lie ideal of $[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$ such that $[\mathrm{V}, \mathrm{V}] = (0)$, then $\mathrm{V} = (0)$.

#### Proof
In the proof of the previous lemma, we showed that the non-zero elements of $\mathrm{V}$ are invertible. Since for $u \in \mathrm{V}$, $k \in \mathrm{K}$, $u k - k u \in [\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$, $[u, [u, [u, k]]] = 0$. Writing $d(k) = u k - k u$, $d^{3}(k) = 0$ for $k \in \mathrm{K}$.

Now $k u k \in \mathrm{K}$, hence $d^{3}(k u k) = 0$. Working this out, we get $3\left(d^{2}(k) u d(k) + d(k) u d^{2}(k)\right) = 0$. Applying $d$ to this, we get $d^{2}(k) u d^{2}(k) = 0$. Since $d^{2}(k) \in \mathrm{V}$, either it is $0$ or invertible. We thus get $d^{2}(k) = 0$ for all $k \in \mathrm{K}$. Thus $d^{2}(k u k) = 0$, hence $d(k) u d(k) = 0$. If $k \in [\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$, then $d(k) \in \mathrm{V}$, so it is $0$ or invertible. The net result is $d(k) = 0$ for $k \in [\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$. But then $u$ centralizes $[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$, hence $[\overline{\mathrm{K}}, \overline{\mathrm{K}}] = R$, putting $u \in Z$, a contradiction. Thus $\mathrm{V} = (0)$.

### Theorem 2.15
If $R$ is a simple ring of characteristic not 2 and if $\operatorname{dim} R / Z > 16$, then any proper Lie ideal of $[\mathrm{K}, \mathrm{K}]$ is in $Z$.

Proof. In case the involution is of the second kind, this is contained in Theorem 2.1. If the involution is of the first kind and if $U$ is a proper Lie ideal of $[K, K]$, by Theorem 2.14, $[[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}], [\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}]] = (0)$, hence by Lemma 2.11, $[\mathrm{U}, \mathrm{U}] = (0)$. By Lemma 2.11 again, we get $\mathrm{U} = (0)$.

## Chapter 3

### Jordan Homomorphisms and Derivations

Having studied the Jordan structure of certain rings and subsets of rings, we now turn to the study of mappings related to the Jordan structure. We shall say that $R$ is 2-torsion free if in $R$, $2x = 0$ forces $x = 0$.

**Definition.** A ring $R$ is a prime ring if $\mathrm{aRb} = (0)$ implies that $\mathrm{a} = 0$ or $\mathrm{b} = 0$.

The definition just given is equivalent to:

1. The product of two non-zero 2-sided ideals of $R$ is not $(0)$.  
2. The left annihilator of a non-zero left ideal is $(0)$.

**Definition.** A mapping $\phi$ from a ring $R$ into a ring $R^{\prime}$, which is 2-torsion free, is a Jordan homomorphism if:

\[
\phi(a + b) = \phi(a) + \phi(b)
\]

and

\[
\phi(ab + ba) = \phi(a)\phi(b) + \phi(b)\phi(a).
\]

We shall define a Jordan homomorphism even when there is 2-torsion a little later. When $R^{\prime}$ is 2-torsion free, note that we could have defined a Jordan homomorphism by merely insisting that $\left(a^{2}\right)^{\prime} = \left(a^{\prime}\right)^{2}$.

### Lemma 3.1
If $\phi$ is a Jordan homomorphism of $R$ into $R^{\prime}$, which is 2-torsion free, then $(aba)^{\prime} = a^{\prime}b^{\prime}a^{\prime}$ for all $a, b \in R$.

### Proof
Let $x \circ y = xy + yx$; then $(a \circ (a \circ b))^{\prime} = a^{\prime} \circ \left(a^{\prime} \circ b^{\prime}\right)$. Evaluating, using $\left(a^{2}\right)^{\prime} = \left(a^{\prime}\right)^{2}$, we get $2(aba)^{\prime} = 2a^{\prime}b^{\prime}a^{\prime}$. Hence, $(aba)^{\prime} = a^{\prime}b^{\prime}a^{\prime}$.

We use the lemma to define a Jordan homomorphism even in the case of 2-torsion by defining a Jordan homomorphism $\phi$ to be an additive mapping such that $\left(a^{2}\right)^{\prime} = \left(a^{\prime}\right)^{2}$ and $\left((aba)\right)^{\prime} = a^{\prime}b^{\prime}a^{\prime}$. In the 2-torsion free case, this is the same as the original definition we gave.

Linearizing $(aba)^{\prime} = a^{\prime}b^{\prime}a^{\prime}$, we get that $\left(\mathrm{abc} + \mathrm{cba}\right)^{\prime} = a^{\prime}b^{\prime}c^{\prime} + c^{\prime}b^{\prime}a^{\prime}$. We use this to prove.

LEMMA 3.2. If $\phi$ is a Jordan homomorphism of $R$ into $R^{\prime}$, then $\left((ab)^{\phi}-a^{\phi}b^{\phi}\right)\left((ba)^{\phi}-a^{\phi}b^{\phi}\right)=0$ and $\left((ba)^{\phi}-a^{\phi}b^{\phi}\right)\left((ab)^{\phi}-a^{\phi}b^{\phi}\right)=0$ for all $a, b \in R$.

**Proof.** We expand

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left((ab)^{\phi}-a^{\phi}b^{\phi}\right)\left((ab)^{\phi}-b^{\phi}a^{\phi}\right) & = \left((ab)^{\phi}\right)^{2} + a^{\phi}\left(b^{\phi}\right)^{2}a^{\phi} - (ab)^{\phi}b^{\phi}a^{\phi} - a^{\phi}b^{\phi}(ab)^{\phi} \\
& = \left((ab)^{2} + ab^{2}a - abba - (ab)^{2}\right)^{\phi} \\
& = 0.
\end{aligned}
$$

Similarly, for the other assertion.

Note that this is enough to answer the question about the nature of Jordan homomorphisms for the special case in which $R^{\prime}$ has no zero divisors, for then we get $(ab)^{\phi} = a^{\phi}b^{\phi}$ or $(ab)^{\phi} = b^{\phi}a^{\phi}$ for every pair $a, b \in R$. From this, it is an easy exercise to prove that $(xy)^{\phi} = x^{\phi}y^{\phi}$ for all $x, y \in R$ or $(xy)^{\phi} = y^{\phi}x^{\phi}$ for all $x, y \in R$, that is, the mapping is either a homomorphism or an anti-homomorphism.

We want to show that this conclusion holds in the much more general case wherein $R^{\prime}$ is any prime ring, provided the mapping is onto.

**DEFINITION**: For $a, b \in R$, let $'$ denote a Jordan homomorphism (a linear map satisfying $(ab)' = a'b + ab'$ for all $a, b \in R$). Define:

- $a^b = (ab)' - a'b'$
- $a_b = (ab)' - b'a'$

A few easy properties of these symbols:

1. $a^b = -b^a$ (this follows from the fact that $'$ is a Jordan homomorphism). Similarly, $a_b = -b_a$.
2. $\left[a^t, b^t\right] = a_b - a^b$, where $a^t$ and $b^t$ denote the transpose of $a$ and $b$ under the operation $'$.
3. $[a, b]^t = a_b + a^b$, where $[a, b] = ab - ba$ is the commutator of $a$ and $b$.
4. $a_b b_b = a_b a^b = 0$ (Lemma 3.2).

**LEMMA 3.3**: For $a, b, r \in R$,

$$
([a, b] r)' = r' a^b + a_b r'
$$

and

$$
(r[a, b])^t = a^b r^t + r' a b_b.
$$

**Proof**: 

Starting with $r' a^b + a_b r'$:

$$
r' a^b + a_b r' = r' ((ab)' - a'b') + ((ab)' - b'a') r'
$$

Expanding and simplifying using the properties of $'$:

$$
= r' (ab)' - r' a'b' - b'a'r' + (ab)' r'
$$

Combine terms:

$$
= (r ab + ab r)' - (r ab + b a r)' = ((ab - ba) r)'.
$$

Similarly, for the second formula, expand and simplify to verify the result.

**LEMMA 3.4**: If $'$ is a Jordan homomorphism of $R$ into $R'$, then $a_b r' a^b + a^b r' a_b = 0$.

**Proof**: From Lemma 3.3,

1. $\quad r' a^b + a_b r' = ([a, b] r)'$.

Multiply by $a^b$ from the left, using the property $a^b a_b = 0$ (from Lemma 3.2), we get:

2. 
$$
a^b r' a^b = a^b ([a, b] r)'.
$$

Similarly, multiplying by $a_b$ and using $a^b a_b = 0$, we conclude:

$$
a_b r' a^b + a^b r' a_b = 0.
$$

Multiplying (1) from the right by $a_b$, we get  
3. $\quad a_b r^2 a_b = ([a, b] r)' a_b$.

In $a^b r' + r' a_b = ([a, b] r)'$, replace $r$ by $[a, b] r$; using (1), (3), and Lemma 3.1, we get  

$$
a^b r' a^b + a_b r' a_b = [a, b]' r' [a, b]' = \left(a^b + a_b\right) r' \left(a^b + a_b\right).
$$

Expanding out, we end up with  
$$
a^b r^a a_b + a_b r^a a^b = 0,
$$  
the assertion of the lemma.

We now prove the  

### THEOREM 3.1  
Let $'$ be a Jordan homomorphism of $R$ onto $R^t$. If $R'$ is a prime ring, then $'$ is either a homomorphism or an anti-homomorphism.

**Proof.** By the previous lemma:  
(1) $\quad a^b r' a_b + a_b r' a_b = 0$.

Suppose that $\left(c^d\right)^2 \neq 0$ for some $c, d \in R$. Multiplying (1) by $c^d$ (where $a = c, b = d$ in (1)), we get  
$$
\left(c^d\right)^2 r' c_d = 0.
$$  
Since $R'$ is prime and $\left(c^d\right)^2 \neq 0$, then $c_d = 0$.

Thus, if $\left(c^d\right)^2 \neq 0$, we can conclude that $c_d = 0$.  
We linearize (1) on $b$ to get:

### Corrected Markdown

Let \( r, x \in R \), and consider the following equation:

$$
a^{b} r_{x} + a^{x} r' a_{b} + a_{b} r' a^{x} + a_{x} r' a^{b} = 0. \tag{2}
$$

We linearize \( a^{b} = 0 \) to obtain:

$$
a^{b} a_{x} + a^{x} a_{b} = 0. \tag{3}
$$

Now, substituting \( a = c \) and \( b = d \) into (2) and (3), and assuming \( (c^{d})^{2} \neq 0 \), we derive:

$$
c^{d} r' c_{x} + c_{x} r' c^{d} = 0, \tag{4}
$$

$$
c^{d} c_{x} = 0.
$$

Multiplying the first equation of (4) from the left by \( c^{d} \), and using \( c^{d} c_{x} = 0 \), we get:

$$
\left(c^{d}\right)^{2} r' c_{x} = 0.
$$

Since \( r' \) is a non-zero scalar and \( (c^{d})^{2} \neq 0 \), it follows that \( c_{x} = 0 \).

Thus, if \( (c^{d})^{2} \neq 0 \), then \( c_{x} = 0 \) for all \( x \in R \). Similarly, \( (d^{c})^{2} = (-c^{d})^{2} \neq 0 \) implies \( d_{x} = 0 \) for all \( x \in R \). If \( (c_{d})^{2} \neq 0 \), then \( c^{x} = 0 \) for all \( x \in R \).

We linearize (2) with respect to \( a \) to obtain:

$$
a^{b} r' y_{x} + a^{x} r' y_{b} + y^{b} r' a + a_{b} r' y^{x} + y^{x} r' a_{b} + y_{b} r' a^{x} + y_{x} r' a^{b} = 0. \tag{5}
$$

If \( (c^{d})^{2} \neq 0 \), then \( c_{x} = 0 \) implies \( d_{x} = 0 \) for all \( x \). Substituting \( a = c \), \( b = d \) into (5), we get:

$$
c^{d} r' y_{x} + y_{x} r' c^{d} = 0. \tag{6}
$$

For all $x, y \in R$, we have:

Now, from $x^y x_y = 0$, interpreted as a product of terms, we get:

$$
x^y w_z + w^y x_z + x^z w_y + w^z x_y = 0. \tag{1}
$$

In (1), let $w = c$ and $z = d$. Since $c_r = d_s = 0$ for all $r, s$, we deduce that $c^d x_y = 0$. Multiplying (1) by $c^d$, we obtain:

$$
\left(c^d\right)^2 x_y = 0,
$$

hence $x_y = 0$ for all $x, y \in R$. This implies that $xy = yx$, which is to say that $R$ is commutative. Similarly, if for some $c, d$, $\left(c^d\right)^2 \neq 0$, then $R$ must be a homomorphism.

We are left to consider the situation in which $\left(x^{y}\right)^{2}=0$, $\left(x_{y}\right)^{2}=0$ for all $x, y \in R$. Here, $R$ is a ring, and $R^{\prime}$ is a subring or related structure. Note that $x_{y}$ and $x^{y}$ represent specific operations in $R$, which must be explicitly defined for clarity. For instance, $x_{y}$ could denote a product or operation involving $x$ and $y$, while $x^{y}$ might represent a power or another operation. 

We define the commutator as $\left[x^{\prime}, y^{\prime}\right]=x_{y}-x^{y}$, where $x^{\prime}, y^{\prime} \in R^{\prime}$. Since $x_{y} x^{y}=x^{y} x_{y}=\left(x^{y}\right)^{2}=\left(x_{y}\right)^{2}=0$, it follows that $\left[x^{\prime}, y^{\prime}\right]^{2}=0$ for all $x^{\prime}, y^{\prime} \in R^{\prime}$. 

Now, consider the case where $\left(a^{\prime}\right)^{2}=0$. In this situation, $a^{\prime}R^{\prime}$ forms a nil ideal of index $3$, meaning that any product of three elements from this ideal is zero. By Lemma 1.1, $R^{\prime}$ would then have a nilpotent ideal, which contradicts the assumption that $R^{\prime}$ is a prime ring. Thus, $R^{\prime}$ has no nilpotent elements. Consequently, $\left[x^{\prime}, y^{\prime}\right]=0$, implying that $R^{\prime}$ is commutative. 

Since $R^{\prime}$ is commutative and prime, it has no zero-divisors. As noted at the end of the proof of Lemma 3.2, this forces the identity element $1$ to act as a homomorphism, meaning that $1$ preserves the ring operations. We have now proved the theorem.

We now consider mappings that are additive and preserve $n^{\text{th}}$ powers.

**DEFINITION.** A mapping $\psi$ of $R$ into $R^{\prime}$ is an $n$-Jordan mapping if $\psi$ is additive and satisfies $\psi\left(a^{n}\right)=\psi(a)^{n}$ for all $a \in R$ and for a fixed integer $n \geq 1$.

**THEOREM 3.2.** Let $q$ be an $n$-Jordan mapping from $R$ onto a prime ring $R^{\prime}$ whose characteristic is 0 or larger than $n$. Then if $R$ has a unit element, $\varphi \in T$, where $T$ is either a homomorphism or an anti-homomorphism, and $\varphi$ is an $(n-1)$-st root of unity lying in the center of $R^{\prime}$.

**Proof.** Since $\varphi\left(x^{n}\right)=\varphi(x)^{n}$, expanding $\varphi$ on $x$ we get

\[
\sum_{\pi \in S_{n}} \varphi\left(x_{\pi(1)} \cdots x_{\pi(n)}\right)=\sum_{\pi \in S_{n}} \varphi\left(x_{\pi(1)}\right) \cdots \varphi\left(x_{\pi(n)}\right), \tag{1}
\]

where $S_{n}$ is the symmetric group of degree $n$, consisting of all permutations of $n$ elements.

Let $\alpha=\varphi(1)$; thus $\alpha^{n}=\varphi(1)^{n}=\varphi(1)=\alpha$. In (1), set $x_{1}=x$, $x_{2}=\ldots=x_{n}=1$. We obtain

\[
n! \varphi(x)=(n-1)!\left(\alpha^{n-1} \varphi(x)+\alpha^{n-2} \varphi(x) \alpha+\ldots+\varphi(x) \alpha^{n-1}\right). \tag{2}
\]

Here, $n!$ represents the factorial of $n$, and $(n-1)!$ represents the factorial of $(n-1)$.

Hence $n \varphi(x)=\left(\alpha^{n-1} \varphi(x)+\ldots+\varphi(x) \alpha^{n-1}\right)$, since the characteristic of $R^{\prime}$ exceeds $n$. Considering the commutativity of $\varphi(x)$ with $\alpha$, and using $\alpha^{n}=\alpha$, we derive $(n-1)(\alpha \varphi(x)-\varphi(x) \alpha)=0$. Therefore, $\alpha \varphi(x)=\varphi(x) \alpha$. 

Thus, $\alpha$ is an $(n-1)$-st root of unity, meaning $\alpha^{n-1}=1$, and lies in the center of $R^{\prime}$.

We return to (1) putting $x_{1}=x$, $x_{2}=y$, and $x_{3}=\ldots=x_{n}=1$. (1) then gives, from the condition on the characteristic, $\phi(xy + yx) = \alpha^{n-2}\varphi(x) + \varphi(y)\varphi(x)$. Let $\tau = \alpha^{n-1}\varphi$; then $\tau$ is a Jordan homomorphism of $R$ onto $R$. By Theorem 3.1, it is either a homomorphism or anti-homomorphism. This completes the proof of the theorem.

We now turn to the question of Jordan derivations. We assume all rings to be 2-torsion free.

### Definition
A Jordan derivation $'$ on $R$ is an additive mapping of $R$ into itself such that $(a^{2})' = aa' + a'a$.

Note that this is equivalent to

$$
(a \circ b)' = a' \circ b + a \circ b'.
$$

We begin with:

### Lemma 3.5
If $'$ is a Jordan derivation on $R$, then $(aba)' = a'b a + ab'a + aba'$ for all $a, b \in R$.

#### Proof
We compute $(a \circ (a \circ b))'$ in two ways.

$$
\left(a^{2} \circ b + 2aba\right)' = \left(a \circ (a \circ b)\right)' = a' \circ (a \circ b) + a \circ (a \circ b)'.
$$

Evaluating, we get the result. Linearizing, we have:

### Corollary
For $a, b, c \in R$,

$$
(abc + cba)' = a'bc + ab'c + abc' + c'ba + cb'a + cba'.
$$

### Lemma 3.6
For $a, b \in R$,

$$
\left((ab)^{\prime} - a^{\prime}b - ab^{\prime}\right)(ab - ba) = 0.
$$

### Proof
Let 
$$
w = \left((ab)ab + ba(ab)\right)^{\prime} = (ab)^{\prime}ab + (ab)a^{\prime}b + abab^{\prime} + b^{\prime}a(ab) + ba^{\prime}ab + ba(ab)^{\prime}.
$$ 
But 
$$
w = (ab)^{2} + \left(ba^{2}b\right)^{\prime} = ab(ab)^{\prime} + (ab)^{\prime}ab + b^{\prime}a^{2}b + b\left(aa^{\prime} + a^{\prime}a\right)b + ba^{2}b^{\prime}.
$$ 
Comparing the results, we get the lemma.

### Definition
Let 
$$
a^{b} = (ab)^{\prime} - a^{\prime}b - ab^{\prime}.
$$ 
In these terms, the lemma reads $a^{b}(ab - ba) = 0$. Note that $a^{b}$ is linear in $a$ and $b$, and $a^{b} = -b^{a}$. The linearity of $a^{b}$ follows from the distributive property of derivatives and the ring operations.

### Corollary 1
Linearizing Lemma 3.6 on $a$, we get:

$$
a^{b}(cb - bc) + c^{b}(ab - ba) = 0 \quad \text{for } a, b, c \in R.
$$
Here, "linearizing" refers to applying the linearity property of $a^{b}$ to expand the expression over multiple terms.

### Corollary 2
$$
a^{c}(ab - ba) + a^{b}(ac - ca) = 0.
$$ 
This last corollary is merely the linearization on $b$.

### Lemma 3.7
If $R$ is a prime ring and if $ab = 0$, then $a^{b} = 0$.

### Additional Notes
The proof of Lemma 3.6 assumes the equality of $w$ expressions, which can be rigorously justified by expanding and comparing terms step by step. Further clarification of intermediate steps may be necessary for completeness.

**Proof.**  
If $ab = ba = 0$, then by the corollary to Lemma 3.6, $a^b \cdot (cb - bc) = 0$ for all $c \cdot b$ not in $Z$, since $Z$ has no zero divisors. But then $a^b \cdot (cb - bc) = 0$ leads us to $a^b \cdot (c \cdot b - b \cdot c) = 0$. Since $(b - b) = 0$, the term $a^b \cdot R \cdot (b - b) = 0$ is trivially zero. This gives $a^b = 0$.  

Suppose $ab = 0$, $ba \neq 0$. Then by the corollary to Lemma 3.6, $a^b \cdot (ac - ca) \cdot b = 0$, hence $a^b \cdot a \cdot R \cdot b = 0$. Since $R$ is a prime ring (i.e., $R$ has no zero divisors), $a^b \cdot a = 0$ for any $b$ with $ab = 0$. But then since $a^b = (a \cdot b)' - a' \cdot b - a \cdot b'$, we get $\left(a' \cdot b + a \cdot b'\right) \cdot a = 0$. For any $r \in R$, $a \cdot b \cdot r \cdot b = 0$, hence $\left(a' \cdot b \cdot r \cdot b + a \cdot (b \cdot r \cdot b)'\right) \cdot a = 0$. Since $(b \cdot r \cdot b)' = b' \cdot r \cdot b + b \cdot r' \cdot b + b \cdot r \cdot b'$, we get $\left(a' \cdot b \cdot r \cdot b + a \cdot b' \cdot r \cdot b\right) \cdot a = 0$, whence $\left(a' \cdot b + a \cdot b'\right) \cdot R \cdot b \cdot a = 0$. Since $ba \neq 0$, it follows that $a^2 \cdot b + a \cdot b' = 0$. Thus $a^b = 0$.  

Note that in this case $b^a = -a^b = 0$, so that $(b \cdot a)' = b' \cdot a + b \cdot a'$.  

**Lemma 3.8.**  
If $ab = 0$, then for $c \in R$, where $R$ is a prime ring, $(c \cdot (b \cdot a))^t = c' \cdot (b \cdot a) + c \cdot (b \cdot a)'$.  

**Proof.**  
By the corollary to Lemma 3.5:  
- Since $ab = 0$, $a$ and $b$ satisfy the conditions of Lemma 3.5.  
- For $c \in R$, $(c \cdot (b \cdot a))^t$ expands as $c' \cdot (b \cdot a) + c \cdot (b \cdot a)'$ by the corollary.

$$
\begin{aligned}
(c b a)' &= (c b a + a b c) \\
&= c' b a + c b' a + c b a' + a' b c + a b' c + a b c' \\
&= c'(b a) + c\left(b' a + b a'\right) + \left(a' b + a b'\right)c.
\end{aligned}
$$

By Lemma 3.7, $a' b + a b' = 0$ and $b' a + b a' = (b a)'$. Thus the lemma.

**DEFINITION.** Let $R$ be a ring. Define $V = \left\{x \in R \mid (a x)' = a' x + a x', \forall a \in R\right\}$.  
Note that by Lemma 3.8, if $a b = 0$, then $b a \in V$.

We need one more lemma.  
**LEMMA 3.9.** If $v, w \in V$, then $a(b v - v b) = 0$ for all $a, b \in R$.

**Proof.** By the corollary to Lemma 3.5:

$$
a(b v - v b) + v(a b - b a) = 0,
$$

hence, since $v(a b - b a) = 0$, we have $a(b v - v b) = 0$ for all $b$. Expanding this linearly in $b$, we get:

$$
a(c v - v c) + a(b v - v b) = 0.
$$

Setting $c = w \in V$, and noting that $a w = 0$, we conclude $a(b w - w b) = 0$.

We now prove the  
**THEOREM 3.3.** If $R$ is a prime ring of characteristic different from $2$, then any Jordan derivation of $R$ is a derivation of $R$.

**Proof.** Suppose that $a^b \neq 0$. Here, $a^b$ represents exponentiation in the ring $R$, where $R$ is a prime ring. Note that $b \notin Z$, where $Z$ denotes the center of the ring $R$ (i.e., $Z = \{z \in R \mid zr = rz \text{ for all } r \in R\}$). 

Given $c \in R$, since $c^b (cb - bc) = 0$ (Lemma 3.6), we get, for any $r \in R$, $v = (cb - bc) r c^b \in V$, where $V$ is the annihilator of all elements of the form $(cb - bc)$ for $c \in R$ (as defined in Lemma 3.8). Similarly, for $s \in R$, $(ab - ba) s a^b \in V$. Using these $v, w$ in Lemma 3.9, and in conjunction with Lemma 3.6, we get:  
\[
a^b (cb - bc) + c^b (ab - ba) = 0.
\]  

Since $R$ is prime and $a^b \neq 0$, we conclude that either $a^b (cb - bc) = 0$ or $c^b (ab - ba) = 0$. By the corollary to Lemma 3.6, we have:  
\[
a^b (cb - bc) = 0 \quad \text{for all } a, b, c \in R.
\]

If $b \notin Z$, since the annihilator of all $cb - bc$, $c \in R$, is an ideal, we get $a^b = 0$. If $Z \neq R$ and $b \in Z$, choosing $c \notin Z$, then $b + c \notin Z$, hence:  
\[
0 = a^c = a^{b+c} = a^b \cdot a^c,
\]  
leading to $a^b = 0$.  

Finally, if $Z = R$, then the definition of a derivation (a map $d: R \to R$ satisfying $d(ab) = d(a)b + ad(b)$ for all $a, b \in R$) yields:  
\[
2(ab)' = 2\left(a'b + ab'\right),
\]  
whence, assuming the characteristic of $R$ is not 2, we conclude that:  
\[
(ab)' = a'b + ab'.
\]

Thus, in all cases, $a^b = 0$ for all $a, b \in R$. Therefore, $a'$ is a derivation of $R$.

If one checks the proof given, one sees that the assumption that the characteristic of $R$ is not 2 enters only at two points in proving $(aba)^{t} = a^{\prime}ba + ab^{\prime}a + aba^{\prime}$ and at the very end of the argument just given.

If we redefine a Jordan mapping by $\left(a^{2}\right)^{x} = aa^{x} + a^{x}a$ and $(aba)^{x} = a^{x}ba + ab^{x}a + aba^{x}$, then in characteristic not 2 we have imposed no extra restriction, yet in characteristic 2 it allows us to conclude:

**THEOREM 3.4.** If $R$ is a prime ring and ${}^{x}$ is a Jordan derivation (as newly redefined) of $R$, then ${}^{x}$ is a derivation except if $R$ is both commutative (and so an integral domain) and of characteristic 2.

At this point, we should like to redo the theorem on Jordan homomorphisms from a slightly different point of view. It follows fairly easily from a simple result about prime rings but suffers from the defect that we must insist that the characteristic is not 2. Outside of this lemma, which we are about to prove, we only need the result of Lemma 3.1.

**LEMMA 3.10.** Let $R$ be a prime ring of characteristic not 2 and suppose $a, b \in R$ are such that $arb + bra = 0$ for all $r \in R$. Then either $a = 0$ or $b = 0$.

### Proof

By assumption, $\mathrm{a} R \mathrm{b} + \mathrm{b} R \mathrm{a} = 0$. Replace $R$ by $st$ with $s, t \in R$. We get $\mathrm{a} s \mathrm{b} t \mathrm{a} + \mathrm{b} s \mathrm{a} t \mathrm{b} = 0$. But $\mathrm{b} s \mathrm{a} = -\mathrm{a} s \mathrm{b}$ and $\mathrm{a} t \mathrm{b} = -\mathrm{b} t \mathrm{a}$; substituting these, we get $-\mathrm{a} s \mathrm{b} t \mathrm{a} - \mathrm{a} s \mathrm{b} t \mathrm{a} = 0$, that is, $2 \mathrm{a} s \mathrm{b} t \mathrm{a} = 0$. Since $R$ is a prime ring of characteristic not $2$, this immediately gives $\mathrm{a} = 0$ or $\mathrm{b} = 0$.

We now reprove Theorem 3.1, assuming that $^{\mathrm{t}}$ is a Jordan homomorphism of $R$ onto $R^{\prime}$, where $R^{\prime}$ is a prime ring of characteristic not $2$.

Consider $\mathrm{u} = (\mathrm{a} \mathrm{b})^{t} - \mathrm{a}^{t} \mathrm{b}^{t}$, $\mathrm{v} = (\mathrm{a} \mathrm{b})^{t} - \mathrm{b}^{t} \mathrm{a}^{t}$; then

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm{u} R^{\prime} \mathrm{v} & = \left((\mathrm{a} \mathrm{b})^{t} - \mathrm{a}^{t} \mathrm{b}^{t}\right) R^{\prime} \left((\mathrm{a} \mathrm{b})^{t} - \mathrm{b}^{t} \mathrm{a}^{t}\right) \\
& = (\mathrm{a} \mathrm{b})^{t} R^{\prime} (\mathrm{a} \mathrm{b})^{t} + \mathrm{a}^{t} \mathrm{b}^{t} R^{\prime} \mathrm{b}^{t} \mathrm{a}^{t} - \mathrm{a}^{t} \mathrm{b}^{t} R^{\prime} (\mathrm{a} \mathrm{b})^{t} - (\mathrm{a} \mathrm{b})^{t} R^{\prime} \mathrm{b}^{t} \mathrm{a}^{t} \\
& = (\mathrm{a} \mathrm{b} R \mathrm{a} \mathrm{b})^{t} + (\mathrm{a} \mathrm{b} R \mathrm{b} \mathrm{a})^{t} - \mathrm{a}^{t} \mathrm{b}^{t} R^{\prime} (\mathrm{a} \mathrm{b})^{t} - (\mathrm{a} \mathrm{b})^{t} R^{\prime} \mathrm{b}^{t} \mathrm{a}^{t},
\end{aligned}
$$

using Lemma 3.1. Similarly,

$$
\mathrm{v} R^{\prime} \mathrm{u} = (\mathrm{a} \mathrm{b} R \mathrm{a} \mathrm{b})^{t} + (\mathrm{b} \mathrm{a} R \mathrm{a} \mathrm{b})^{t} - \mathrm{b}^{t} \mathrm{a}^{t} R^{\prime} (\mathrm{a} \mathrm{b})^{t} - (\mathrm{a} \mathrm{b})^{t} R^{\prime} \mathrm{a}^{t} \mathrm{b}^{t}.
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
u r v + v r u & = \left\{2 a b r a b + a b r b a + b a r a b\right\} \\
& - \left\{\left(a^{\prime} b^{\prime} + b^{\prime} a^{\prime}\right) r \left(a b\right) + \left(a b\right) r \left(a^{\prime} b^{\prime} + b^{\prime} a^{\prime}\right)\right\} \\
& = \left\{2 a b r a b + a b r b a + b a r a b\right\} \\
& - \left\{\left(a b + b a\right) r \left(a b\right) + \left(a b\right) r \left(a b + b a\right)\right\} \\
& = \left\{2 a b r a b + a b r b a + b a r a b\right\} \\
& - \left\{\left(a b + b a\right) r \left(a b\right) + \left(a b\right) r \left(a b + b a\right)\right\}.
\end{aligned}
$$

(using the corollary to Lemma 3.1)

Since $u r v + v r u = 0$ for all $r$, using Lemma 3.10 we have $u = 0$ or $v = 0$, that is, $(a b)^{\prime} = a^{\prime} b^{\prime}$ or $(a b)^{\prime} = b^{\prime} a^{\prime}$.

Given $a \in R$, let $W_{a} = \{b \in R \mid (ab)^{+} = a^{+}b^{+}\}$ and $V_{a} = \{b \in R \mid (ab)^{\prime} = b^{\prime}a^{\prime}\}$. Clearly, each of $W_{a}$ and $V_{a}$ are additive subgroups of $R$. Moreover, by the discussion given, $R$ is the set-theoretic union of $W_{a}$ and $V_{a}$. But a group cannot be the set-theoretic union of two proper subgroups; hence $W_{a} = R$ or $V_{a} = R$, that is, $(ab)^{+} = a^{+}b^{+}$ for all $b \in R$ or $(ab)^{\prime} = b^{\prime}a^{\prime}$ for all $b \in R$. 

Let $W = \{a \in R \mid (ax)^{t} = a^{t}x^{t} \text{ for all } x \in R\}$ and $V = \{a \in R \mid (ax)^{t} = x^{t}a^{t} \text{ for all } x \in R\}$. Both $W$ and $V$ are additive subgroups of $R$ and, from the above, their union is $R$. In consequence, $W = R$ or $V = R$. 

If $W = R$, then $(xy)^{t} = x^{t}y^{t}$ for all $x, y \in R$ and $t$ is a homomorphism. If $V = R$, then $(xy)^{t} = y^{t}x^{t}$ for all $x, y \in R$ and $t$ is an anti-homomorphism. This is Theorem 3.1.

We close the chapter with a discussion, without proofs, of some highly interesting theorems proved in the last few years by Martindale.

The first of these concerns Jordan maps of the symmetric elements of a ring with involution, that is, additive maps $\phi$ defined on the symmetric elements such that $\phi\left(s^{2}\right)=\phi(s)^{2}$ for $s$ symmetric. He proves:

Let $R$ be a ring with involution, $\#$, such that:

1. $R$ contains three non-zero orthogonal idempotents $e_{1}, e_{2}, e_{3}$ such that $e_{1}+e_{2}+e_{3}=1$.
2. $e_{i} R e_{j} R e_{i}=e_{i} R e_{i}$ for $i, j=1,2,3$.

Let $\phi$ be a Jordan homomorphism of the symmetric elements $S$ of $R$ in a ring $R^{\prime}$ whose characteristic is not 2. Then $\phi$ can be extended uniquely to an associative homomorphism $\Phi$ of $R$ into $R^{\prime}$.

Martindale has obtained very decisive results about Lie homomorphisms of one ring into another. A Lie homomorphism $\phi$ is an additive map such that $\phi(ab - ba) = \phi(a)\phi(b) - \phi(b)\phi(a)$. We now give the statement of some of these:

1. Let $\phi$ be a Lie isomorphism of a primitive ring $R$ onto a primitive ring $R^{\prime}$, where $R$ is not of characteristic 2 or 3. Suppose that $R$ contains three orthogonal idempotents whose sum is 1. Then $\phi$ is of the form $\sigma+\tau$, where $\sigma$ is either an isomorphism or the negative of an anti-isomorphism of $R$ into a primitive ring $L^{\prime}$ containing $R^{\prime}$, and $\tau$ is an additive mapping of $R$ into the center of $L^{\prime}$ taking all commutators $ab - ba$ into 0.

### Notes:
- The involution symbol $\#$ denotes an operation on the ring $R$ such that $(a^\#)^\# = a$ for all $a \in R$.
- Jordan maps refer to additive maps $\phi$ satisfying $\phi(s^2) = \phi(s)^2$ for symmetric elements $s$ of the ring.

In an unpublished result (which will appear), he has extended this result significantly in the following way:

1. $R$ and $R^{\prime}$ are merely assumed to be prime rings.
2. Instead of three orthogonal idempotents, he only requires that $R$ has an idempotent $e \neq 0, 1$.

With these weakened hypotheses, he still gets the same conclusion about the nature of Lie isomorphisms.

In the case when $R$ and $R^{\prime}$ are simple and $R$ is of characteristic not 2 or 3 with a unit element and having an idempotent $e \neq 0, 1$, a little more can be said, namely, that a Lie isomorphism of $R$ onto $R^{\prime}$ is of the form $\sigma + \tau$, where $\sigma$ is an isomorphism or the negative of an anti-isomorphism of $R$ onto $R^{\prime}$ (here one does not need the extension $\mathrm{L}^{\dagger}$ or $\mathrm{R}^{\prime}$), and $\tau$ is an additive map of $R$ into the center of $R^{\prime}$ vanishing on commutators $ab - ba$.

A student of Martindale, R. A. Howland, has recently proved a related result: Let $R$ be a simple ring of characteristic not 2 or 3 with three non-zero orthogonal idempotents whose sum is 1, and let $T$ be a simple ring with 1. Then any Lie isomorphism of $[R, R]$ onto $[T, T]$ can be uniquely extended to an associative isomorphism of $R$ onto $T$.

These results give much information about the automorphisms of $R$ and $[R, R]$ as Lie algebras in terms of associative automorphisms and trace-like mappings of $R$.

Finally, there is the closely connected question of Lie derivations, that is, additive mappings, $\delta$, such that

$$
\delta([a, b]) = [\delta(a), b] + [a, \delta(b)].
$$

## Martindale has proved:

Let $R$ be a primitive ring containing an idempotent $e \neq 0, 1$ and suppose that the characteristic of $R$ is not 2. Then any Lie derivation of $R$ is of the form $D + T$, where $D$ is an associative derivation of $R$ into a primitive ring $\overline{R} \supset R$ and $T$ is an additive mapping.

$(a, b) \sim (c, d)$ if $d_1 a = b_1 c$ where $b_1 d = d_1 b$, $b_1$ regular (and so $d_1$ is regular). We claim this is independent of $b_1, d_1$, for if $b_2 d = d_2 b$, pick $e_1, e_2$ regular so that $e_2 b_2 = e_1 b_1$. Then

$$
\begin{aligned}
e_1 b_1 d &= e_1 d_1 b, \\
e_2 b_2 d &= e_2 d_2 b.
\end{aligned}
$$

By the regularity of $b$, $e_1 d_1 = e_2 d_2$. Hence, from $d_1 a = b_1 c$, $e_2 d_2 a = e_1 d_1 a = e_1 b_1 c = e_2 b_2 c$, and since $e_2$ is regular, $d_2 a = b_2 c$.

We claim the relation defined on $M$ is an equivalence relation. Let the class of $(a, b)$ be written as $a / b$.

If $b_1 d = d_1 b$, define $a / b + c / d = \frac{d_1 a + b_1 c}{b_1 d}$ and $(a / b)(c / d) = \frac{d_1 a c}{b_1 d b}$, where $d_1 a = a d_1$ and $b_1 c = c b_1$. Note that $d_1$ and $b_1$ are scaling factors ensuring consistency in the operations.

One can easily verify that the equivalence classes form a ring with unit, denoted as $Q(R)$. Here, $Q(R)$ is defined as the left quotient ring of $R$, which consists of equivalence classes of fractions $a / b$ with $a, b \in R$ and $b$ not a zero divisor.

**Definition.** If $S \subset R$ is a subset, then 
\[
\ell(S) = \{x \in R \mid xS = (0)\},
\]
the left-annihilator of $S$, and 
\[
r(S) = \{x \in R \mid Sx = (0)\},
\]
the right-annihilator of $S$.

Clearly, $\ell(S)$ is a left ideal of $R$, and $r(S)$ is a right ideal of $R$. We say that a left ideal $\lambda$ of $R$ is a left annihilator if $\lambda = \ell(S)$ for some $S \subset R$.

We now introduce the Goldie chain conditions.

**Definition.** $R$ satisfies the Goldie chain conditions if:
1. Any ascending chain of left annihilators becomes stationary after some point.
2. $R$ has no infinite direct sum of left ideals.

A ring satisfying the Goldie conditions will be called a Goldie ring.

**Lemma 4.1.** Let $R$ be a Goldie ring without zero divisors. Then $R$ has a left quotient ring which is a division ring.

**Proof.** We shall show that the intersection of any two nonzero left ideals of $R$ is not $(0)$. Let $a, b \in R$, neither $0$. Then $\mathrm{Ra} \cap \mathrm{Rb} \neq (0)$ because $R$ has no zero divisors and satisfies the ascending chain condition on left annihilators. By Theorem 4.1, $R$ will have a ring of left quotients, which will obviously be a division ring.

We claim that there is a left ideal $\mathrm{U} \neq (0)$ of $R$ such that any two non-zero left ideals of $R$ lying in $\mathrm{U}$ have a non-zero intersection. If not, there are two non-zero left ideals $\mathrm{U}_1, \mathrm{U}_2$ with intersection $(0)$, i.e., $\mathrm{U}_1 \cap \mathrm{U}_2 = (0)$. If in $\mathrm{U}_1$ there are two non-zero left ideals of $R$, $\mathrm{U}_1^{(1)}, \mathrm{U}_1^{(2)}$, with intersection $(0)$, then $\mathrm{U}_1^{(1)} + \mathrm{U}_1^{(2)} + \mathrm{U}_2$ is a direct sum. Continue this on $\mathrm{U}_1^{(1)}$, and so on, to get longer and larger direct sums. Since there are no infinite direct sums of left ideals of $R$, we finally must stop. We thus produce the required left ideal $\mathrm{U}$ of $R$.

Suppose that $\lambda, \mu$ are non-zero left ideals of $R$. Pick $a \neq 0$ in $\mathrm{U}$; then $\lambda a$ and $\mu a$ are non-zero left ideals of $R$ lying in $\mathrm{U}$, hence must have a non-zero intersection. So there is an $x \neq 0$ such that $x = \alpha a = \beta a$, where $\alpha \in \lambda, \beta \in \mu$. Since $R$ has no zero divisors, $0 \neq \alpha = \beta \in \lambda \cap \mu$, whence $\lambda \cap \mu \neq (0)$.

**DEFINITION.** A ring $R$ will be called an Ore domain if it has no zero divisors and the intersection of any two non-zero left ideals of $R$ is not zero.

Properly speaking, such a ring should be called a left Ore domain, but for the sake of brevity, we drop the "left." Note that by Theorem 4.1, a ring is an Ore domain if it has a ring of left quotients which is a division ring.

Let $R$ be an Ore domain and let $D$ be the division ring of left quotients of $R$. If $k_{1}, \ldots, k_{n} \in D$, then $k_{i} = a_{i}^{-1} b_{i}$ with $a_{i}, b_{i} \in R$. We claim that we can find an element $a \in R$ and $c_{i} \in R$ such that each $k_{i} = a^{-1} c_{i}$. We proceed by induction on $n$; suppose that $t$ has been found in $R$ such that $k_{1} = t^{-1} d_{1}, \ldots, k_{n-1} = t^{-1} d_{n-1}$, where $d_{1}, \ldots, d_{n-1} \in R$. Now $k_{n} = a^{-1} b_{n}$; moreover, there is an element $a \neq 0$ such that $a = s t = u a_{n}$. Then 

\[
k_{1} = a^{-1} s d_{1}, \ldots, k_{n-1} = a^{-1} s d_{n-1}, k_{n} = a^{-1} u b_{n}
\]

is the desired representation. We shall use this remark often, so we record it as:

**Sublemma A.** If $R$ is an Ore domain with a ring of left quotients $D$, then, given $k_{1}, \ldots, k_{n}$ in $D$, we can find an element $a \in R$, and $c_{1}, \ldots, c_{n} \in R$ such that $k_{i} = a^{-1} c_{i}$ for $i = 1, 2, \ldots, n$.

We now prove a condition that a ring be an Ore domain.

**Lemma 4.2.** Let $R$ be a ring without zero divisors. Then the following are equivalent:

1. $R$ is an Ore domain.  
2. In $R$ there is no infinite direct sum of left ideals.  
3. If $\lambda \neq 0$ is a fixed given left ideal of $R$, then there is no infinite direct sum of left ideals of $\lambda$.  

**Proof.**  
That (1) implies (2) is trivial, for any two left ideals in an Ore domain intersect.  

**(2) implies (3):**  
If $\lambda \neq 0$ is a left ideal of $R$, and if $\lambda_{1} \neq 0$ is a left ideal of $\lambda$, then $0 \neq \lambda \lambda_{1} \subset \lambda_{1}$ and $\lambda \lambda_{1}$ is a left ideal of $R$.  
So if $\lambda_{1} + \ldots + \lambda_{n}$ is a direct sum of left ideals of $\lambda$, then $\lambda \lambda_{1} + \ldots + \lambda \lambda_{n}$ is one of the left ideals of $R$.  

**(3) implies (1):**  
Suppose $\lambda \neq 0$ is a left ideal of $R$ having no infinite direct sums. As in Theorem 4.1, we can find a left ideal $\lambda_{1} \neq 0$ of $\lambda$ in which any two left ideals intersect. Since $\lambda \lambda_{1} \subset \lambda_{1}$, we may assume that $\lambda_{1}$ is a left ideal of $R$.  

Let $a, b$ be non-zero in $R$; pick $\alpha \neq 0$ in $\lambda_{1}$. Then $R a \alpha$, $R b \alpha$ are left ideals in $\lambda_{1}$, so they intersect. That is, there are $x, y \in R$ with $x a \alpha = y b \alpha \neq 0$. Since $R$ is without zero divisors, $x a = y b \neq 0$ follows.  

We record one more fact about the relationship between an Ore domain and its ring of quotients.

LEMMA 4.3. Let $E$ be an Ore domain, $K$ its ring of left quotients, and suppose that $A \neq 0$ is a left ideal of $E$. Then $A$ has $K$ as its ring of left quotients.

**Proof.** Given $k \in K$, $k = a^{-1}b$ where $a, b \in E$. Pick $c \neq 0$ in $A$; therefore, there are elements $x, y \in E$, not zero, such that $xa = yc \neq 0$. Also, there are elements $r, s \in E$ such that $rxb = sc \neq 0$. Thus, $k = a^{-1}b = (rxa)^{-1}rxb = (ryc)^{-1}sc = u^{-1}v$ where $u = ryc \in A$ and $v = sc \in A$. This proves the lemma.

We recall from Chapter 3 that a ring $R$ is a prime ring if $\mathrm{aRb} = 0$ implies that $\mathrm{a} = 0$ or $\mathrm{b} = 0$. If $\lambda \neq 0$ is a left ideal of $R$ and $p \neq 0$ is a right ideal of $R$ where $R$ is prime, then $0 \neq \rho \lambda \subset \lambda \cap \rho$, that is, every non-zero left ideal intersects every non-zero right ideal non-trivially.

In what follows, $R$ will be a prime Goldie ring. Let $F$ be a maximal left annihilator in $R$ and let $F_{r} = r(F)$. We shall maintain this notation. (If $F_{r} = 0$, we know from Lemma 4.1 that $R$ has a ring of left quotients which is a division ring. So we suppose henceforth that $F_{r} \neq 0$, $F \neq 0$.) We follow the proof given by Procesi.

**DEFINITION.** For $u \in F_{r}$, let $\lambda_{u}: F_{r} \rightarrow F_{r}$ be defined by $\lambda_{u}(x) = ux$.

Let $E = \{\lambda_{u} \mid u \in F_{r}\}$; clearly $E$ is a ring. However, much more can be said.

### THEOREM 4.2
E is an Ore domain, and so has a ring of left quotients K which is a division ring.

### Proof
Since $R$ is prime and $Fr \neq 0, Fr^2 \neq 0$, that is, $EFr \neq 0$; thus $E \neq 0$. Let $\lambda_u \neq 0, \lambda_v \neq 0 \in E$ and suppose that $\lambda_u \lambda_v = 0$; then $uvEr = 0$. Since $\lambda_v \neq 0, vEr \neq 0$; but then since $R \neq l(vFr) D F$ by the maximality of $F$, we conclude that $l(vFr) = F$. Since $u \in l(vFr), u$ must be in $F$ so $\lambda_u = 0$ contrary to assumption. 

We have shown $E$ to be free of zero divisors.

# Claims on Direct Sums and Prime Rings

## Direct Sums of Left Ideals in $E$

Now claim that $E$ has no infinite direct sum of left ideals, for if $E \lambda_{u_{1}} + \ldots + E \lambda_{u_{k}}$ is a direct sum, since $\lambda_{u_{i}} \neq 0$, $F_{r} \cdot u_{i} = E \lambda_{u_{i}}$ from the definition of $\lambda$. Therefore, $F_{r} \cdot u_{1} + \ldots + F_{r} \cdot u_{k}$ is a direct sum.

## Direct Sums in $R$

We claim that $R_{1} + \ldots + R_{k}$ is a direct sum of left ideals of $R$, for if $r_{1} \cdot u_{1} + \ldots + r_{k} \cdot u_{k} = 0$ with $r_{1} \cdot u_{1} \neq 0$, from the primeness of $R$, since $F_{r} \cdot r_{1} \cdot u_{1} \neq 0$, there is a $v \in F_{r}$ with $v \cdot r_{1} \cdot u_{1} \neq 0$. But then $v \cdot r_{1} \cdot u_{1} + \ldots + v \cdot r_{k} \cdot u_{k} = 0$ with $v \cdot r_{1} \cdot u_{1} \neq 0$, contradicting the directness of $F_{r} \cdot u_{1} + \ldots + F_{r} \cdot u_{k}$.

## Implications for $E$

Since $R$ has no infinite direct sum of left ideals, we see that $E$ also has no infinite direct sums of left ideals. By Lemma 4.2, $E$ is an Ore domain. By Theorem 4.1, $E$ has a ring of left quotients which is a division ring $K$.

## Context and Symbols

We return to our study of prime rings, using the symbols $E$, $K$, $F_{r}$, etc., as they were used prior to and in Theorem 4.2. 

- $E$: A prime ring under consideration.
- $K$: The division ring of left quotients of $E$.
- $F_{r}$: A field or subring associated with $E$.
- $R$: Another prime ring related to $E$ in the context of theorems.

We first make the observation that the structure of $E$ and $R$ imposes constraints on the direct sums of left ideals. Specifically, the absence of infinite direct sums in $R$ implies the same for $E$.

## Mathematical Justification

The claim that $E$ has no infinite direct sum of left ideals follows from the structure of $R$ and the properties of prime rings. If $E \lambda_{u_{1}} + \ldots + E \lambda_{u_{k}}$ were an infinite direct sum, it would contradict the primeness of $R$ and the directness of $F_{r} \cdot u_{1} + \ldots + F_{r} \cdot u_{k}$. This reasoning is grounded in the definitions and properties of $\lambda$, $F_{r}$, and the primeness of $R$.

DEFINITION, $\quad V=K \otimes_{E} F_{r}$.  
Notice that $V$ is a left vector space over the division ring $K$. Since $F_{r}$ is a right ideal of $R$, $V$ is a right $R$-module where $(k \otimes a) s = k \otimes (a s)$ for $a \in F_{r}$, $s \in R$. Since $R$ is prime, $V$ is a faithful $R$-module. The elements of $R$ induce $K$-linear transformations on $V$, so we can consider $R$ to be contained in $L(V)$.

Let $j: F_{r} \rightarrow K \otimes_{E} F_{r} = V$ be defined by $j(u) = 1 \otimes u$ for $u \in F_{r}$. $j$ is an injection of $F_{r}$ into $V$. Given $v \in V$, $v = \sum k_{i} \otimes u_{i}$ and since, by Sublemma $A$, $k_{i} = \lambda_{a}^{-1} \lambda_{b_{i}}$, $a, b_{i} \in F_{r}$, we get

$$
v = \lambda_{a}^{-1} \sum \lambda_{b_{i}} \otimes u_{i} = \lambda_{a}^{-1} \sum \left(1 \otimes \lambda_{b_{i}} u_{i}\right) = \lambda_{a}^{-1} \left(1 \otimes \sum b_{i} u_{i}\right),
$$

that is, every $v \in V$ has the form $v = \lambda_{a}^{-1} j(w)$ where $a, w \in F_{r}$.  
We now establish some vital facts about $V$ which will allow us to get the first Goldie theorem.

## THEOREM 4.3  
$V$ is finite-dimensional over $K$.

## Proof

As we have just seen, $V = K_{j}\left(E_{I}\right)$, so we can find a basis of $V$ over $K$ of the form $j\left(u_{\alpha}\right), u_{\alpha}, F_{r}$. 

### Step 1: Linear Independence
If $j\left(u_{1}\right), \ldots, j\left(u_{n}\right), u_{1}, \ldots, u_{n} \in F_{r'}$ are linearly independent over $K$, we claim that $\mathrm{R} u_{1} + \ldots + \mathrm{R} u_{n}$ is a direct sum. 

Suppose that $a_{1} u_{1} + \ldots + a_{n} u_{n} = 0$. If $a_{1} u_{1} \neq 0$, say, then we can find an element $b \in F_{T}$ such that $b a_{1} u_{1} \neq 0$, by the primeness of $\mathrm{R}$. Thus, $b a_{1} u_{1} + \ldots + b a_{n} u_{n} = 0$. Since $b a_{i} \in F_{I}$, this implies that $\lambda_{b a_{1}} j\left(u_{1}\right) + \ldots + \lambda_{b a_{n}} j\left(u_{n}\right) = 0$, contradicting the linear independence of the $j\left(u_{\alpha}\right)$'s over $K$. Therefore, $a_{i} u_{i} = 0$, and the sum is direct.

### Step 2: Finite Dimensionality
Since $\mathrm{R}$ has no infinite direct sums of left-ideals, we conclude that $V$ is finite-dimensional over $K$. 

### Step 3: Dimensionality and Action of $\mathrm{R}$
Since $V$ is $n$-dimensional over $K$ and $\mathrm{R}$ acts faithfully on $V$ as a ring of linear transformations, we get that $\mathrm{R} \subset \mathrm{K}_{n}$, the ring of all $n \times n$ matrices over $K$. 

This is not at all obvious ab initio and gives enough information to prove most of what we would usually want. However, we want to push deeper and find out exactly how $\mathrm{R}$ sits in $\mathrm{K}_{n}$.

### Consequence of Theorem 4.3
We prove an easy consequence of Theorem 4.3: the ring of $K$-linear transformations on $V$ is $\mathrm{K}_{n}$.

**LEMMA 4.4.** If $j\left(u_{1}\right), \ldots, j\left(u_{n}\right) \in V \quad\left(u_{i} \in F_{r}\right)$ is a basis of $V$ over $K$, then $R u_{1} + \ldots + R u_{n}$ intersects every non-zero left-ideal of $R$ in a non-zero fashion.

**Proof.** Let $\mathrm{I} = R u_{1} + \ldots + R u_{n}$ and suppose that $\mathrm{J} \neq \{0\}$ is a left-ideal of $R$ such that $\mathrm{I} \cap \mathrm{J} = \{0\}$. Since $R$ is prime, $\mathrm{J} \cap F_{r} \neq \{0\}$; let $u \neq 0$ be in $\mathrm{J} \cap F_{r}^{*}$.

We claim that $j\left(u_{1}\right), \ldots, j\left(u_{n}\right), j\left(u\right)$ are linearly independent over $K$; this has already been proved in the course of proving Theorem 4.3. Since this contradicts the fact that $j\left(u_{1}\right), \ldots, j\left(u_{n}\right)$ is a basis of $V$, we get the lemma.

**DEFINITION.** We say that a left-ideal $\mathrm{I}$ of $R$ meets all left-ideals of $R$ if, given $\mathrm{J} \neq \{0\}$ a left-ideal of $R$, then $\mathrm{I} \cap \mathrm{J} \neq \{0\}$.

**Sublemma B.** Let $V$ be a vector space of dimension $n$ over a division ring $K$ and suppose that $T_{1}, \ldots, T_{k}$ are linear transformations on $V$ of rank 1 such that:

1. $T_{i}^{2} \neq 0$,
2. $T_{i} T_{j} = 0$ for $j < i$.

Then $S = T_{1} + \ldots + T_{k}$ is of rank $k$ and the null space of $S$ is the intersection of the null spaces of the $T_{i}$ transformations.

Proof. Since $T_{i}$ is of rank 1, $V T_{i} = K v_{i}$ where $v_{i} T_{i} \neq 0$ (since $T_{i}^{2} \neq 0$) and $v_{i} T_{j} = 0$ for $j < i$ (since $T_{i} T_{j} = 0$ for $j < i$). From this, it is trivial that $v_{1} + \ldots + v_{k}$ are linearly independent over $K$ and that $V S$ is the subspace spanned by the $v_{i}$'s. If $v S = 0$ then $v S T_{k} = 0$ giving $v T_{k} = 0$. Also $v S T_{k-1} = 0$, and since $v T_{k} = 0$ this gives $v T_{k-1} = 0$. Continuing, we get $v T_{1} = 0$ for $i = 1, 2, \ldots, k$.

Let $\varphi$ be the isomorphism embedding $R$ into $Q$, the ring of $K$-linear transformations on $V$. It is clear that $a \in R$ is regular if and only if $\varphi(a)$ is invertible in $Q$.

Basic to our needs is the

**Lemma 4.5.**  
If $I$ meets all left ideals of $R$, then $I$ contains a regular element.

**Proof.**  
If $x \in R$ is such that $\varphi(x) \in Q$ has rank 1, then $\mathrm{axb} = 0$ forces $\mathrm{ax} = 0$ or $\mathrm{xb} = 0$. We will find $n$ elements $u_{1}, \ldots, u_{n}$ in $I$ such that:

1. $\varphi\left(u_{i}\right)$ is of rank 1 for each $i$  
2. $u_{i}^{2} \neq 0$  
3. $u_{i} u_{j} = 0$ for $j < i$

# Inductive Proof

We proceed by induction, supposing that we have $u_{1}, \ldots, u_{k}$, $k < n$ with the desired properties. There is $a u \neq 0$ in $F_{r}$ so that $j(u) \varphi\left(u_{i}\right) = 0$ for $i = 1, 2, \ldots, k$, since there is a $j(u)$ annihilated by $\varphi\left(u_{1}\right) + \ldots + \varphi\left(u_{k}\right)$ (which is of rank less than $n$, so by the Sublemma $B$, the result holds). Since $u$ and $F_{r^{\prime}} \varphi(u)$ have rank 1. Also, $\mathrm{Ru} \neq 0$, thus it must intersect $I$; by the primeness of $R$, $u(R u \cap 1) \neq 0$. But then there is $a = r u \neq 0$ in $R_{u} \cap 1$ such that $u r u \neq 0$. 

If $a^{2} = 0$, then $r u r u = 0$, whence, since $\varphi(u)$ is of rank 1, $\mathrm{Ru} = 0$ or $\mathrm{uRu} = 0$; at any rate, $u r u$ would result contrary to choice. Let $u_{k+1} = a$; then $a^{2} \neq 0$, so $u_{k+1}^{2} \neq 0$, $\varphi\left(u_{k+1}\right)$ is of rank 1, $u_{k+1}$ is in $1$, and since $j(u)$ is annihilated by all the $\varphi\left(u_{i}\right)$, $i = 1, 2, \ldots, k$, and $a = r u$, $j\left(u_{k+1}\right) \varphi\left(u_{j}\right) = 0$ for all $j \leq k$. We have established the existence of $u_{1}, \ldots, u_{n}$ with the required properties.

But then $\varphi\left(u_{1} + \ldots + u_{n}\right)$ is of rank $n$, so it is invertible in $Q$. Therefore, $u_{1} + \ldots + u_{n}$ is regular and is in $I$. This proves the lemma.

We pass to the first Goldie theorem.

**THEOREM 4.4.**  
The ring $Q$ of all $K$-linear transformations on $V$ is a left quotient ring for $R$. In other words, a prime Goldie ring has a left quotient ring which is a simple ring with descending chain conditions on left ideals.

**Proof.**  
We already know that a regular element in $R$ picks up an inverse in $Q$. It remains to show that if $m \in Q$, then $m = \varphi(a)^{-1} \varphi(b)$ where $a, b \in R$ and $a$ is regular.

Let $T = \{x \in R \mid \psi(x) m < \varphi(R)\}$; $T$ is clearly a left ideal of $R$. We claim that $T$ meets all left ideals of $R$. Let $I \neq (0)$ be a left ideal of $R$ and let $u \neq 0 \in I \cap F_r$. The element $J(u) m \in V$, so we know that $J(u) m = \lambda_v^{-1} J(w)$ for suitable $v, w \in F_r$. Thus, $\lambda_v J(u) m = J(w)$, giving us $J(vu) m = J(w)$. 

But then for $x \in F_r$, $J(x) \varphi(vu) m = \lambda_x J(vu) m = \lambda_x J(w) = J(x) \varphi(w)$, that is, $\varphi(vu) m = \varphi(w)$. Therefore, $vu \in T$, and since $\lambda_v \neq 0$, $vu \neq 0$. The element $vu$ is in $T \cap I$. By Lemma 4.5, $T$ has a regular element $a$, hence $\varphi(a) m \in \varphi(R)$, that is, $\varphi(a) m = \varphi(b)$. Therefore, $m = \varphi(a)^{-1} \varphi(b)$. This proves the theorem.

We prove a converse to Goldie's theorem. However, since we shall need a wider result for later use, we now prove this more general result.

**DEFINITION**: $R$ is said to be left-order in $S$ if $S$ is a ring of left quotients for $R$.

**THEOREM 4.5**: Let $R$ be a left-order in $S$ where $S$ is a semi-simple ring with descending chain condition. Then $R$ is a Goldie ring having no non-zero nilpotent ideals. Moreover, if $S$ is simple, then $R$ is prime.

**Proof**: We first show that $R$ is a Goldie ring. Since $S$ is semi-simple with descending chain conditions, then, as is well known, $S$ must satisfy the ascending chain condition on left ideals, hence in particular the ascending chain condition on left annihilators. Since this last condition is inherited by subrings, we see that $R$ satisfies condition 1 of the Goldie conditions.

Let $\lambda_{1}, \ldots, \lambda_{k}$ form a direct sum of left ideals of $R$; we claim that $S \lambda_{1}, \ldots, S \lambda_{k}$ form a direct sum of left ideals in $S$. If $s_{1} \alpha_{1} + \ldots + s_{k} \alpha_{k} = 0$ where $s_{i} \in S, \alpha_{i} \in \lambda_{i}$, then, as in Sublemma A, we can write each $s_{i} = s^{-1} b_{i}, s, b_{i} \in R$, $s$ regular, thus $b_{1} \alpha_{1} + \ldots + b_{k} \alpha_{k} = 0$. By the directness of the sum in $R$, we get each $b_{i} \alpha_{i} = 0$, hence $s_{i} \alpha_{i} = 0$. Therefore, $R$ is a Goldie ring.

Let $\mathrm{N} \neq \{0\}$ be a nilpotent ideal in $R$ with $\mathrm{N}^m = \{0\}$, $\mathrm{N}^{m-1} \neq \{0\}$. The ideal $\mathrm{N}_S$ of $S$ is not $\{0\}$; since $S$ is a semisimple ring with minimum condition, $\mathrm{N}_S = \mathrm{eS}$ where $e$ is a central idempotent contained in $\mathrm{N}_S$. 

Now $e = \sum a_i u_i b_i$, $a_i, b_i \in S$ and $u_i \in \mathrm{N}$. We can write each $a_i$ as $a_i = a^{-1} c_i$, where $a \in R$ is regular and $c_i \in R$. Thus, $e = a^{-1} \sum c_i u_i b_i$ whence $e = a^{-1} \sum w_i b_i$, where $w_i \in \mathrm{N}$. 

Since $e$ is central, $a e = e a$. Therefore, $\mathrm{N}^{m-1} a e = \mathrm{N}^{m-1} \sum w_i b_i = \{0\}$. Because $a$ is regular, this forces $\mathrm{N}^{m-1} e = \{0\}$. Therefore, $\left(\mathrm{N}^{m-1} S\right)^2 = \mathrm{N}^{m-1} S \mathrm{N}^{m-1} S \subset \mathrm{N}^{m-1} e S = \{0\}$. Since $S$ is semisimple, this is impossible unless $\mathrm{N}^{m-1} = \{0\}$. This contradiction proves that $\mathrm{N} = \{0\}$.

Finally, suppose that $S$ is simple. Let $A \neq \{0\}$ be an ideal of $R$. Thus, $\mathrm{SAS} = S$. Since $S$ has a unit element $1$, $1 = \sum b_i a_i c_i$, where $b_i, c_i \in S$ and $a_i \in A$. As before, $b_i = b^{-1} d_i$, where $b$ is regular and $d_i \in A$. Thus, $b = \sum d_i a_i c_i \in A S$. 

If for some ideal $B$ of $R$, $B A = \{0\}$, then $B A S = \{0\}$ whence $B b = \{0\}$. The regularity of $b$ forces $B = \{0\}$. Consequently, $R$ is a prime ring.

We have all that we need to prove a theorem due to Faith and Utumi. The very simple, elegant proof we give is due to Claudio Procesi.

**THEOREM 4.6.**  
Let $R$ be a left-order in $\mathrm{D}_{n}$, the ring of $n \times n$ matrices over the division ring $D$. Then we can find an Ore domain $\Lambda$ whose ring of quotients is isomorphic to $D$ and such that $R$ contains a subring isomorphic to $\Lambda_{n}$.

**Proof.**  
By the previous theorem, $R$ is a prime Goldie ring, so it has a ring of left quotients isomorphic to $K_{n}$. By Wedderburn's Theorem, $K_{n}$ is a simple Artinian ring, and hence $K \cong D$, where $K$ is the division ring of quotients of $R$. We use our previous notation $F_{r'}$, $E$, $K_{1}$, $V = K Q_{E} F_{r}$. Here, $F_{r'}$ and $F_{r}$ denote subsets of $K_{n}$ associated with the construction of $V$, where $F_{r}$ is a subset of $K_{n}$ containing elements that form a basis of $V$ over $K$. Since $K, F_{r} \subset K_{n}$, we can identify $V$ with $K F_{r}$. Let $u_{1}, \ldots, u_{n} \in F_{r}$ form a basis of $V$ over $K$. By Lemma 4.4, $R u_{1} + \ldots + R u_{n}$ meets all left ideals of $R$. Hence, by Lemma 4.5, it contains a regular element $c$, where $c = \sum r_{i} u_{i}$. The elements $u_{i} c^{-1}$ still form a basis of $V$. Let  
\[
\Lambda = \left\{\lambda \in E \mid \lambda u_{i} c^{-1} \in F_{r} \ \text{for all } i \right\}.
\]  
Since $v \in V$ can be written as $\lambda_{a}^{-1} w$, where $a, w \in F_{r}$, we know that $\Lambda \neq \{0\}$. Here, $\Lambda \neq \{0\}$ indicates that $\Lambda$ contains non-zero elements, ensuring it is a non-trivial subset of $E$. Now $\Lambda$ is a left ideal of $E$, so by Lemma 4.3, its ring of quotients is also $K$. Now $Q$ contains the ring  
\[
S = \left\{q \in Q \mid u_{i} c^{-1} q \in \sum_{j} \Lambda u_{j} c^{-1} \ \text{for all } i \right\},
\]  
that is, the matrices in the basis $u_{i} c^{-1}$ having entries in $\Lambda$. $S$ is isomorphic to $\Lambda_{n}$.

We claim that $S \subset R$ since if $u_{i} c^{-1} q \in \sum \Lambda u_{j} c^{-1} \subset F_{\mathrm{r}}$ (by definition of $\Lambda$), then  
$q = c c^{-1} q = \left(\sum r_{i} u_{i}\right) c^{-1} q = \sum r_{i}\left(u_{i} c^{-1} q\right) \in R F_{\mathrm{r}} \subset \mathrm{R}$.  
Thus, $\mathrm{R} \supset \mathrm{S}$ and $\mathrm{S} \approx \Lambda_{\mathrm{n}}$.

We should like to extend Goldie's Theorem from prime rings to a much wider context. We do so soon, but first a definition.

# Definition  
A ring $R$ is said to be semi-prime if it has no non-zero nilpotent ideals.

Until further notice, $R$ will denote a semi-prime Goldie ring.

Let $m$ be the set of all two-sided ideals of $R$ which are the left annihilators of non-zero two-sided ideals of $R$.  
If $R$ consisted merely of $(0)$, then $R$ would be a prime ring, so we would know its structure from Theorem 4.4.  
So we may assume that $(0)$ is not maximal in $m$.

As a set of left annihilators, $T$ (which has maximal elements) shall denote these by $A$ or by $A$ with a subscript.  
Our first task is to study these. But first, we note: if $A$ is maximal in $m$ and $r(A)$ is the right-annihilator of $A$,  
then $A$ is precisely the left annihilator of any non-zero ideal of $R$ which is contained in $r(A)$.  
In particular, if $x r(A) = 0$, then $x \in A$.

## Lemma 4.6  
If $A$ is maximal in $m$, then $A$ is a prime ideal of $R$.

## Proof of Lemma 4.7

Suppose that $U, V$ are ideals of $R$ such that $UV \subseteq A$; thus $UV \cdot r(A) = (0)$. Here, $r(A)$ denotes the right annihilator of $A$, defined as $r(A) = \{x \in R \mid Ax = 0\}$. If $V \not\subseteq A$, then $\operatorname{Ann}(A) \neq (0)$ is an ideal of $R$ which is contained in $r(A)$. Since $U$ annihilates $V \cdot r(A)$, $U$ must lie in $A$. Therefore, $A$ is a prime ideal of $R$.

## Corollary

If $A_1 \neq A_2$ are maximal in $m$, then $r(A_1) \subseteq A_2$.

## Lemma 4.7

If $A_1, \ldots, A_n$ are maximal in $m$, then $r(A_1) + \ldots + r(A_n)$ is a direct sum. In particular, $m$ has only a finite number of maximal elements.

## Proof of Lemma 4.7

Consider $B = r(A_i) \cap \left(r(A_1) + \ldots + r(\hat{A}_i) + \ldots + r(A_n)\right)$; then $B$ annihilates $A_i + \prod_{j \neq i} A_j$ from the right. Since the $A_i$ are prime ideals, $\prod_{j \neq i} A_j \not\subseteq A_i$, so $A_i + \prod_{j \neq i} A_j$ annihilates $B$ and is larger than $A_i$. Therefore, by the choice of the $A_i$, $B = (0)$. Thus, the sum $r(A_1) + \ldots + r(A_n)$ is direct.

Since $R$ is a Goldie ring (a ring satisfying the ascending chain condition on right annihilators and having no infinite direct sums of ideals), it has no infinite direct sums of ideals. Hence, there are only a finite number of distinct $r(A_i)$ and so a finite number of maximal elements in $m$.

Let $A_{1}, \ldots, A_{n}$ be a complete list of the maximal elements in $M$. We want to show that $A_{1} \cap A_{2} \cap \ldots \cap A_{n} = \{0\}$. Before doing this, we first make a remark.

### Remark
In any semi-prime ring, if $A, B$ are two-sided ideals such that $AB = \{0\}$, then $BA = \{0\}$.

To see this, merely note that $(BA)^{2} = BABA = \{0\}$ since $AB = \{0\}$. Thus $BA = \{0\}$.

### Lemma 4.8
$A_{1} \cap A_{2} \cap \ldots \cap A_{n} = \{0\}$.

### Proof
Let $W = A_{1} \cap \ldots \cap A_{n}$. Now $W \left(r\left(A_{1}\right) + \ldots + r\left(A_{n}\right)\right) = \{0\}$; consequently, by the above remark, $\left(r\left(A_{1}\right) + \ldots + r\left(A_{n}\right)\right) W = \{0\}$. If $W \neq \{0\}$, then $r\left(A_{1}\right) + \ldots + r\left(A_{n}\right)$ would annihilate a non-zero ideal of $R$ from the left, hence would be contained in an element of $M$ and so in a maximal element of $M$. In short, $r\left(A_{1}\right) + \ldots + r\left(A_{n}\right) \subset A_{j}$ for some $j$; since $r\left(A_{j}\right) \subset r\left(A_{1}\right) + \ldots + r\left(A_{n}\right) \subset A_{j}$, we get $r\left(A_{j}\right)^{2} \subset A_{j} r\left(A_{j}\right) = \{0\}$, forcing $r\left(A_{j}\right) = 0$, a contradiction. Therefore $W = \{0\}$.

We now have an embedding of $R$ into $R / A_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus R / A_{n}$, each of whose constituents, by Lemma 4.6, is a prime ring. We want — in fact, need — that these are themselves Goldie rings. It is towards this goal we proceed, but first, we establish a lemma which holds for any ring.

**LEMMA 4.9.** Let $R$ be any ring and suppose that $\mathrm{A} = \ell(\mathrm{S})$ happens to be a two-sided ideal of $R$. Then if $\bar{\lambda} \subset \overline{\mathrm{R}} = \mathrm{R} / \mathrm{A}$ is a left annihilator in $\overline{\mathrm{R}}$, the left ideal $\lambda = \{\mathrm{x} \in \mathrm{R} \mid \overline{\mathrm{x}} \in \bar{\lambda}\}$ is also a left annihilator in $R$.

**Proof.** Suppose that $\bar{\lambda} = I(\bar{T})$; let $T$ be the inverse image of $\bar{T}$ in $R$. Since $\bar{T} \neq (0)$ (otherwise $\bar{\lambda} = \bar{R}, \lambda = R$), $T \not\subset \mathrm{A}$ so $\mathrm{TS} \neq (0)$. We claim that $\lambda = \ell(\mathrm{TS})$. Clearly, $\lambda \mathrm{TS} = \langle 0 \rangle$ since $\bar{\lambda} \bar{T} = (0)$, hence $\lambda T \subset \mathrm{A}$. Also, if $x T S = (0)$, then $x T \subset \mathrm{A}$ so that $\bar{x} \bar{T} = (0)$, putting $\bar{x} \in \bar{\lambda}$. This proves the lemma.

We return to our semi-prime Goldie ring.

**LEMMA 4.10.** If $\mathrm{A}$ is maximal in $m$, then $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{A}$ is a Goldie ring.

Proof. By Lemma 4.7, there is a correspondence between left annihilators in $R / A$ (since $A = \ell(r(A))$) and certain left annihilators in $R$. Thus, the ascending chain condition on left annihilators in $R$ implies that the same holds in $R / A$.
