What about direct sums of left ideals? Let $\bar{\lambda}_{1}, \ldots, \bar{\lambda}_{k}$ in $R / A$ form a direct sum of left ideals, and let $\lambda_{1}, \ldots, \lambda_{k}$ be their inverse images in $R$. Hence, 

\[
\lambda_{i} \cap \left(\lambda_{1} + \cdots + \lambda_{i-1} + \lambda_{i+1} + \cdots + \lambda_{k}\right) \subset A.
\]

Since $\lambda_{i} \notin A$, $\lambda_{i} r(A) \neq 0$. Therefore, by the remark made earlier, $r(A) \lambda_{i} \neq 0$. Let $\mu_{i} = r(A) \lambda_{i}$; these are non-zero left ideals of $R$ and are contained in the corresponding 

\[
\lambda_{i} \cap r(A).
\]

However, 

\[
\tau_{i} = \mu_{i} \cap \left(\mu_{1} + \cdots + \mu_{i-1} + \mu_{i+1} + \cdots + \mu_{k}\right) \subset \lambda_{i} \cap \left(\lambda_{1} + \cdots + \lambda_{i-1} + \lambda_{i+1} + \cdots + \lambda_{k}\right) \subset A.
\]

Since $\tau_{i}$ is also in $r(A)$, we get $\tau_{i} \in A \cap r(A) = 0$. The $\mu_{i}$ thus give us a direct sum of non-zero left ideals in $R$ of the same length as that given by the $\bar{\lambda}_{i}$'s. Since there are no infinite direct sums of non-zero left ideals in $R$, we get the same conclusion in $R / A$, that is, $R / A$ is a Goldie ring.

We have all the requisite pieces to prove the second Goldie theorem.

**THEOREM 4.7.** Let \( R \) be a semi-prime Goldie ring; then \( R \) has a ring of left quotients which is a semi-simple ring with descending chain conditions on left ideals.

**Proof.** 
- We already know that we have a finite set of prime ideals \( A_{1}, \ldots, A_{n} \) such that each \( R_{i} = R / A_{i} \) is a prime Goldie ring and such that \( R \) is isomorphically embeddable in \( S = R_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus R_{n} \). 
- Moreover, by Theorem 4.4 each \( R_{i} \) has a ring of left quotients \( Q\left(R_{i}\right) \) which is a total matrix ring over a division ring \( D_{i} \).

We claim that \( T = Q\left(R_{1}\right) \oplus \ldots \oplus Q\left(R_{n}\right) \) is a ring of left quotients of \( R \).

- Now for any \( I \neq 0 \), by the corollary to Lemma 4.6, \( r\left(A_{i}\right) \subseteq A_{j} \) so its image in \( S \) lies in \( r\left(A_{i}\right) \), \( r\left(A_{i}\right) \neq 0 \), and is a two-sided ideal of \( R_{i} \). 
- Since \( R_{i} \) is prime, the image of \( r\left(A_{i}\right) \) in \( R_{i} \) must meet every left ideal of \( R_{i} \). Thus, by Lemma 4.5, it contains a regular element \( \bar{c}_{i} \). 
- Let \( c_{i} \in r\left(A_{i}\right) \) be an inverse image of \( \bar{c}_{i} \); the element \( \left(c_{1}, c_{2}, \ldots, c_{n}\right) \) is the image in \( S \) of \( c_{1} + c_{2} + \ldots + c_{n} \), and so, quite easily, is regular. 
- But then \( \left(c_{1}, \ldots, c_{n}\right) \) is invertible in \( T \).

Let 
\[
T^{t} = \left\{a^{-1} b \mid a, b \in R, a \text{ invertible in } T\right\}.
\] 
From the above, 
\[
e_{i} = (0, 0, \ldots, 1, 0, \ldots, 0) = \left(c_{1}, \ldots, c_{n}\right)^{-1} \left(0, \ldots, c_{i}, 0, \ldots, 0\right)
\] 
is in \(T^{\prime}\) (we have identified \(c_{1} + \cdots + c_{n}\) with its image in \(S\)). Consequently, \(T^{\prime} \supset R_{i}\) for each \(i\), where \(R_{i}\) denotes the subring generated by \(c_{i}\). But then \(T^{t} = T\). We have proved that \(T\) is a ring of left quotients for \(R\). This proves the second Goldie theorem.

We now go to the third Goldie theorem, wherein we shall investigate the nature of rings in which every left ideal is principal. The proof we give here is due to Procesi.

**Lemma 4.11.** Let \(R\) be a prime ring in which every left ideal is principal. Then \(R \cong\) a total matrix ring over an Ore domain.

Proof. We use the notation used earlier in proving Theorem 4.4. If \(1 \otimes u_{1}, \ldots, 1 \otimes u_{n}\) is a basis of \(V\) over \(K\), \(u_{i} \in F_{r}\), we have seen in Lemma 4.4 that \(T = \mathrm{R}u_{1} + \ldots + \mathrm{R}u_{n}\) meets all left ideals of \(R\). Thus, by Lemma 4.5, it contains a regular element \(d\). Since every left ideal is principal, \(T = Rc\) and so \(d = rc\). The regularity of \(d\) implies that of \(c\), hence \(c\) is regular.

Moreover, \(c = \sum b_{i} u_{i}\) being in \(T = \mathrm{R}u_{1} + \ldots + \mathrm{R}u_{n}\). Since each \(u_{i} \in T\), \(u_{i} = r_{i}c\), therefore \(c = \sum b_{i}r_{i}c = \left(\sum b_{i}r_{i}\right)c\). Since \(c\) is regular, we deduce from this that \(\sum b_{i}r_{i} = 1\). Since \(u_{i} \in F_{r}\), \(F_{u_{i}} = (0)\), so we get \(F_{r_{i}}c = (0)\), therefore \(F_{r_{i}} = (0)\), that is, each \(r_{i} \in F_{r}\).

We claim that \(1 \otimes r_{1}, \ldots, 1 \otimes r_{n}\) form a basis of \(V\) over \(K\), since the \(1 \otimes \left(r_{i}c\right)\) do and \(c\) is regular. If \(u \in F_{r}\), then \(u = ui = \sum ub_{i}r_{i}\), \(ub_{i} \in F_{r}\), so that \(u = \sum \lambda_{ub_{i}}j\left(r_{i}\right)\) and hence \(F_{r}\) is a free \(R\)-module of rank \(n\) with generators \(j\left(r_{1}\right), \ldots, j\left(r_{n}\right)\).

Every element in $R$ induces an $E$-endomorphism on $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{r}}$. Given an $E$-endomorphism of $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{r}}$, by Goldie's theorem (Theorem 4.4), it is inducible by right multiplication by $a^{-1} b$, where $a, b \in R$ and $a$ is regular. Since $a^{-1} b$ is an $E$-endomorphism of $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{r}}$, each $\mathrm{R}_{i} \cdot a^{-1} b$ is in $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{r}}$. But 

\[
a^{-1} b = 1 \cdot a^{-1} b = \left(\sum b_{i} \cdot \mathrm{R}_{i}\right) \cdot a^{-1} b = \sum b_{i} \cdot \left(\mathrm{R}_{i} \cdot a^{-1} b\right)
\]

is an element of $R \cdot \mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{r}} \subset R$ since each $\mathrm{R}_{i} \cdot a^{-1} b \in \mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{r}}$. Therefore, $a^{-1} b \in R$ and the result is proved.

---

One could ask if the Ore domain in the statement of the lemma also has every left ideal principal. This is false as is shown by an example due to Swan.

---

**Lemma 4.12**  
A semi-prime ring whose left ideals are principal is a direct sum of a finite number of prime rings.

**Proof**  
In the proof of Theorem 4.7, it is enough to show that $A_{i} + A_{j} = R$ for $i \neq j$, for then the result holds by the Chinese Remainder Theorem.

Now $A_{i} = R \cdot a$, $A_{j} = R \cdot b$, so $A_{i} + A_{j} = R \cdot t$. The element $t$ is regular, for if $t \cdot x = 0$, then $A_{i} \cdot x = (0)$ and $A_{j} \cdot x = (0)$, so 

\[
x \in \mathrm{R}(A_{i}) \cap \mathrm{R}(A_{j}) = (0).
\]

However, $t = xa + yb$, $a = ut$, $b = vt$, so $(xu + yv)t = t$, whence $xu + yv = 1$. If $I = \ell(Ra)$, then since $IA_{i} = (0)$ and $A_{i}I = (0)$ by the semi-primeness of $R$, it follows that $I = r\left(Ra\right)$. But $IRa = (0)$ forces $IRu = (0)$, hence $RuI = (0)$. Since $Ru\left(\ell\left(r\left(A_{i}\right)\right)\right) = A_{i}$, we get $Ru \subseteq Ra$. Similarly, $Rv \subseteq Rb$, so $1 \in Ru + Rv \subseteq Ra + Rb = A_{i} + A_{j}$. Thus $A_{i} + A_{j} = R$.

### Definition
$R$ is a primary ring if $R / N$ is a prime ring where $N$ is the maximal nilpotent ideal of $R$.

### Lemma 4.13
A principal left ideal ring is the direct sum of primary rings.

### Proof
Let $N$ be the maximal nilpotent ideal of $R$. By Lemma 4.10, $R / N = \bar{R}_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus \bar{R}_{k}$, where the $\bar{R}_{i}$ are prime rings. We want to show that $R = R_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus R_{k}$, where $R_{i}$ maps onto $\bar{R}_{i}$ modulo $N$.

Now $\overline{\mathrm{R}}=\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{N}=\overline{\mathrm{A}} \oplus \overline{\mathrm{B}}$ where $\overline{\mathrm{A}}$ is semi-prime and $\overline{\mathrm{B}}$ is prime $\left(\overline{\mathrm{B}}=\mathrm{R}_{k}, \overline{\mathrm{A}}=\overline{\mathrm{R}}_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus \overline{\mathrm{R}}_{k-1}\right)$. Now $r(\overline{\mathrm{A}})=\ell(\overline{\mathrm{A}})=\overline{\mathrm{B}}$ and $r(\overline{\mathrm{B}})=\ell(\overline{\mathrm{B}})=\overline{\mathrm{A}}$ by the semi-primeness of $\overline{\mathrm{A}}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{B}}$. Let $\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{B}$ be the inverse images of $\overline{\mathrm{A}}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{B}}$ respectively. Thus $\mathrm{A}=\mathrm{R}\mathrm{a}, \mathrm{B}=\mathrm{R}\mathrm{b}$ and $\mathrm{NC}(\mathrm{R}\mathrm{a} \cap \mathrm{R}\mathrm{b})$. If $\mathrm{x}\mathrm{a} \in \mathrm{N}$ then $\overrightarrow{\mathrm{x}} \overrightarrow{\mathrm{a}}=0$; but $\overline{\mathrm{R}} \overline{\mathrm{a}}=\overline{\mathrm{A}}$ so $\overline{\mathrm{a}}$ is regular in $\overline{\mathrm{A}}$ which forces $\overline{\mathrm{x}} \in \overline{\mathrm{B}}$, hence $\mathrm{x}\mathrm{a} \in \mathrm{N}$ implies that $\mathrm{x} \in \mathrm{B}$. Since $\mathrm{N} \subset \mathrm{CR}\mathrm{a}$ we get $\mathrm{N} \subset \mathrm{CR}\mathrm{b}\mathrm{a}$ (for $\mathrm{n} \in \mathrm{N}$, $\mathrm{n}=\mathrm{x}\mathrm{a}$ forces $\mathrm{x} \in \mathrm{B}=\mathrm{R}\mathrm{b}$) and $\mathrm{b}\mathrm{a} \in \mathrm{N}$ since $\overline{\mathrm{b}} \overline{\mathrm{a}}=0$. Thus $\mathrm{N}=\mathrm{R}\mathrm{b}\mathrm{a}$.

Also $\mathrm{N}=\mathrm{R}\mathrm{a}\mathrm{b} \subset \mathrm{R}\mathrm{a}\mathrm{R}\mathrm{b} \subset \mathrm{N}$ so we find that $\mathrm{A}\mathrm{B}=\mathrm{B}\mathrm{A}=\mathrm{A} \cap \mathrm{B}=\mathrm{N}$. We claim that for any integer $m>0$, $\mathrm{R}=\mathrm{A}^{m}+\mathrm{B}^{m}$; we go by induction. Now $\mathrm{R}=\mathrm{R}^{2}=\left(\mathrm{A}^{m}+\mathrm{B}^{m}\right)(\mathrm{A}+\mathrm{B})=\mathrm{A}^{m+1}+\mathrm{B}^{m+1}+\mathrm{U}$ where $\mathrm{U} \subset \mathrm{N}$. Since $\mathrm{N} \subset \mathrm{A}$ and $\mathrm{N} \subset \mathrm{B}$ and $\mathrm{U} \subset \mathrm{N}=\mathrm{N}\mathrm{R}=\mathrm{N}\left(\mathrm{A}^{m}+\mathrm{B}^{m}\right) \subset \mathrm{A}^{m+1}+\mathrm{B}^{m+1}$ we get $\mathrm{R}=\mathrm{A}^{m+1}+\mathrm{B}^{m+1}$.

Since we have the ascending chain condition on left ideals in $R$, there is an integer $k$ such that $t(a^k) = t(a^{k+1}) = \ldots$. But then $Ra^k \cap \ell(a^k) = (0)$, for if $x \in Ra^k \cap \ell(a^k)$, then $0 = x a^k a^k = x a^{2k}$, hence $x \in I(a^{2k}) = I(a^k)$, giving us $x a^k = 0$.

Suppose that $N^t = (0)$; pick $m > \max(k, t)$. Then $B^m A^m = (BA)^m = (0)$ (since $BA = AB = N$); also $B^m \subset I(a^m)$, so $B^m \cap R^m = (0)$. Since $A = Ra$ is a 2-sided ideal, $aR = Ra$, hence $A^m = (Ra)^m \subset Ra^m \subset A^m$, thus $A^m = Ra^m$ and $B^m \cap A^m = (0)$.

Since $A^m \oplus B^m = R$, and since $B^m$ maps onto $\bar{B}$ and $\bar{A}^m$ maps onto $\overleftarrow{A}$ modulo $N$, we get the result.

We now prove the third Goldie theorem.

**THEOREM 4.8.** Let $R$ be a principal left ideal ring which satisfies the ascending chain condition on right ideals. Then $R$ is the direct sum of a semi-prime ring in which all left ideals are principal and a ring with descending chain condition on left and right ideals.

**Proof.** In light of the preceding lemmas, in order to prove the theorem, it is enough to show that a primary but not prime ring of this type satisfies the descending chain conditions.

Let $\mathrm{R}$ be a primary ring and let $(0) \neq \mathrm{N}$ be its maximal nilpotent ideal. We first want to show that $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{N}$ satisfies the descending chain condition, or in other words (by Goldie's theorem) that $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{N}$ is its own ring of left quotients.

We claim that we can reduce to the case in which $\mathrm{N}^2 = (0)$. For this, note that:
\[
\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{N} \cong \left(\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{N}^2\right) / \left(\mathrm{N} / \mathrm{N}^2\right),
\]
where $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{N}^2$ is primary but not prime (if $\mathrm{N}^2 \neq (0)$). Thus, we may assume $\mathrm{N}^2 = (0)$.

1. $r(N) = N$. For if $r(N) \neq N$, being an ideal in $R$ which contains $N$, $r(N)/N \neq \{0\}$ is an ideal of $R/N$ which is prime, so meets all left ideals of $\mathrm{R}/N$. Hence, it has a regular element. So there would be an element $c \in R$ with $N_c = \{0\}$ where $\bar{c}$ is regular in $\overline{R} = R/N$. Since $\bar{c}$ is not nilpotent, neither is $c$, and there is an integer $k$ such that $\ell(c^k) \cap Rc^k = \{0\}$. Thus, since $N \cap Rc^k = \{0\}$, $N + Rc^k$ is a left ideal. So $N + Rc^k = Rd$, $c^k = sd$, $n = rd$, $an + bc \cdot c^k = d$. Therefore, in $\overline{R}$, $\bar{d}$ is regular (since $\bar{c}^k$ is), and since $\bar{r} \bar{d} = 0$, $\bar{r} = 0$, so $x \in N$. Thus, $ran + rbc^k = rd = n$; since $ran \in N^2 = \{0\}$ and $rb \in N$, $rbc^k = 0$, we get $n = 0$, a contradiction.

2. If $\bar{c}$ is regular in $\bar{R}$, then $Nc = N = Nc^k$. For if $c \rightarrow \bar{c}$, then $Rn + Rc = Rd$ where $\bar{d}$ is regular in $\bar{R}$ (since $\bar{c} = \overline{sd}$). Now $n = td$ and $d = pn + qc$; since $\overline{td} = 0$, $t \in N$ since $\bar{d}$ is regular. So $n = td = tpn + tqc = tqc$ since $tpn \in N^2 = \{0\}$. Thus, $N \subset Nc \subset N$ proving $Nc = N$, and so also $Nc^k = N$.

3. If $\bar{c}$ is regular in $\bar{R}$, then $c$ is left-regular in $R$, that is, $t(c) = \{0\}$. For we have $N = Nc^k$; pick $k$ so that $Rc^k \cap \ell(c^k) = \{0\}$. Since $\bar{c}$ is regular, $\ell(c^k) \subset N = Nc^k$, hence $\ell(c^k) = \{0\}$. Thus, $c^k$, and so $c$, is left-regular.

We finish the proof of the theorem. Let $\overline{\mathrm{C}}$ be regular in $\overline{\mathrm{R}}$ and pick $m \in N$ such that $Rm = N$ and $mR$ is maximal over all such $m$. Now, since $\mathrm{Nc} = \mathrm{N}$, $\mathrm{m} = \mathrm{m}_1 \mathrm{c}$ with $\mathrm{m}_1 \in \mathrm{N}$. Also, $\mathrm{Nc} = \mathrm{N} = \mathrm{Rm} = \mathrm{Rm} \mathrm{m}_1 \mathrm{c}$. Hence, since $c$ is left-regular, $\mathrm{N} = \mathrm{Rm} \mathrm{m}_1$. 

Since $m = m_1 c < m_1 R$, by the maximality of $mR$, we must have $mR = m_1 R$, that is, $m_1 = mx = m_1 cx$. Hence, $m_1(1 - cx) = 0$.

Consequently, $1 - cx$ and $r(N) = N$, whence in $\overline{R} = R / N$, $1 = \overline{cx}$. So, $\overline{c}$ is invertible in $\overline{\mathrm{R}}$. This proves $\overline{\mathrm{R}} \approx \mathrm{D}_n$ by Goldie's theorem, so it certainly satisfies the descending chain conditions.

We now must merely show that $R$ also satisfies the descending chain conditions. Now, $N^k = (0)$, and the $R / N$-modules $N / N^2$, $N^2 / N^3$, $\ldots$, $N^i / N^{i+1}$ are finitely generated. The nature of $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{N}$ from above then tells us $N$ has a finite composition series, which, together with the fact that $R / N$ also has one, tells us $R$ satisfies the descending chain condition. 

A recent example due to Jategaonkar shows that one cannot drop the assumption that $R$ be right Noetherian.

# Goldie's Theorem for Semi-Prime Rings

We close this chapter with a fast, simple, and self-contained proof of Goldie's theorem for semi-prime rings recently worked out jointly by Procesi and Small.

## Lemma 4.14

If \( A \supset B \) are left ideals of \( R \) and \( r(A) \subset r(B) \) but \( r(A) \neq r(B) \), then there exists an \( a \in A \) such that \( A_a \neq (0) \) and \( A a \cap B = (0) \).

### Proof

Let \( U \) be a right annihilator minimal with respect to being contained in \( r(B) \) and properly containing \( r(A) \). By choice, \( AU \neq (0) \), and since there are no nilpotent ideals, \( AU \cdot AU \neq (0) \). Pick \( u a \in AU \) such that \( A u a U \neq (0) \). We claim that \( A u a \cap B = (0) \). 

If not, \( 0 \neq x u a \in A u a \cap B \) where \( x \in A \). Since \( x u a \in B \), and \( U \subset r(B) \), \( x u a U = (0) \). Since \( x \in A \), \( r(x) \supset r(A) \). Consider \( r(x) \cap U \); it is a right annihilator, contains \( r(A) \), and is contained in \( U \). Moreover, since \( u a \notin r(x) \cap U \) and is not in \( r(A) \), we get that \( r(x) \cap U = U \), that is, \( U \subset r(x) \), so \( xU = (0) \), contradicting that \( x u a \neq 0 \). This proves the lemma.

## Definition

A left ideal \( I \) is **essential** if it intersects every non-zero left ideal non-trivially.

## Corollary 1

If \( R_x \) and \( R_y \) are essential, then \( R_{xy} \) is essential.

Proof. Let $\mathrm{A} \neq 0$ be a left ideal of $R$ and let $\bar{A} = \{r \in R \mid r y \in A\}$. By the essentiality of $R y$, $\bar{A} y = R y \cap A \neq 0$. By definition, $\bar{A} \supset \ell(y)$ and $\bar{A} y \neq 0$, so we can apply Lemma 4.14 to $\bar{A}$ and $\ell(y)$ to get a left ideal $T \neq 0$ such that $T \subset \bar{A}$ and $T \cap \ell(y) = 0$. Let $\bar{T} = \{r \in R \mid r x \in T\}$; thus $\bar{T} x = R x \cap T \neq 0$, and $\bar{T} x y \neq 0$ since $T \cap \ell(y) = 0$. Thus, since $T x y \subset T y \subset A y \subset A$, we get $R x y \cap A \neq 0$, proving that $R x y$ is essential.

**COROLLARY 2.** If $\mathrm{R}a$ is essential, then $a$ is regular.  
Proof. That $r(a) = 0$ follows from the special case of Lemma 4.14 in which $A = R$ and $B = R a$.

We consider $\ell(a)$. We know there is an integer $k$ such that $\ell(a^{k}) = \ell(a^{k+1}) = \ldots$. If $x \in R a^{k} \cap \ell(a)$, then $x = y a^{k}$ and $0 = x a = y a^{k+1}$, placing $y \in I(a^{k+1}) = I(a^{k})$, that is, $x = 0$. Since $R a^{k}$ is essential by Corollary 1, we get that $I(a) = 0$.

From now on, $R$ will be a semi-prime Goldie ring.

**LEMMA 4.15.** $R$ satisfies the descending chain condition on left annihilators.

**Proof.** Let $L_{1} \supset L_{2} \supset \ldots \supset L_{n} \supset \ldots$ be a properly descending chain of left annihilators. By Lemma 4.14, there exist non-zero left ideals $C_{n} \subset L_{n}$ such that $C_{n} \cap L_{n+1} = \{0\}$. Since the $C_{n}$ form a direct sum of left ideals, we get that the chain of annihilators must terminate.

**LEMMA 4.16.** If $f(c) = \{0\}$, then $\mathrm{Rc}$ is essential, and so $c$ is regular.

**Proof.** If $\mathrm{A} \neq \{0\}$ is a left ideal satisfying $\mathrm{Rc} \cap \mathrm{A} = \{0\}$, since $\ell(c) = \{0\}$, we get that the $\mathrm{A}c^{n}$ form a direct sum of left ideals. Therefore, $\mathrm{Rc} \cap \mathrm{A} \neq \{0\}$, and $\mathrm{Rc}$ is essential.

A two-sided ideal of $S$ is said to be an annihilator ideal if $S$ is the left annihilator of some left ideal $T$.

**LEMMA 4.17.**  
a) A non-zero minimal annihilator ideal is a prime Goldie ring.  
b) There is a finite direct sum of such ideals which is an essential left ideal of $R$.

**Proof.**  
a) Let $S$ be a minimal annihilator ideal. If $T \neq \{0\}$ is a left ideal of $S$, since $R$ is semi-prime, $ST \neq \{0\}$. Since $ST \subseteq T$, $ST$ is a left ideal of $R$. This immediately implies that $S$ has no infinite direct sums of left ideals. It is immediate that the chain condition on left annihilators is inherited by subrings; hence, $S$ is a Goldie ring.  
b) Since $R$ satisfies the descending chain condition on left annihilators, there exists a finite direct sum of minimal annihilator ideals which is an essential left ideal of $R$.

Suppose that $AB = 0$ where $A$ and $B$ are ideals of $S$. Here, $A$ and $B$ are explicitly defined as ideals of $S$. Since $SB \subset B$, we have $ASB = 0$. Thus, $A \subset (SB \cap S)$. To justify this, note that $ASB = 0$ implies $A$ annihilates $SB$, and since $SB \subset S$, it follows that $A \subset (SB \cap S)$. If $A \neq 0$, then by the minimality of $S$ (i.e., $S$ is minimal with respect to being a prime Goldie ring), we deduce $S \subset \ell(SB)$, where $\ell(SB)$ denotes the left annihilator of $SB$. Since $SB \subset S$, this implies $SB = 0$, and consequently $B = 0$. In summary, $S$ is a prime Goldie ring.

### Lemma 4.18  
If $I$ is an essential left ideal of $R$, then it contains a regular element.

**(b)** Let $A = S_1 \oplus S_2 \oplus \ldots \oplus S_n$ be a maximal direct sum of minimal annihilator ideals, where $S_1, S_2, \ldots, S_n$ are minimal annihilator ideals of $R$. We claim that $A$ is essential. Suppose that $A \cap K = 0$, where $K$ is a non-zero left ideal of $R$. Since $AK \subset A \cap K = 0$, it follows that $AK = 0$. Thus, $K \subset r(A)$, where $r(A)$ denotes the right annihilator of $A$. Since $R$ is semi-prime, we have $A \cap r(A) = 0$. Furthermore, since $r(A)A = 0$ (as $R$ is semi-prime), we can find a minimal, non-zero annihilator ideal in $r(A)$. This allows us to lengthen the direct sum defining $A$, contradicting the maximality of $A$. Therefore, $A$ is essential.

# Proof

We first establish the result for prime rings. Choose an $a$ so that $\ell(a)$ is minimal. If $a$ is regular, we are done; if not, by Corollary 2 to Lemma 4.14, $\mathrm{Ra}_{a} \cap \mathrm{J} = \{0\}$ for some left ideal $\mathrm{J} \neq \{0\}$. Since $\mathrm{I}$ is essential, $\mathrm{I} \cap \mathrm{J} \neq \{0\}$; hence we may suppose that $\mathrm{J} \subseteq \mathrm{I}$. If $x \in \mathrm{J}$, $r \in \ell(a+x)$, $r(a+x) = 0$, hence $r a = -r x \in \mathrm{Ra} \cap \mathrm{J} = \{0\}$, thus $r \in \ell(a) \cap \ell(x)$. By the minimality of $\ell(a)$, we get $\ell(a) \subseteq \ell(x)$ for all $x \in \mathrm{J}$, thus $\ell(a) \mathrm{J} = \{0\}$. Since $\mathrm{J} \neq \{0\}$ and $R$ is prime, $\ell(a) = \{0\}$ results. Lemma 4.16 then tells us that $a$ is regular.

Now let $R$ be semi-prime and let $A = S_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus S_{n}$ be as in Lemma 4.17. It is clear that $\mathrm{I} \cap S_{i}$ is essential in $S_{i}$. Since $S_{i}$ is prime, $\mathrm{I} \cap S_{i}$ contains an element $r_{i}$ regular in $S_{i}$. Let $r = r_{1} + \ldots + r_{n}$; we claim that $r$ is regular in $R$. If not, $\ell(r) \cap A \neq \{0\}$, so we can find $t_{1}, \ldots, t_{n} \in S_{1}, \ldots, S_{n}$ respectively, and not all $0$, such that $t r = \sum t_{i} r_{i} = 0$. Then $t_{i} r_{i} = 0$ for all $i$, violating the regularity of the $r_{i}$ in $S_{i}$. The element $r$ is the required regular element in $R$.

# Theorem 4.9

Let $R$ be a semi-prime Goldie ring. Then $R$ has a ring of left quotients $Q$.

### Proof

Suppose that $a \in R$ is regular and $b \in R$. By Lemma 4.16, $Ra$ is essential. Moreover, as can be readily verified, $M = \{r \in R \mid rb \in Ra\}$ is also essential. Thus, by Lemma 4.18, $M$ contains a regular element $c$. Therefore, $cb = da \neq 0$, where $c$ and $d$ are elements of $R$ such that this equality holds. The condition for a left quotient ring to exist, as given in Theorem 4.1, holds in $R$, so $R$ has a ring of left quotients.

### Remarks

We note the following two facts without proof:

1. If $I$ is a left ideal of $Q$, then $I = Q(I \cap R)$.
2. If $A_1 \oplus \ldots \oplus A_n$ is a direct sum of left ideals in $R$, then $QA_1 \oplus \ldots \oplus QA_n$ is a direct sum in $Q$.

### Theorem 4.10

**Theorem 4.10.** $Q$ is a semi-simple ring satisfying the descending chain condition on left ideals.

### Reproof of Goldie's Theorem (Theorem 4.7)

We now reprove Goldie's theorem.

## Proof

If $I \neq (0)$ is a left ideal of $Q$, then there is a left ideal $K$ in $R$ such that $(I \cap R) \oplus K$ is essential in $R$. Take $K$ to be the complement in a largest direct sum containing $I \cap R$ as a constituent.

Since $Q$ is a left quotient ring for $R$, and $(I \cap R) \oplus K$ is essential in $R$, we get that $Q = I \oplus QK$.

If $1$ is the unit element in $Q$, $i = e + f$, where $e \in I$, $f \in QK$. Thus, $e = e^{2} + ef$, giving $e^{2} = e \neq 0$. Also, $I = Ie = Qe$.

Thus, every non-zero left ideal in $Q$ is generated by an idempotent. In particular, $Q$ can have no non-zero nilpotent ideals, so is semiprime.

Since every left ideal of $Q$ is principal, $Q$ satisfies the ascending chain condition on all left ideals, so has no infinite direct sums of left ideals.

Let $L \neq (0)$ be a left ideal of $Q$. As we have seen, $L = Qe$, so $r(L) = r(Qe) = (1-e)Q$, whence $\ell(r(L)) = \ell((1-e)Q) = eQ = L$. Therefore, every left ideal of $Q$ is a left annihilator.

However, by Lemma 4.15, $Q$ satisfies the descending chain condition on left annihilators, thus on all left ideals. Since, as we have seen, $Q$ has no nilpotent ideals and satisfies the descending chain condition, it must be semi-simple.

## Explanation of Formulas

- $Q = I \oplus QK$: This formula expresses that $Q$ can be decomposed into the direct sum of $I$ and $QK$.
- $e = e^{2} + ef$: Here, $e$ is shown to be idempotent, as $e^{2} = e \neq 0$.
- $I = Ie = Qe$: This indicates that $I$ is generated by the idempotent $e$.
- $r(L) = r(Qe) = (1-e)Q$: This formula defines the right annihilator of $L$.
- $\ell(r(L)) = \ell((1-e)Q) = eQ = L$: This shows that the left annihilator of the right annihilator of $L$ is $L$ itself.

## Chapter 5

### More on Rings with Ascending Chain Conditions

We begin this chapter with an exposition of some results obtained jointly with Lance Small. We shall study nil subrings of rings satisfying the ascending chain condition on left annihilators. Our objective is to show that, quite generally, these are nilpotent.

Until further notice, $R$ shall be a ring satisfying the ascending chain condition on left annihilators. It is appropriate to remind the reader that this is equivalent to the descending chain condition on right annihilators. Also, since the left annihilator in a subring is merely the intersection of this subring with the left annihilator in the entire ring, our condition is inherited by all subrings of $R$.

## We begin with

### **LEMMA 5.1**  
Let $R$ be a ring satisfying the ascending chain condition on left annihilators. If $R$ is nil, then every nonzero homomorphic image of $R$ contains a non-zero nilpotent ideal.

> **Proof**  
> Let $\bar{R}$ be a non-zero homomorphic image of $R$; thus $\bar{R}$ is isomorphic to $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{M}$ where $\mathrm{M} \neq \mathrm{R}$ is a two-sided ideal of $R$.  
>  
> Let $Z = \{\ell(x) \mid x \in \mathrm{R}, x \notin \mathrm{M}\}$; by hypothesis, $\ell(x)$ has a maximal element, that is, there is an element $a \in R$ and $a \notin \mathrm{M}$ such that $\ell(a)$ is maximal in $Z$.

For any $x$ in $R$, $\ell(a x) \to \ell(a)$; if, in addition, $a x \notin M$, then by the maximality of $t(a)$, we are forced to conclude that $\ell(a x) = \ell(a)$. Suppose that $a r \notin M$; since $a r$ is nilpotent, there is an integer $k$ such that $(a r)^{k} \in M$ but $(a r)^{k-1} \notin M$. However, $a r$ is nilpotent; thus, there is a least positive integer $n$ such that $(a r)^{n} = 0$. Since $(a r)^{k-1}$ is of the form $a x$ and is not in $M$, $\ell\left((a r)^{k-1}\right) = \ell(a)$. Because $(a r)^{n-k+1}$ is in $\ell\left((a r)^{k-1}\right)$, we are led to $(a r)^{n-k+1} a = 0$, and so $(a r)^{n-k+2} = (a r)^{n-k+1} a r = 0$. By our choice of $n$, we must conclude that $n-k+2 \geq n$, and so $k \leq 2$. Consequently, $(a x)^{2}$ is in $M$ for every $x$ in $R$.

For any $x$ in $R$, since $x+1$ is formally invertible, we have that $a x + a \notin M$; from this, it results that $\ell(a x + a) = \ell(a)$, and the argument used above yields $(a x + a)^{2} \in M$.

In $\overline{\mathrm{R}}=\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{M}$ these translate into: $\overline{\mathrm{a}} \neq 0,(\overline{\mathrm{a}} \overline{\mathrm{x}})^{2}=0$, $(\overline{\mathrm{a}} \overrightarrow{\mathrm{x}}+\overrightarrow{\mathrm{a}})^{2}=0$ for all $\overrightarrow{\mathrm{x}}$, which immediately yield that $\overrightarrow{\mathrm{a}}^{2}=0$ and also $\overline{\mathrm{a} \times \overline{\mathrm{a}}}=0$ for all $\overline{\mathrm{x}}$ in $\overline{\mathrm{R}}$. Thus $\overline{\mathrm{a}} \overline{\mathrm{R}}$ is a nilpotent right ideal of $\overline{\mathrm{R}}$, If $\overline{\mathrm{a}} \overline{\mathrm{R}}=(0)$ then the left annihilator of $\overline{\mathrm{R}}$, would be a non-zero nilpotent ideal. If $\overline{\mathrm{a}} \overline{\mathrm{R}} \neq 0$ is generates a nilpotent two-sided ideal. In this way the lemma has been established.

As an immediate corollary we have the basic result:
COROLLARY. If R is a nil ring satisfying the ascending chain condition on left annihilators then R is locally nilpotent.

Proof. As is well known (see for instance (N)) $R$ contains a maximal locally nilpotent ideal $L(R)$, the Levitzki radical of $R$, which contains all locally nilpotent ideals of R. Moreover, $L(R / L(R))=(0)$, hence $R / L(R)$ certainly contains no nilpotent. ideals.

If $L(R) \neq R$, by the lemma, $R / L(R)$ would have a non-zero nilpotent ideal, which it can not. Thus $R / L(R)=(0)$, hence $R=L(R)$. Therefore, $R$ is locally nilpotent.

We continue the investigation of our class of nil rings with
LEMMA 5.2. Let $R$ be a nil ring satisfying the ascending chain condition on left annihilators. Then there exists an element $\mathrm{x}_{0} \neq 0$ in R such that $R \mathrm{x}_{0}=(0)$.

Proof. Let $M$ be the set of finitely generated subrings in $R$ and let $\eta=\{r(S) \mid S, m\}$. By the remark previously made, there is an $S_{0}$ in $M$ such thar $r\left(S_{0}\right)$ is minimal in $n$. Let $x \in R$; then $S_{1}$, the subring generated by $S_{0}$ and $x_{1}$ is finitely generated. Since $S_{0}\left(S_{1}, r\left(S_{0}\right) \supset r\left(S_{1}\right)\right.$, whence, by the minimality of $r\left(S_{0}\right)$ we conclude that $r\left(S_{0}\right)=r\left(S_{1}\right)$. However, $r\left(S_{1}\right)=r\left(S_{0}\right) \cap r(x)$; together with the above equality we conclude that $r\left(S_{0}\right)=r\left(S_{0}\right) \cap r(x)$, that is $r\left(S_{0}\right) C_{r}(x)$ for every $x$ in $R$. But then $\operatorname{Rr}\left(S_{0}\right)=(0)$.

To finish the proof we note that since $S_{0}$ is finitely generated by the corollary to Lemma 5.1, it must be nilpotent. Hence for some integer $k, S_{o}^{k}=(0), S_{o}^{k-1} \neq(0)$. Since $(0) \neq S_{o}^{k-1} \subset r\left(S_{o}\right)$, thus: $r\left(S_{0}\right) \neq(0)$ and we have proved the lemma.

LEMMA 5.3. If R satisfies the ascending chain condition on left (respectively, right) annihilators and if A is a two-sided ideal of R which happens to be a left annihilator in R then $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{A}$ satisfies the ascending chain condition on left (respectively, right) annihilators.

Proof. As we have previously observed the ascending chain condition on right annihilators translates into the descending chain condition on left annihilators. In view of the one-to-one correspondence set up by Lemma 4.7 between the left annihilators
in $\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{A}$ and certain left annihilators in R the lemma now becomes clear.

We are now in a position to prove the first of the theorems of this chapter.

THEOREM 5.1. If $R$ is a ring which satisfies the ascending chain conditions on left and right annihilators then any nil subring of R is milpotent.

Proof. Since the ascending chain conditions on left and right annihilators are inherited from the ring by its subrings we may, without loss of generality, assume that $R$ is nil. We wish to show that $R$ is nilpotent. Let $T_{k}=\left\{x \in R \mid \times R^{k}=(0)\right\}$. Since $\mathrm{T}_{1} \subset \mathrm{~T}_{2} \subset \ldots . \mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{k}} \subset \ldots$ and since they are left annihilators, by our hypothesis there is an $n$ such that $T_{n}=T_{n+1}=\ldots$. If $T_{n}=R$ then $R^{n+1}=(0)$ and we are done. Suppose $T_{n} \neq R$; then $\overline{\mathrm{R}}=\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{n}} \neq(0)$. By Lemma $5.3, \overline{\mathrm{R}}$ satisfies the ascending chain condition on right annihilators so by Lemma 5.2 (in its reflected form) there exists an $\bar{x} \neq 0$ in $\bar{R}$ such that $\bar{x} \bar{R}=(0)$. Therefore $x R C T_{n}$ (where $x$ is an inverse image of $\bar{x}$ in $R$ ) and so $x R R^{n}=(0)$. Thus $x R^{n+1}=(0)$; from our choice of $n$ this forces $x R^{D}=(0)$ and so $x \in T_{n}$. But then $\bar{x}=0$ contrary to hypothesis. This contradiction proves that $\bar{R}=(0)$ and so $R=T_{n}$ and $R^{n+1}=(0)$.

If one examines the proof given for Theorem 5.1, one sees that a more general result has actually been proved, that only a part of the ascending chain condition on the right has been used, The exact theorem proved was:

THEOREM 5.1'. Let $R$ be a nil ring satisfying the ascending chain condition on left annihilators and such that the ascending chain of right annihilators $\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{n}}=\left\{\mathrm{x} \mid \mathrm{R}^{\mathrm{n}} \mathrm{x}=(0)\right\}$ becomes stationary. Then R is nilpotent,

An example by Sasiada shows that one can not get by with just the ascending chain condition on left annihilators. Let R be the ring generated over the integers by the elements $x_{1}, x_{2}, \ldots, x_{n}, \ldots$ subject to the conditions $x_{j} x_{i}=0$ for $j \geq i$. Then as can easily be checked, $R$ is nil, satisfies the ascending chain condition on left annihilators yet is not nilpotent.

We now consider nil rings satisfying a polynomial identity. Using a very simple argument due to Posner (see the proof of Lemma 5,7 ) we have that any prime ring satisfying a polynomial identity over its centroid satisfies the ascending chain condition on left and right annihilators. Thus, by our Theorem 5.1, any nil subring of such a ring is nilpotent. We summarize this in

LEMMA 5.4. Let $R$ be a prime ring satislying a polynomial identity over its centroid. Then any nil subring of R is nilpotent.

The above result can also be derived as a consequence of the characterization given by Posner of prime rings satisfying polynomial identities. We shall treat this theorem of Posner later.

Let R be any nil ring satisfying a polynomial identity with properly conditioned coefficients (e.g., R may be an algebra over a field satisfying a polynomial identity with coefficients in the field, $R$ may be any ring, the identity having as coefficients operators, some of which are invertible, $R / P$ may be supposed to satisfy some polynomial identity, depending on $P$, for each prime ideal $P$ of $R$, etc., etc.). A theorem of Kaplansky asserts that such a ring is locally nilpotent. We give a new, and very easy proof of this in the

COROLLARY 1. A nil ring satisfying a polynomial identity is locally nilpotent.

Proof. Let $S$ be a finitely generated subring of $R$ which we suppose is not nilpotent. Since $S^{k}$ is also finitely generated, by Zorn's lemma there exists an ideal $P$ of $R$ maximal with respect to not containing any $S^{k}$. However, $P$ is a prime ideal of $R$ for if $A B C P$ with $A, B$ ideals properly larger than $P$ then $A \supset s^{k_{1}}, B \supset s^{k_{2}}$ whence $P \supset A B \supset s^{k_{1}+k_{2}}$, a contradiction. However, $\bar{R}=R / P$ is a prime ring satisfying a polynomial identity; since, in addition, it is nil, by the lemma it is nilpotent. This is inconsistent with $\overline{\mathrm{R}}$ being prime. Thus no such $P$ exists and we conclude that $S^{k}=(0)$ for some $k$.

THEOREM 5.2. Let $R$ be a nil Goldie ring; then $R$ is nilpotent.

Proof. Let $A=\{x \in R \mid R x=(0)\}$ and let $T$ be the torsion part of $A$. Of course, $T$ is a two-sided ideal of $R$. We can write $T$ as a direct sum of its primary components, $T_{p}$, where if $t \& T_{p}$ then $p^{m(t)} t=0$ where $p$ is a prime.

Let $V_{0}=(0)$ and $V_{1}=\left\{x \in T_{p} \mid p x=0\right\} . \quad V_{1}$ is an ideal of $R$ and is a vector space over the field $P$ of $p$ elements. Since there are no infinite direct sums of left ideals in $R$ and since every subgroup of $V_{1}$, which is an algebra over $P$, is a left ideal of $R$ we must conclude that $V_{1}$ is finite-dimensional over $P$. Let $V_{2}=\left\{x \in T_{p} \mid p^{2} x=0\right\}$, and let $W_{2}=V_{2} / V_{1}$. If $u_{1}, \ldots, u_{k} \ldots$ are in $V_{2}$ then $p u_{1}, \ldots, p u_{k} \ldots$ are in $V_{1}$ which is finite-dimensional over $P$, hence $\Sigma a_{i} p u_{i}=0$ for some nonzero $a_{i}$ in $P$; hence $p \sum a_{i} u_{i}=0$ and so $\sum a_{i} u_{i} \in V_{1}$. But then $W_{2}$ is finite-dimensional over $P$. Similarly, if $V_{i}=\left(x \in T_{p} \mid p^{i} x=0\right)$ then $W_{i}=V_{i} / V_{i-1}$ is a finite-dimensional vector space over $P$.

Each $V_{i}$ is a right ideal of $R_{\text {, hence }} V_{i} / V_{i-1}$ is an $R$-module which is a finite-dimensional vector space over $P$.

Since $R$ induces a nil finite ring of linear transformations on these, this ring of linear transformations must be nilpotent. Therefore $V_{i} R^{n_{i}} \subset V_{i-1}$ for some integer $n_{i}$. From this we get that $V_{i} R^{m_{i}}=(0)$ where $m_{i}=n_{1}+n_{2}+\ldots+n_{i}$.

However, by the ascending chain condition on left annihilators there exists an integer $k_{0}$ such that if $x R^{m}=\langle 0\rangle$ then $x R^{k_{0}}=(0)$. Given any element a in $T_{p}$ it is in some $V_{i}$ hence $a R^{m_{i}}=(0)$ and so $a R^{k_{0}}=(0)$. In other words, $T_{P} R^{k_{0}}=(0)$.

Since $T$ is a direct sum of the $T_{p}{ }^{\prime} s$ we get that $T R^{k_{0}}=(0)$.
Consider $\bar{R}=R / T$ and let $\bar{A}=A / T ; \bar{A}$ is torsion free. Let $V=\bar{A} \otimes_{Z} Q$ where $Z$ is the ring of integers and $Q$ is the field of rational numbers. If $\bar{u}_{1} \ldots ., \bar{u}_{n}$ in $\bar{A}$ are linearly independent (as elements in $\bar{V}$ ) over $Q$ they are a priori linearly independent over $Z$, hence their inverse images $u_{1}, \ldots, u_{n}$ in $A$ are linearly independent over $Z$. Since these then generate a direct sum of left ideals of R (since any subgroup of $A$ is a left ideal of $R$ ) we get, from our hypothesis, that $\bar{V}$ is finite-dimensional over $Q$. Thus $\bar{R}$ induces a ring of linear transformations on $\bar{V}$ by $(\bar{a} \times q) t \overline{\bar{T}}=\bar{a} \bar{r} \times q$. The hornomorphism $\bar{r} \rightarrow t$ has kernel exactly $r(\bar{A})$, thus $\bar{r} / r(\bar{A})$ is a nil ring of $n \times n$ matrices over the rationals, where $n=d i n_{Q} \bar{V}$. By invoking our Theorem 5.1, $\overline{\mathrm{R}} / \mathrm{r}(\overline{\mathrm{A}})$ is nilpotent, hence $(\overline{\mathrm{R}} / \mathrm{r}(\overline{\mathrm{A}}))^{\mathrm{m}}=(0)$, so $\overline{\mathrm{R}}^{\mathrm{m}} \mathrm{Cr}(\overline{\mathrm{A}})$ and so $\overline{\mathrm{A}}^{\mathrm{m}}=(0)$, whence $A R^{m} \subset T$. Since $T R^{k_{0}}=(0)$ we get $A R^{m^{\prime}} R^{k_{0}}=(0)$ and so $A R^{m+k_{0}}=(0)$. By our choice of $k_{0}$ it results that $A R^{k_{0}}=(0)$.

Let $\mathrm{U}=\left\{\mathrm{x}<\mathrm{R} \mid \times \mathrm{R}^{\mathrm{k}_{\mathrm{o}}}=(0)\right\}$. In $\mathrm{R}^{\mathrm{t}}=\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{U}$ we know, by Lemma 5.3 that the ascending chain condition on left armihilators holds. If $\mathrm{R}^{\mathrm{f}} \neq(0)$, since it is nil, we would have by Lemma 5.2
that there is an ideal $\mathrm{B}^{\prime} \neq(0)$ so that $\mathrm{R}^{\prime} \mathrm{B}^{\prime}=(0)$. Thus if B denotes the inverse image in $R$ of $B^{\prime}, R B \subset U$, whence $R B R^{k_{0}}=(0)$. However, this places $B R^{k_{0}}$ in $A$, and the discussion above allows us to conclude that $\left(B R^{k_{o}}\right) R^{k_{o}}=(0)$. Since $B R^{2 k_{0}}=(0)$, by the choice of $k_{0}$ we are lod to $B R^{k_{0}}=(0)$ and so $B \subset U$ and $B^{\prime}=(0)$. This last conclusion is in variance with $B^{\prime} \neq(0)$, forcing us to conclude that $R^{\prime}=(0)$, that is, $R=U$. From the definition of $U$ we obtain $R^{k_{0}+1}=(0)$ and so $R$ is nilpotent. This proves the theorem.

Lanski has generalized Theorem 5.2 to the following: any nil subring of a Goldie ring is nilpotent. His method of proof is to show that the example of Sasiada cited right after Theorem 5.1' can not be imbedded in a Goldie ring. Using this, given a nil but not nilpotent subring of a Goldie ring, he constructs in this subring a sequence of elements that behave like those in Sasiada's example. This enables Lanski to arrive at his result.

We prove another theorem in the vein of nil implies nilpotent. In this theorem we suppose that R is an algebra over an integral domain $\Omega$, that is, $\alpha x=0, \alpha \in \Omega, x \in R$ implies that $\alpha=0$ or $\mathrm{x}=0$ and $\alpha(\mathrm{xy})=(\alpha \mathrm{x}) \mathrm{y}=\mathrm{x}(\alpha \mathrm{y})$.

THEOREM 5.3. Let $R$ be a nil ring satisfying the ascending chain condition on left annihilators. If R satisfies a polynomial identity (with coefficients in $\Omega$ ) then R must be nilpotent.

Proof. By the ascending chain condition on left annihilators there is an integer $k$ such that $x R^{t}=(0)$ then $x R^{k}=(0)$. Let $U=\left\{x, R \mid \times R^{k}=(0)\right\}$ and consider $\bar{R}=R / U$. If $\bar{x} \bar{R}=(0)$ then $x R \subset U$ so $x R^{k+1}=(0)$ resulting in $x R^{k}=(0), x \in U$ and so $\bar{x}=0$. If $U=R$ then $R$ is nilpotent; if $U \neq R$ since $\Omega U$ clearly is in $U, \bar{R}$ is a nil ring in which $\bar{x} \bar{R} \neq(0)$ for $\bar{x} \neq 0$ and satisfies the same identity as $R$. All in all we have shown that if $R$
is not nilpotent we may assume that $x R=(0)$ only if $x=0$. We proceed to show that this situation is impossible.

As is well known (and easy) we can suppose that the identity satisfied by $R$ is of the form

$$
p\left(x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n}\right)=\sum_{\sigma \in S_{n}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(1)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n)}
$$

where $\alpha_{\sigma} \in \Omega, S_{n}$ is the symmetric group of degree $n$.
Let $A=\{x, R \mid R x=(0)\}$; by Lemma 5.2, $A \neq(0)$. If $x_{1}=a \in A, p\left(a, x_{2}, \ldots, x_{n}\right)=0$ gives us, since $R a=(0)$, that

$$
a \sum_{\sigma \in S_{n-1}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(2)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n)}=0
$$

for $x_{i} \in R$, that is

$$
A\left(\sum_{\sigma \in S_{n-1}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(2)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n)}\right)=(0),
$$

Since $\Omega r(A) C_{r}(A), \bar{R}=R / r(A)$ satisfies the identity

$$
q\left(x_{2}, \ldots, x_{n}\right)=\sum_{\sigma \in S_{n-1}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(2)} \ldots x_{\sigma(n)}
$$

of degree $n-1$. By induction, since $\bar{R}$ is nil, satisfies the ascending chain condition on left annihilators (Lemma 5.3) and a polynomial identity of degree $n-1$ or less we conclude that $\bar{R}$ is nilpotent, say $\bar{R}^{m}=(0)$. Therefore $R^{m} \subset r(A)$ leading to $A R^{m}=(0)$. Since, as we have seen above, $l(R)=(0)$ we get the contradiction $A=(0)$. The theorem is now proved,

Let us recall something done in (N) about identities. The standard identity of degree $k$ is defined by

$$
\left[x_{1}, \ldots, x_{k}\right]=\Sigma(-1)^{\sigma} x_{\sigma(1)}, \ldots x_{\sigma(k)}
$$

where $(-1)^{\sigma}$ is the signature of the permutation $\sigma$ in $S_{k}$ and the sum runs over all $\sigma \in \mathrm{S}_{\mathrm{k}}$. If an algebra. A is of dimension
$k$ over a field $F$ then it satisfies the standard identity $\left[x_{1}, \ldots, x_{k+1}\right]$. Thus the $n \times n$ matrices over a field satisfies $\left[x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n^{2}+1}\right]$. It is a fact, first proved by Amitsur and Levitski that they indeed satisfy $\left[\mathrm{x}_{1}, \ldots, \mathrm{x}_{2 \mathrm{n}}\right]$ and that this is the minimal identity. However we shall not need so sharp a result here. For a field $F$ let $r(n)$ be the degree of the minimal identity satisfied by $F_{n}$. Following a very easy argument by Kaplansky we show

LEMMA 5.5. $\mathrm{r}(\mathrm{n}) \geq \mathrm{r}(\mathrm{n}-1)+2$.
Proof. We imbed $F_{n-1}$ in $F_{n}$ as $\left(\begin{array}{cc}F_{n-1} & 0 \\ 0 & 0\end{array}\right)$, Let $t=r(n-1)-1$; hence we can find $a_{1} \ldots, a_{t} \in F_{n-1}$ such that $\left[\mathrm{a}_{1}, \ldots, \mathrm{a}_{\mathrm{t}}\right] \neq 0$. As imbedded in $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{n}}$ we see immediately that $\left[a_{1}, \ldots, a_{t^{\prime}}, e_{k n}, e_{n n}\right]=\left[a_{1}, \ldots, a_{t}\right] e_{k n} e_{n n}=\left[a_{1}, \ldots, a_{t}\right] e_{k n} \neq 0$ for some $k$ (since $\left[a_{1}, \ldots, a_{t}\right]=\left(\begin{array}{ll}A & 0 \\ 0 & 0\end{array}\right)$ where $A \neq 0$ ). Thus indeed $r(n)>t+2=r(n-1)+1$.

From this we clearly have that $\mathrm{r}(\mathrm{n}) \geq 2 \mathrm{n}$. We proceed to a result about finite-dimensional simple algebras,

THEOREM 5.4, Let $R$ be a simple algebra finitedimensional over its center $F$; let $A \subset R$ be a subalgebra over $F$ such that any polynomial identity satisfied by $A$ over the prime field ${ }^{P}$ of $F$ is satisfied by $R$. Then $A=R$.

Proof. Let $\bar{F}$ be the algebraic closure of $F$; the hypothesis carries over to $\mathrm{R} \otimes_{\mathrm{F}} \overline{\mathrm{F}}$ and $\mathrm{A} \otimes_{\mathrm{F}} \overline{\mathrm{F}}$. Hence to prove the theorem we may assume, without loss of generality, that $F$ is algebraically closed. Thus by Wedderburn's theorem $\mathrm{R}=\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{n}}$.

If $A$ is semi-simple then by Wedderburn's theorem $A \approx F_{n_{1}} \oplus \ldots \oplus F_{n_{k}}$ hence $A$ satisfies all the identities satis-
fied over $P$ by $F_{n_{t}}$ where $n_{t}=\max \left(n_{1}, \ldots, n_{k}\right)$ thus by assumption $F_{n}$ satisfies all the polynomial identities over $P$ satisfied by $F_{n_{t}}$. By Lemma 5.10 we deduce that $n_{t}=n$ and hence $A=F_{n}$ the desired conclusion.

Assume then that $A$ is not semi-simple and that its radical $N$ is not ( 0 ). Now $A / N \approx F_{n_{1}} \oplus \ldots \oplus F_{n_{k}}$ where each $n_{i}<n$ since $\operatorname{dim} A / N<\operatorname{dim} A \leq \operatorname{dim} F_{n}$. If $m=\max \left(n_{1}, \ldots, n_{k}\right)$ then $A / N$ satisfies the identities of $F_{m}$ hence $\left[x_{1}, \ldots, x_{r}\right]$ where $r=r(m)$, that is to say, $\left[a_{1} \ldots . a_{r}\right] \in N$ forall $a_{i}$ \& A. Now $N$ is nilpotent, say $N^{t}=(0)$, and is an ideal of $A$, hence satis fies the identity over $P_{1}\left(\left[x_{1}, \ldots, x_{r}\right] y\right)^{t}$, By hypothesis, $F_{n}$ must satisfy this identity and since $F_{n}$ is simple and has no nilpotent ideals we get $F_{n}$ must satisfy $\left[x_{1}, \ldots, x_{r}\right] y$ and so, $\left[x_{1}, \ldots, x_{r}\right]$. Since $r=r(m)$ and $m=\max n_{i}<n$ we get a contradiction. This proves the theorem.

We now turn to a slightly different direction; we intend to introduce the idea of an ultra-product. While this has been used ${ }^{a}$ great deal in logic it has not, until quite recently in the work of Amitsur, been used in ring theory. Much of the material to follow is treated along lines laid out by Amitsur.

Let $\mathrm{A}=\{\alpha\}$ be a set; a set $\mathcal{Z}=\{\mathrm{S}\}$ of subsets of A is a filter if:

1. $\phi$, the empty set, is not in
2. $S_{1}, S_{2} \in \mathcal{F}$ implies $S_{1} \cap S_{2} \in \mathcal{F}$
3. $S \in \mathcal{F}$ and $T \supset S$ then $T \in \mathcal{F}$

A filter is said to be an ultra-filter if it is maximal in the natural ordering of filters: $\mathcal{G}_{1} \leq \mathcal{F}_{2}$ if $s \in \mathcal{F}_{1}$ implies $s \in \mathcal{F}_{2}$. By Zorn's lemma it is clear that any filter can be enlarged to an ultra-filter.

Ultra-filters have the very simple characterization given by
LEMMA 5.6. A filter $f$ on $A$ is an ultra-filter if and only if for any subset $T$ of $A$ either $T \in \mathcal{F}$ or the complement of $T$, $A-T \in \mathcal{J}$.

Proof. If $\mathcal{F}$ is a filter and T is a subset of $\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{T} / \mathcal{F}$ then only one of the following can hold: $\mathrm{T} \cap \mathrm{S}=0$ for some $\mathrm{S}, \mathcal{F}$ or $(A-T) \cap S^{t}=\varphi$ for some $S^{t} \in \mathcal{F}$. Otherwise
$\phi \neq S \cap S^{t}=A \cap S \cap S^{t}=((A-T) \cup T) \cap S \cap S^{t} \subset\left((A-T) \cap S^{t}\right) U(T \cap S)=\epsilon$.

If $\mathcal{F}$ is an ultra-filter and there exists $S^{\prime} \in \mathcal{F}$ with $(A-T) \cap S^{x}=\phi$ then $T \cap S \neq \phi$ for all $S \in \mathcal{F}$. Thus $\{T, \mathcal{F}\}$ forms a base of a filter which, by the maximality of $\mathcal{f}$ forces $T, \mathcal{I}$. By the same argument, if $(A-T) \cap S \neq \phi$ for all $S \in \mathcal{I}$, $A-T \& \mathcal{F}$. This proves the result in one direction.

If, on the other hand, $\mathscr{G}$ is a filter with the property that for any subset $T$ of $A$ either $T \in \mathcal{F}$ or $A-T<\mathcal{F}$ then we claim that $\mathcal{F}$ is an ultra-filter. Suppose $\mathcal{F}^{\prime}$ is a properly larger filter and $T \in \mathcal{F}^{\prime}, T \nmid \mathcal{F}$. Thus $A-T \in \mathcal{F}$ so $A-T \in \mathcal{F}^{\prime}$, whence $\phi=T \cap(A-T)<\mathcal{F}^{\prime}$, a contradiction.

Let $\mathrm{R}_{\alpha}$ be a set of rings indexed by $\alpha \in \mathrm{A}$, and let $\prod_{\alpha \in \mathrm{A}} \mathrm{R}_{\alpha}$ be the complete product of the $\mathrm{R}_{\alpha}$ 's. As usual we can consider this ring as the set of functions with $f(\alpha)$ \& $R_{\alpha}$ under pointwise addition and multiplication.

Let $\$$ be a filter on $\mathrm{A}_{\text {; }}$ we define $\mathrm{f}, \mathrm{g} \in \Pi \mathrm{R}_{\alpha}$ to be equivalent if $\{\alpha \in A \mid f(\alpha)=g(\alpha)\}$ is in $\mathcal{G}$. This is indeed an equivalence relation $\sim$. Let $I=\{f \mid f \sim 0\}$; $I$ is an ideal of $\prod R_{\alpha}$. We define $\prod \mathrm{R}_{\alpha} / \mathcal{F}$ to be $\prod \mathrm{R}_{\alpha} / \mathrm{I}$. We call $\prod \mathrm{R}_{\alpha} / \mathcal{F}$ an ultra-product if $\mathcal{F}$ is an ultra-filter.

A basic property of ultra-products is given in
THEOREM 5.5, Let $\mathrm{R}_{\alpha}$ be a primitive ring with irreducible module $\mathrm{V}_{\alpha^{\prime}}$ commuting ring $\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{R}_{\alpha}}\left(\mathrm{V}_{\alpha^{\prime}}, \mathrm{V}_{\alpha}\right)$ and let $\mathrm{C}_{\alpha}$ be the center of $\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}$. For any ultra-filter $\mathscr{F}^{\alpha}$ on $\{\alpha\}, \mathrm{R}=\prod \mathrm{R}_{\alpha} / \mathcal{F}$ is a primitive ring with irreducible module $\mathrm{V}=\prod \mathrm{V}_{\alpha} / \mathrm{J}$ and commuting ring $\mathrm{D}=\prod \mathrm{D}_{\alpha} / \mathcal{F}$ with center $\mathrm{C}=\prod \mathrm{C}_{\alpha} / \mathcal{F}$.

Proof. We prove the large part of the theorem, leaving it to the reader to show that the commuting ring is indeed as described. In what is to follow we shall not need the result about D or C .

Let $\overline{\mathrm{R}}=\prod \mathrm{R}_{\alpha}$, the complete product of the $\mathrm{R}_{\alpha}$. For $r<\overline{\mathrm{R}}=\prod \mathrm{R}_{\alpha}$ we define an action on $\overline{\mathrm{V}}=\prod \mathrm{V}_{\alpha}$ by $(\mathrm{vr}) \alpha=\mathrm{v}(\alpha) \mathrm{r}(\alpha)$. If $r \equiv 0(\mathcal{I})$ and $v \in \bar{V},\{\alpha \mid(v r)(\alpha)=0\}$ contains $\{\alpha \mid r(\alpha)=0\}$ which is in $\mathcal{F}$ so $\mathrm{vr} \equiv 0(\mathcal{F})$; similarly if $\mathrm{v} \equiv 0(\mathcal{F})$ then $\mathrm{vr} \equiv 0$ (F) for all $\mathrm{r} \in \overline{\mathrm{R}}$. Thus we get $\mathrm{R}=\prod \mathrm{R}_{\alpha} / \mathcal{F}$ to act on $\mathrm{V}=\prod \mathrm{V}_{\alpha} \mathcal{F}$.

We claim that $V$ is an irreducible $R$-module; for let $\mathrm{V} \equiv 0$ (f) and let $\mathrm{u} \in \mathrm{V}$. Let $\mathrm{T}_{0}=\{\alpha \mid \mathrm{v}(\alpha) \neq 0\}$; since $\mathrm{v} \neq 0(\mathcal{f})$, $\{\alpha \mid v(\alpha)=0\} / \mathcal{F}$ hence $T_{0}=A-\{\alpha \mid v(\alpha)=0\}$ is in $\mathcal{F}$. For $\alpha \in T_{0}$ pick $r_{\alpha} \in R_{\alpha}$ so that $v_{\alpha} r_{\alpha}=u_{\alpha}(=u(\alpha))$; we can do this since $R_{\alpha}$ acts primitively on $\mathrm{V}_{\alpha}$ and $\mathrm{v}_{\alpha} \neq 0$. Therefore $\mathrm{vr}=\mathrm{u}$ (F) whence $\mathrm{vR}=\mathrm{V}$ confirming the irreducibility of V . Moreover R acts faithfully on $V$ for if $v r=0$ for all $V \in V$ and $r \in R$ if $r_{1} \in \bar{R}$ is a representative for $r$ then $\left.\{\alpha\}\left(v r_{1}\right)(\alpha)=0\right\}$ is in $\mathcal{J}$ and is contained in $\left\{\alpha \mid r_{1}(\alpha)=0\right\}$. Hence $\left\{\alpha \mid r_{1}(\alpha)=0\right\}$ is in $\mathcal{F}$ leading to $r_{1} \equiv 0$ (f) that is, $r=0$.

We now consider prime rings satisfying a polynomial identity.
LEMMA 5.7. If $R$ is a prime ring satisfying a polynomial identity over its centroid then $R$ is a left Goldie ring and a right Goldie ring.

Proof. We may assume that R satisfies a multilinear identity

$$
p\left(x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n}\right)=\sum_{\sigma \in S_{n}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(1)} \ldots x_{\sigma(n)}
$$

where the $\alpha_{\sigma}$ are in the centroid of $R$ and $\sigma$ runs over the symmetric group $\mathrm{S}_{\mathrm{n}}$.

We claim that in R no direct sum of left ideals can have length $n$, for if $I_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus I_{n}$ is such a direct sum pick $x_{1} \in I_{i}$. From $0=p\left(x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n}\right)=\Sigma \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(1)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n)}$ and from the directness of the sum we get

$$
\left\langle\sum_{\sigma \in S_{n-1}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(1)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n-1)}\right)_{n}=(0) .
$$

Since R is prime this forces

$$
\sum_{\sigma \in S_{n-1}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(1)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n-1)}=0
$$

for $x_{1}, I_{1}, x_{2}, I_{2}, \ldots, x_{n-1}, I_{n-1}$. Repeating the argument we eventually get $I_{1}=(0)$, a contradiction. Similarly we get no infinite direct sums of right ideals.

We now turn to the ascending chain condition on left annihilators. Let $I_{1} \subset I_{2} \subset \ldots C I_{n}$ be a properly ascending chain of left annihilators where $I_{i}=f\left(K_{i}\right), I_{i} \neq I_{i+1}$

By as sumption $I_{i} K_{i-1} \neq(0), K_{1} \supset K_{2} \supset \ldots \supset K_{n}$. In $p\left(x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n}\right)$, put $x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n-1} \in I_{n}, x_{n} \in K_{n}$; using

$$
p\left(x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n}\right)=\sum \alpha_{n} x_{\sigma(1)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n)}
$$

we get

$$
K_{n} \sum_{\sigma \in S_{n-1}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(1)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n-1)}=0
$$

for $x_{i}<I_{n}$. Now (0) $\neq K_{n-1} I_{n-1} C I_{n-1} \cap K_{n-1}$; pick $x_{n-1} \in x_{n-1} \in K_{n-1} I_{n-1}$ and $x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n-2} \in I_{n-1}$. We get

$$
K_{n-1} I_{n-1} \sum_{\sigma \in S_{n-2}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(1)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n-2)}=(0)
$$

so that by the primeness of R ,

$$
I_{n-1} \sum_{\sigma<S_{n-2}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma 1)} \ldots x_{\sigma(n-2)}=(0)
$$

for $x_{1} \ldots \ldots x_{n-2} \in I_{n-1}$. In particular, if they are in $I_{n-2}$ except for $x_{n-2}<K_{n-2} I_{n-2}$ we get

$$
I_{n-1} K_{n-2} I_{n-2} \sum_{\sigma \in S_{n-3}} \alpha_{\sigma} x_{\sigma(1)} \cdots x_{\sigma(n-3)}=(0) .
$$

Continuing we get $I_{n-1} K_{n-2} I_{n-2} K_{n-3} \cdots=(0)$. Since $I_{i} K_{i-1} \neq(0)$ we get a contradiction.

We can argue similarly for right annihilators. This proves the lemma.

We now have all the required pieces to prove an interesting theorem due to Posner.

THEOREM 5.6. Suppose that $R$ is a prime ring satisfying a polynomial identity over its centroid. Then R can be imbedded as an order in $D_{n}$ where $D$ is a division algebra finite-dimensional over its center. Otherwise put, the ring of quotients of R exists and satisfies the same identity as does R .

Proof. Since $R$ is a prime ring it has no non-zero nilpotent ideals. In conjunction with the fact that it is a left and right Goldie ring (Lemma 5.7) and Theorem 5.1 it has no non-zero nil ideals. Thus the polynomial ring $\mathrm{R}[\mathrm{t}]$, by Theorem 1.18 of (N), is semi-simple and satisfies the identity of $R$ (if we assume, as we may, that this identity is multilinear). In other words, we may assume that $R$ is semi-simple. As such, by Theorem 2.39 of ( N ), R satisfies some standard identity $\left[\mathrm{x}_{1} \ldots \ldots \mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{r}}\right]$. As a
semi-simple ring R is a subdirect sum of primitive ring $\mathrm{R}_{\alpha}$, $\alpha$ in some index set $Q$, each of which is a homomorphic image of $R$. As a homomorphic image of $R$ each $R_{\alpha}$ satisfies the same standard identity as does R so is a simple ring finitedimensional over its center.

Given $r \neq 0 \in \mathrm{R}$ let $\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{r}}=\{\alpha \in Q \mid \mathrm{r}(\alpha) \neq 0\}$. Since R is a prime ring, given $\mathrm{a} \neq 0, \mathrm{~b} \neq 0$ in R there is an $\mathrm{x} \in \mathrm{R}$ such that $\operatorname{axb} \neq 0$; but then $\mathrm{T}_{\operatorname{axb}} \neq 0 \subset \mathrm{~T}_{\mathrm{a}} \cap \mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{b}}$. Therefore we can enlarge $\left\{\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{r}} \mid r \neq 0 \in \mathrm{R}\right\}$ to a filter and so to an ultra-ilter 7 on $Q$. From our construction we have that $R$ is isomorphically imbedded in $\mathrm{S}=\prod_{\alpha \epsilon} \mathrm{R}_{\alpha} / \mathcal{F}$. By Theorem 5.5, S is a primitive ring and we already know it satisfies $\left[x_{1}, \ldots, x_{r}\right]$ since each $R_{\alpha}$ does, hence $S$ is a simple algebra finite-dimensional over its center $Z$. Consider RZCS.

Any identity over the prime field $P$ of $Z$ satisfied by RZ is satisfied by $R$ (if $P=G F(p), R P C R$; if $P$ is the rational field by clearing of denominators we get an identity with integer coefficients which then holds in $R$ ) hence in each $R_{\alpha}$ and so in $\mathrm{S}=\prod \mathrm{R}_{\alpha} / \mathcal{F}$. By Theorem 5.4, $\mathrm{RZ}=\mathrm{S}$.

Given $r \in R$ which is not a zero divisor in $R$, it is not a zero divisor in $R[Z]$. If it were, pick $w \neq 0 \in R[Z]$ such that $w = s_1 Z^{i_1} + \ldots + s_k Z^{i_k}$, $s_i \neq 0 \in R$, $i_j \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$, of shortest length $k$ such that $r w = 0$. Now $r(w r s_1 - s_1 r w) = 0$ for any $x \in R$; but $w r s_1 - s_1 r w = (s_2 r s_1 - s_1 r s_2) Z^{i_2} + \ldots + (s_k r s_1 - s_1 r s_k) Z^{i_k}$ is of shorter length than $w$ and annihilates $r$, hence $w r s_1 - s_1 r w = 0$, that is $w r s_1 = 0$ for all $x \in R$. Hence $w R[Z] r s_1 = (0)$; since $R[Z]$ is simple and $w \neq 0$, we conclude that $r s_1 = 0$, contrary to the assumption that $r$ is not a zero divisor in $R$. Now, as a non-zero divisor in a finite-dimensional algebra, $r$ must be invertible in $R[Z]$. We have shown that all regular elements in $R$ are invertible in $R[Z]$.

By Lemma 5.7, $R$ is a prime Goldie ring so has a ring of quotients $Q(R) = M_n(D)$, $D$ a division ring. We can embed $Q(R)$ in $R[Z]$, for every $x \in Q(R)$ is of the form $x = a^{-1} b$, $a, b \in R$, $a$ regular; map it to $a^{-1} b$ in $R[Z]$, where $a^{-1}$ is the inverse of $a$ in $R[Z]$. Thus $Q(R)$ satisfies the identities over $P$ that $R$ does, hence $D$ also does. By Theorem 2.38, $D$ is finite-dimensional over its center. The theorem is now completely proved.

## Bibliography

1. I.N. Herstein and Lance Small, "Nil rings satisfying certain chain conditions", Canadian Journal, 16 (1964), pp. 771-776.
2. C. Lanski, "Nil subrings of Goldie rings", (to appear).
3. E. Posner, "Prime rings satisfying a polynomial identity", Proc. Amer. Math. Soc., 11 (1960), pp. 180-184.
4. Lance Small, Thesis, University of Chicago, 1965.

## CHAPTER 6

## DECOMPOSITION IN NOETHERIAN RINGS${ }^{1}$

The topic treated in this chapter is an attempt to simulate in a non-commutative Noetherian ring the Lasker-Noether primary decomposition in a commutative Noetherian ring. For a large part, the results we shall give are due to Lesieur and Croisot.

A decomposition is given for an arbitrary left ideal as an intersection of special left ideals — the so-called tertiary ones — and one could hope that this would be a suitable vehicle for a profound study of non-commutative Noetherian rings. Unfortunately, at least as yet, this has not been the case; we know of no effective application of these ideas to obtain the central, deep and not-yet-established theorems that would reveal the make up of a general Noetherian ring.

Let us consider what might be considered a down-to-earth question, whose answer would generalize a known theorem of Levitzki: Let $R$ be a left Noetherian ring and $\lambda, \mu$ left ideals of $R$; suppose that for all $a \in \lambda, a^{n(a)} \in \mu$ for a suitable integer $n(a)$; is it then true that $\lambda^{k} \subset \mu$ for some integer $k$ ? This would seem to be a natural target for such a decomposition theory yet in this general setting the theory falls short of providing an answer. However, in all fairness, making use of some of its concepts if not the results themselves we have been able to establish the special case of the result in which $a^{n} \in \mu$ for all $a \in \lambda$, $n$ a fixed integer, and in the general case when $R$ satisfies a polynomial identity.

All rings $R$ to be considered will be left Noetherian, that is, satisfy the ascending chain condition on left ideals, and will have unit elements. All modules $M$ will be unital, finitely generated left $R$-modules.

**Definition.** An element $a \in R$ is an annihilating element for $M$ if $aN = (0)$ for some submodule $N \neq (0)$ of $M$. A left ideal $I$ of $R$ is an annihilating left ideal for $M$ if $I N = (0)$ for some submodule $N \neq (0)$ of $M$.

We denote by $O(M)$ the ideal $\{a \in R \mid a M=(0)\}$ of $R$.
The next few definitions make sense for any ring and all or parts of the very early lemmas hold in this more general context, but all these will be of most interest in the Noetherian case.

**Definition.** If $N$ is a submodule of $M$ then the primary radical of $N$, written $\operatorname{rad}(N)$, is the intersection of all prime ideals of $R$ which contain $O(M/N)$. $N$ is a primary submodule of $M$ if all elements that annihilate $M/N$ are in $\operatorname{rad}(N)$.

A remark which holds for any ring $R$; if $x \in \operatorname{rad}(N)$ then $x^{t} \in O(M/N)$ for some $t$ depending on $x$. This is true since the intersection of all prime ideals of a ring is a nil ideal.

One further definition at this point:
**Definition.** The tertiary radical of $N$, $N$ a submodule of $M$, written $t\text{-}\operatorname{rad}(N)$, is the set of all elements of $R$ that annihilate every non-trivial submodule of $M/N$.

One immediately sees that:
$t\text{-}\operatorname{rad}(N)=\{a \in R \mid$ for all $\alpha \notin N$ there exists $\beta \in M, \beta \notin N$ with $aR \beta \subset N\}$.

**LEMMA 6.1.** Let \(N\) be a submodule of \(M\). If \(a_{1},\dots,a_{n} \in t\text{-rad}(N)\), then, given \(\alpha \in M\) with \(\alpha \notin N\), there is a \(\beta \in R\alpha\) with \(\beta \notin N\) such that \(a_{i}R\beta \subset N\) for \(i=1,2,\dots,n\).

*Proof.* We proceed by induction on \(n\). If \(n=1\), this is merely the definition of \(t\text{-rad}(N)\). Suppose then that we have found a \(\gamma \in R\alpha\), \(\gamma \notin N\) such that \(a_{i}R\gamma \subset N\) for \(i=1,2,\dots,n-1\). Since \(a_{n} \in t\text{-rad}(N)\), there is a \(\beta \in R\gamma \subset R\alpha\) with \(\beta \notin N\) such that \(a_{n}R\beta \subset N\). However, for \(i=1,\dots,n-1\), we have \(a_{i}R\beta \subset a_{i}R\gamma \subset N\). Thus the lemma is proved. ∎

## **COROLLARY 1.** \(t\text{-rad}(N)\) is a two-sided ideal of \(R\).

*Proof.* From the very definition of \(t\text{-rad}(N)\) we immediately have that \(aR \subset t\text{-rad}(N)\) and \(Ra \subset t\text{-rad}(N)\) for all \(a \in t\text{-rad}(N)\). To finish we merely need that \(a, b \in t\text{-rad}(N)\) forces \(a-b \in t\text{-rad}(N)\); this is however clear from the lemma. ∎

COROLLARY 2. Let $I = t\text{-}\operatorname{rad}(N)$; given $\alpha \notin N$ there is a $\beta \in R\alpha$ such that $\beta \notin N$, $I \beta \subset N$.

Proof. Since $R$ is left Noetherian and $I$ is an ideal of $R$, $I = Ra_1 + \ldots + Ra_n$ for appropriate $a_i \in I$. Pick by the lemma $\beta \in R\alpha, \beta \notin N$ such that $a_i \beta \in N$ for $i=1, \ldots, n$; then $I \beta \subset N$.

COROLLARY 3. Let $I = t\text{-}\operatorname{rad}(N)$ and let $N' = \{\alpha \in M \mid I \alpha \subset N\}$. Then $N' \supset N$ and $N' \neq N$.

Proof. Since $I$ is a 2-sided ideal of $R$, $N'$ is a submodule of $M$. Clearly, $N \subset N'$. By Corollary 2, we can find $\beta \notin N$ such that $I \beta \subset N$; since $\beta \in N'$, $\beta \notin N$ this finishes the proof.

In fact, we have proved a good deal more about the nature of $N'$ for we know that in each $R\alpha, \alpha \notin N$ there are elements of $N'$. We formalize this in Lemma 6.2 but first a definition.

As usual, and as used before in these notes, $M$ is said to be an essential extension of $A$ modulo $B$, where $B \subset A$ and $A$ and $B$ are submodules of $M$, if whenever $C$ is a submodule of $M$ such that $C \cap A \subset B$ then $C \subset B$. That is, in $M / B$ the module $A / B$ meets all non-zero submodules non-trivially.

## Lemma 6.2
$M$ is an essential extension of $N^{\prime}$ modulo $N$.

**Proof.** This is immediate from Corollary 2 to Lemma 6.1.

Before proceeding, we examine the relation of $\operatorname{rad}(N)$ to $t\text{-rad}(N)$.

## Lemma 6.3
$\operatorname{rad}(N) \subset t\text{-rad}(N)$.

**Proof.** As we remarked earlier, for all $x \in \operatorname{rad}(N)$, $x^{n(x)} \in t\text{-rad}(N)$. Since $R$ is Noetherian, by a result of Levitzki (or using results of Chapter 5), $(\operatorname{rad}(N))^{k} \subset t\text{-rad}(N)$. From the definition of $t\text{-rad}(N)$ this implies that $\operatorname{rad}(N) \subset t\text{-rad}(N)$.

In the commutative case we can now easily establish equality for these two radicals.

## Theorem 6.1
If $R$ is a commutative Noetherian ring and if $N$ is a submodule of $M$ then $\operatorname{rad}(N) = t\text{-rad}(N)$.

**Proof.** Lemma 6.3 already tells us that $\operatorname{rad}(N) \subset t\text{-rad}(N)$. Now for the other direction.

Since \(R\) is Noetherian and \(M\) is a finitely generated unital \(R\)-module, \(M\) has the ascending chain condition on submodules. Let \(a \in t\text{-rad}(N)\) and let \(J_{n} = \{\alpha \in M \mid R^{n} \alpha \subset N\}\). Then \(J_{n}\) forms an ascending chain of submodules of \(M\); hence for some integer \(n\), \(J_{n} = J_{n+1}\). If \(a^{n} M \not\subset N\), we can find \(\alpha \in M\) with \(a^{n} \alpha \notin N\); since \(a \in t\text{-rad}(N)\) there is an \(r \in R\) with \(r a^{n} \alpha \in N\) but for which \(a R r a^{n} \alpha \subset N\). Since \(R\) is commutative this yields \(R a^{n+1} r \alpha \subset N\), that is, \(r \alpha \in J_{n+1} = J_{n}\), hence \(a^{n} r \alpha \in N\), contrary to \(r a^{n} \alpha \in N\).

Thus \(a \in t\text{-rad}(N)\) implies \(a^{k} M \subset N\) for some \(k\). Since \(\operatorname{rad}(N)\) is the intersection of prime submodules, this forces \(a \in \operatorname{rad}(N)\), that is, \(t\text{-rad}(N) \subset \operatorname{rad}(N)\). This proves the theorem.

**DEFINITION.** \(N\) is a tertiary submodule of \(M\) if the nonzero annihilating elements for \(M/N\) are all in \(t\text{-rad}(N)\).

A quick verification reveals that \(N\) is a tertiary submodule of \(M\) if and only if \(t\text{-rad}(N) = \{a \in R \mid \text{there is a } \beta \notin N \text{ with } a R \beta \subset N\}\).

**LEMMA 6.1.** Let \(N\) be a tertiary submodule of \(M\). Then \(P = t\text{-rad}(N)\) is a prime ideal of \(R\).

*Proof.* By Lemma 6.2, since \(N\) is tertiary, there exists a \(\beta \in M/N\) such that for all \(a \in P\), \(aR\beta \subset N\). Since \(N\) is tertiary, \(P\) consists of all elements \(a \in R\) such that \(aR\beta \subset N\).

Suppose \(x, y \in R\) are such that \(xRy \subset P\); then \(xRyR\beta \subset N\). If \(y \notin P\), then \(yR\beta \not\subset N\), so by the tertiary nature of \(N\) we get \(x \in P\). Therefore \(xRy \subset P\) implies that \(x \in P\) or \(y \in P\); hence \(P\) is a prime ideal of \(R\). ∎

If \(N\) is a tertiary submodule of \(M\) and the prime ideal \(P\) of \(R\) is \(t\text{-rad}(N)\), then we say that \(N\) is \(P\)-tertiary and that \(P\) is the prime ideal of \(N\).

A submodule \(N\) of \(M\) is called **irreducible** if it is not the intersection of two strictly larger submodules.

**LEMMA 6.5.** If \(N\) is an irreducible submodule of \(M\), then it is tertiary.

*Proof.* If \(N\) is not tertiary, there exist an \(a \notin t\text{-rad}(N)\) and an \(\alpha \notin N\) such that \(aR\alpha \subset N\). Since \(a \notin t\text{-rad}(N)\), there is a \(\gamma \notin N\) with \(aR\gamma \not\subset N\). Because \(aR\alpha \subset N\), we have \(\alpha \in (N :_R aR)\). Consider the submodules \(N_1 = N + R\alpha\) and \(N_2 = N + R\gamma\). Both properly contain \(N\). We claim \(N = N_1 \cap N_2\). If \(x \in N_1 \cap N_2\), then \(x = n_1 + r_1\alpha = n_2 + r_2\gamma\) for some \(n_1, n_2 \in N\) and \(r_1, r_2 \in R\). Then \(a x = a n_1 + a r_1\alpha \in N\) and also \(a x = a n_2 + a r_2\gamma\). Since \(a n_2 \in N\), we have \(a r_2\gamma \in N\). But \(aR\gamma \not\subset N\), so this forces \(r_2 \in (N :_R a\gamma) \subset t\text{-rad}(N)\). However, \(a \notin t\text{-rad}(N)\) and the definition of \(t\)-radical implies a contradiction unless \(r_2\gamma \in N\). Thus \(x = n_2 + r_2\gamma \in N\). Hence \(N = N_1 \cap N_2\), contradicting the irreducibility of \(N\). Therefore, \(N\) must be tertiary. ∎

Let $\mathrm{N}^{*}=(\mathrm{N}+\mathrm{R} \alpha) \cap(\mathrm{N}+\mathrm{R} \gamma)$; clearly $\mathrm{N}^{*} \supset \mathrm{N}$. For $\beta \in \mathrm{N}^{*}$, $\beta=\alpha_{1}+r \alpha=\alpha_{2}+s \gamma$ with $\alpha_{1}, \alpha_{2} \in \mathrm{N}$. Thus $s \gamma=\left(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}\right)+r \alpha$ so $s \gamma \in R \alpha \cap \mathrm{N}$. Since $\gamma^{\prime}=s \gamma \in R \gamma$ satisfies $R \gamma^{\prime} \subset \mathrm{N}$ we have that $\gamma^{\prime} \in \mathrm{N}$, hence $\beta \in \mathrm{N}$. Thus $\mathrm{N}^{*} \subset \mathrm{N}$. We have exhibited $\mathrm{N}$ as an intersection of larger submodules. This proves the lemma.

### DEFINITION
The decomposition $\mathrm{N}=\mathrm{N}_{1} \cap \ldots \cap \mathrm{N}_{\mathrm{r}}$ of $\mathrm{N}$ by the $\mathrm{N}_{\mathrm{i}}$ is irredundant if no $\mathrm{N}_{\mathrm{i}}$ can be omitted.

### LEMMA 6.6
If $\mathrm{N}=\mathrm{N}_{1} \cap \ldots \cap \mathrm{N}_{\mathrm{r}}$ is an irredundant decomposition of $\mathrm{N}$ by $P_{i}$-tertiary submodules $\mathrm{N}_{i}$, $i=1,2, \ldots, r$, then $\operatorname{t-rad}(\mathrm{N})=P_{1} \cap \ldots \cap P_{r}$.

Proof. Let $a \in P_{1} \cap \ldots \cap P_{r}$ and let $\alpha \in M$, $\alpha \notin N$. Since $\alpha \notin N$, $\alpha$ is not in some $N_{i}$, say $\alpha \notin N_{1}$. From the fact that $a \in t\text{-rad}(N_{1})$ there is an $\alpha_{1} \in R\alpha$, $\alpha_{1} \notin N_{1}$ such that $aR\alpha_{1} = \{a r \alpha_{1} : r \in R\} \subset N_{1}$. If $\alpha_{1} \notin N_{2}$ then $aR\alpha_{1} \subset N_{1} \cap N_{2}$; if $\alpha_{1} \in N_{2}$ there is an element $\alpha_{2} \in R\alpha_{1} \subset R\alpha$, $\alpha_{2} \notin N_{2}$ such that $aR\alpha_{2} \subset N_{2}$. Since $aR\alpha_{2} \subset aR\alpha_{1} \subset N_{1}$ we have $aR\alpha_{2} \subset N_{1} \cap N_{2}$. Continuing this way we get an element $\beta \in M$, $\beta \notin N$ such that $aR\beta \subset N_{1} \cap \ldots \cap N_{r}=N$. Thus by the definition of $t\text{-rad}(N)$, $a \in t\text{-rad}(N)$ hence $P_{1} \cap \ldots \cap P_{r} \subset t\text{-rad}(N)$.

Suppose now that $a \in t\text{-rad}(N)$ and that $\alpha \in N_{j}$ for all $j \neq i$, $\alpha \notin N_{i}$. Since $N$ is the irredundant intersection of the $N_{i}$, $\alpha \notin N$. Therefore there is a $\beta \in R\alpha$, $\beta \notin N$ such that $aR\beta \subset N \subset N_{j}$. Since $\beta \in N_{j}$ for $j \neq i$ and $\beta \notin N$ we must have that $\beta \notin N_{i}$. Since $N_{i}$ is $P_{i}$-tertiary we conclude that $a \in P_{i}$. But then $a \in \bigcap_{i=1}^{r} P_{i}$, whence $t\text{-rad}(N) \subset \bigcap_{i=1}^{r} P_{i}$.

LEMMA 6.7. If $N_{1}$ and $N_{2}$ are P-tertiary submodules of M then so is $N_{1} \cap N_{2}$.

Proof. Let $N = N_{1} \cap N_{2}$; by Lemma 6.5, $t\text{-rad}(N) = P$. To finish the proof we merely must show that $N$ is tertiary.

Let $a \in R$, $\beta \in M$, $\beta \notin N$ such that $aR\beta \subset N$. Since $\beta \notin N$, $\beta \notin N_{1}$ or $\beta \notin N_{2}$. If $\beta \notin N_{1}$, since $N_{1}$ is tertiary, $a \in t\text{-rad}(N_{1}) = P$; similarly, if $\beta \notin N_{2}$, $a \in P$. Thus $aR\beta \subset N$, $\beta \notin N$ implies that $a \in P = t\text{-rad}(N)$. Thus $N$ is tertiary.

## DEFINITION
A decomposition $\mathrm{N}=\mathrm{N}_{1} \cap \ldots \cap \mathrm{N}_{\mathrm{r}}$ of a submodule $\mathrm{N}$ by $\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{i}}$-tertiary submodules $\mathrm{N}_{\mathrm{i}}$ is called reduced if it is irredundant and the $\mathrm{P}_{i}$ are distinct.

We now can prove the analog of the primary decomposition for commutative Noetherian rings.

## THEOREM 6.2
Every submodule $\mathrm{N}$ of $\mathrm{M}$ has a reduced decomposition $\mathrm{N}=\mathrm{N}_{1} \cap \ldots \cap \mathrm{N}_{\mathrm{r}}$ where $\mathrm{N}_{\mathrm{i}}$ is $\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{i}}$-tertiary.

**Proof.** That every submodule has an intersection of a finite number of irreducible ones is easy, just as in the commutative case. Merely consider the set of submodules which have no such representation; if this set is non-empty it has a maximal element. However, this maximal element is therefore irreducible, giving a contradiction.

The rest follows from Lemmas 6.4, 6.5, 6.6, and 6.7.

We now want to establish the uniqueness of the associated primes. To this end we prove

## LEMMA 6.8
If $\mathrm{N}, \mathrm{U}, \mathrm{V}, \mathrm{U}', \mathrm{V}'$ are submodules of $\mathrm{M}$ such that $\mathrm{U}$ is $\mathrm{P}$-tertiary, $\mathrm{U}'$ is $\mathrm{P}'$-tertiary, $\mathrm{P} \neq \mathrm{P}'$ and $\mathrm{N}=\mathrm{U} \cap \mathrm{V} = \mathrm{U}' \cap \mathrm{V}'$ then $\mathrm{N}=\mathrm{V} \cap \mathrm{V}'$.

Proof. Let $\alpha \in V \cap V'$. Since $P \neq P'$, there is an element in one and not in the other; say $a \in P$, $a \notin P'$. If $\alpha \notin N$ there exists a $\beta \in R \alpha$, $\beta \notin N$ such that $aR\beta \subset N$. Thus $aR\beta \subset U' \cap V'$.

Since $\beta \notin N$, $\beta \notin U'$. Since $U'$ is $P'$-tertiary and $aR\beta \subset U'$ we conclude that $a \in P'$, a contradiction. Thus $\alpha \in N$. That is, $V \cap V' \subset N$; since clearly $N \subset V$, $N \subset V'$ we get $N \subset V \cap V'$. This proves the lemma.

## The lemma immediately yields

### THEOREM 6.3. 
If the submodule $N$ of $M$ has the two reduced decompositions

$$
N=N_{1} \cap \ldots \cap N_{r}=N_{1}^{\prime} \cap \ldots \cap N_{s}^{\prime}
$$

then $r=s$ and the set of prime ideals $P_{i}$ of the $N_{i}$ coincides with the set of prime ideals $P_{i}^{\prime}$ of the $N_{i}^{\prime}$.

Proof. We show that for some $i$, $P_i^1 = P_i$. Suppose not, i.e., $P_i^1 \neq P_i$ for all $i$. By Lemma 6.8 (which states that if two irredundant primary decompositions intersect to the same submodule, their associated primes can be matched up after removing components whose primes contain others), from $N = N_1 \cap \cdots \cap N_r = N_1^1 \cap \cdots \cap N_s^1$, we may reorder so that for each $j$, $P_j^1$ contains some $P_i$. Since we assume $P_i^1 \neq P_i$ for all $i$, in particular $P_1^1 \neq P_1$ and $P_1^1 \supset P_1$ (strictly). Because the decomposition $N = N_1 \cap \cdots \cap N_r$ is irredundant, $N \subsetneq N_2 \cap \cdots \cap N_r$. However, since $P_1^1 \supset P_1$, we have $N_1 \subset N_1^1$, so
\[
N = N_1 \cap (N_2 \cap \cdots \cap N_r) \subset N_1^1 \cap (N_2 \cap \cdots \cap N_r).
\]
But $N = N_1^1 \cap \cdots \cap N_s^1$, so
\[
N_1^1 \cap (N_2 \cap \cdots \cap N_r) \subset N_1^1 \cap (N_2^1 \cap \cdots \cap N_s^1) = N.
\]
Hence $N = N_1^1 \cap (N_2 \cap \cdots \cap N_r)$. Now, because $P_1^1 \neq P_2$ (otherwise $P_1^1 = P_2 \supset P_1$, contradicting distinctness in an irredundant decomposition unless $P_1 = P_2$, which is not the case), Lemma 6.8 implies we can omit $N_2$ if $P_1^1$ contains $P_2$. More precisely, since $P_1^1 \supset P_2$, we have $N_2 \subset N_1^1$, so
\[
N = N_1^1 \cap N_2 \cap (N_3 \cap \cdots \cap N_r) = N_1^1 \cap (N_3 \cap \cdots \cap N_r).
\]
Iterating, we eventually obtain $N = N_1^1 \cap N_{k+1} \cap \cdots \cap N_r$ for some $k$, and finally $N = N_1^1$, contradicting the irredundancy of $N = N_1^1 \cap \cdots \cap N_s^1$ (since $N_1^1$ alone would then equal $N$, making the intersection redundant). Therefore, our assumption is false, and $P_i^1 = P_i$ for some $i$.

By symmetry, for each $j$, $P_j^1 = P_k$ for some $k$. This shows $s \leq r$. Reversing the roles gives $r \leq s$, so $r = s$ and $\{P_i\} = \{P_j^1\}$.

The theory developed here does not have many of the desirable properties of the primary decomposition theory for commutative Noetherian rings. For instance, the tertiary radical of a left ideal I of R, considered as an R-module does not necessarily contain I. An even greater shortcoming is that one can find left Noetherian rings R and a module M with submodules $\mathrm{N}_{1} \subset \mathrm{N}_{2}$ for which $\operatorname{t\text{-}rad}\left(N_{1}\right) \not\subset \operatorname{t\text{-}rad}\left(N_{2}\right)$. In fact one can show that the t-radical of M is monotonic if and only if for all submodules N of M, $\operatorname{rad}(\mathrm{N})=\operatorname{t\text{-}rad}(\mathrm{N})$, and so if all tertiary modules are primary.

Emmy Noether gave a simple example of a situation where tertiary modules are not always primary. Let $Q$ be the field of rational numbers and let R be the algebra generated over $Q$ by $a, b, c$ where $a^{2}=a, b^{2}=b, c^{2}=a b=b a=c a=b c=0$ and $\mathrm{ac}=\mathrm{cb}=\mathrm{c}$. It is easy to verify that in the R-module R, the submodule (left ideal) Ra is tertiary but not primary.

We now examine when tertiary modules are primary.

**DEFINITION.** An R-module is called an Artin-Rees module if for each left ideal I of R and each submodule N of M and each integer $n \geq 0$ we can find an integer $h=h(n) \geq 0$ such that $I^{h} M \cap N \subset I^{n} N$.

LEMMA 6.9. A P-tertiary submodule N of M is primary if and only if $\mathrm{P}^{n}\mathrm{M} \subset \mathrm{N}$ for some n.

Proof. If N is primary then $\operatorname{rad}(\mathrm{N})=\mathrm{t}\text{-}\operatorname{rad}(\mathrm{N})=\mathrm{P}$ and so since $x \in \operatorname{rad}(N)$ implies $x^{k(x)} \in \operatorname{Ann}(M/N)$, by a theorem of Levitzki, $\mathrm{P}^{n} \subset \operatorname{Ann}(M/N)$ (see Lemma 6.3). Hence $\mathrm{P}^{n}\mathrm{M} \subset \mathrm{N}$.

On the other hand, if $\mathrm{P}^{n}\mathrm{M} \subset \mathrm{N}$ then $\mathrm{P}^{n} \subset \operatorname{rad}(\mathrm{N})$; since $\operatorname{rad}(\mathrm{N})$ is an intersection of prime ideals this puts $\mathrm{P} \subseteq \operatorname{rad}(\mathrm{N})$. Together with $\operatorname{rad}(N) \subset P$ we get the result.

THEOREM 6.4. An R-module M is an Artinian module if and only if every tertiary submodule of M is primary.

Proof. Suppose that M is an Artinian module and that N is a P-tertiary submodule of M. Let $\mathrm{N}^{\prime} = \{m \in \mathrm{M} \mid \mathrm{P}m \subset \mathrm{N}\}$, then there exists an integer $n$ so that $P^{n} M \cap N^{\prime} \subset P N^{\prime} \subset N$. Thus by Lemma 6.9, $\mathrm{P}^{n}\mathrm{M} \subset \mathrm{N}$; Lemma 6.9 then tells us that N is primary.

Conversely, suppose every tertiary submodule of M is primary. Let $\{N_i\}$ be a descending chain of submodules of M. By the tertiary decomposition theory, each $N_i$ has a minimal tertiary decomposition. Since each tertiary submodule in these decompositions is primary, it is finitely determined by its radical P and an integer n such that $P^n M \subset N$ (Lemma 6.9). This finite determinacy implies the decompositions, and hence the chain $\{N_i\}$ itself, must stabilize. Hence M is Artinian.

Suppose on the other hand, that all tertiary submodules of M are primary. Let N be a submodule of M and n an integer, and I a left ideal of $R$.
