Let $I^{n} N=N_{1} \cap \ldots \cap N_{r} \cap N_{1}' \cap \ldots \cap N_{s}'$ be a reduced tertiary decomposition of $I^{n} N$ where each $N_{i}$ is $P_{i}$-tertiary and each $N_{j}'$ is $P_{j}'$-tertiary and where $I^{n} \subset P_{i}$ for
$i=1, \ldots, r$ and $I^{n} \not\subset P_{j}'$ for $j=1,2, \ldots, s$. Since all tertiary modules are primary by assumption, there exists an integer $h$ such that $I^{h} M \subset N_{1} \cap \ldots \cap N_{r}$. Now $N \subset N_{1}' \cap \ldots \cap N_{s}'$ for if $\alpha \in N$, $a \in I^{n}$ then $a R \alpha \subset I^{n} N \subset N_{1}' \cap \ldots \cap N_{s}'$. If $\alpha \notin N_{1}' \cap \ldots \cap N_{s}'$ then $a \in P_{j}'$ for some $j$ since the $N_{j}'$ are tertiary. This would be a contradiction. Thus $I^{h} M \cap N \subset I^{n} N$.

**Corollary (Artin-Rees):** Every finitely generated module over a commutative Noetherian ring is an Artin-Rees module.

*Proof.* This follows from Theorems 6.1 and 6.4 (which are assumed to have been stated previously).

## Bibliography

1. I. N. Herstein, "A theorem on left Noetherian rings", J. Math. Anal. Appl. 15 (1966), pp. 91-96.
2. L. Lesieur and R. Croisot, "Sur la decomposition en ideaux primaires dans un anneau non nécessairement commutatif", Comptes Rendus Acad. Sci. Paris, 243 (1955), pp. 1988-1991.
3. L. Lesieur and R. Croisot, "Théorie Noetherienne des anneaux, des demi-groupes et des modules dans le cas non commutatif 1, II, III", in Colloque Algebre Superieure Bruxelles 1956, Math. Annalen 134 (1958), pp. 485-496, and Acad. Roy. Belg. Bull. Cl. Sci. (5), 44(1958), pp. 75-93 respectively.
4. J. A. Riley, "Axiomatic primary and tertiary decomposition theory", Trans. Amer. Math. Soc. 105 (1962), pp. 177-201.

# CHAPTER 7

## RECENT EXAMPLES AND COUNTER-EXAMPLES

In a remarkable paper published recently, Golod and Shafarevich proved an extremely powerful theorem -- although rather easy to prove -- which has provided a technique to tackle a wide range of problems. As an immediate consequence of their work one can construct a nil, but not locally nilpotent, algebra thereby providing a negative answer to the Kurosh Problem; one can construct a torsion group which is not locally finite, thereby settling in the negative the general Burnside Problem; one can construct an infinite class field tower, in fact, the field $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-3 \cdot 5 \cdot 7 \cdot 11 \cdot 13 \cdot 19})$ carries such a tower above it. It would seem that these are just the beginning of the applications of their theorem.

What is perhaps even more remarkable about what they have done is the beautiful simplicity of their approach. Part of this chapter is devoted to an exposition of a portion of their work; we shall develop it enough to settle the Kurosh and Burnside Problems. We follow the treatment used by Jon Alperin in the Algebra Seminar at the University of Chicago in April, 1964.

Let $F$ be any field and let $T=F\left[x_{1}, \ldots, x_{d}\right]$ be the polynomial ring over $F$ in the $d$ non-commuting variables $x_{1}, x_{2}, \ldots, x_{d}$. $T$ has a degree function and carries the structure of a graded algebra. $T$ can be written as

$$
T = T_{0} \oplus T_{1} \oplus \cdots \oplus T_{n} \oplus \cdots
$$

where $T_{0}=F$ and where $T_{n}$ has a basis of $d^{n}$ elements consisting of the monomials $x_{i_{1}} x_{i_{2}} \cdots x_{i_{n}}$ with each index $i_j \in \{1, \ldots, d\}$. The elements of $T_{n}$ are said to be homogeneous of degree $n$.

Let $I=\left(f_{1}, f_{2}, \ldots\right)$ be the ideal of $T$ generated by the homogeneous elements $f_{1}, f_{2}, \ldots$ of degrees $n_1, n_2, \ldots$ with $2 \leq n_{1} \leq n_{2} \leq \cdots$ respectively. Let $r_{i}$ be the number of $n_{j}$ which are equal to $i$ (so $r_i$ counts the generators $f_j$ of degree exactly $i$).

Now the algebra $A = T / O_t$ inherits the grading of $T$, since $O_t$ is homogeneously generated, in fact $A = A_{0} \oplus A_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus A_{n} \oplus \ldots$ where $A_{n} \approx T_{n} /\left(O_t \cap T_{n}\right)$. Let $b_{n} = \dim_{F}\left(A_{n}\right)$.

The Golod–Shafarevich theorem gives a sufficient condition that $A$ be infinite dimensional over $F$. This is the ultra-important

**THEOREM 7.1. (Golod–Shafarevich)** For $A$ as described above

1. $b_{n} \geq d b_{n-1}-\sum_{n_{i} \leq n} b_{n-n_{i}}$ for $n \geq 1$.
2. if $r \leq\left(\frac{d-t}{2}\right)^{2}$ then $A$ is infinite-dimensional over $F$.

**Proof.** We will exhibit linear mappings $\phi, \psi$ so that the sequence

$$
0 \rightarrow \bigoplus_{n_i \leq n} A_{n-n_i} \xrightarrow{\phi} A_{n-1}^{\oplus d} \xrightarrow{\psi} A_{n} \rightarrow 0
$$

is exact. Note that if we could do this then (1) would be proved, for then $d b_{n-1}=b_{n}+ \dim \operatorname{Ker} \psi$. Since $\operatorname{Ker} \psi$ is a homomorphic image of $\bigoplus_{n_i \leq n} A_{n-n_i}$, $\dim \operatorname{Ker} \psi \leq \sum_{n_{i} \leq n} b_{n-n_{i}}$, hence $d b_{n-1} \leq b_{n}+\sum_{n_{i} \leq n} b_{n-n_{i}}$, the required result.

Our task has been reduced to defining the $\varphi$ and $\psi$. To do this we shall define mappings $\Phi$ and $\Psi$ for the sequence

$$
(2)\quad T_{n-n_1} \oplus T_{n-n_2} \oplus \ldots \xrightarrow{\Phi} T_{n-1} \oplus \ldots \oplus T_{n-1} \xrightarrow{\Psi} T_{n} \rightarrow 0
$$

where $\Phi$ and $\Psi$ are linear and the sequence is exact at $T_{n}$. From this we shall get the $\varphi, \psi$ for the sequence (1).

Let $d$ be the number of summands in $T_{n-1} \oplus \ldots \oplus T_{n-1}$. We define $\Phi$ by:

$$
\Phi: t_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus t_{d} \longrightarrow \sum_{i=1}^{d} t_{i} x_{i}, \quad \text{where } t_i \in T_{n-1}.
$$

To define $\Psi$, consider an element $s_{n-n_{1}} \oplus s_{n-n_{2}} \oplus \ldots$ with $s_{n-n_i} \in T_{n-n_i}$. Since $\sum_{i} s_{n-n_{i}} f_{n_i} = \sum_{i=1}^{d} u_{i} x_{i}$ with $u_{i} \in T_{n-1}$, we define $\Psi: T_{n-n_{1}} \oplus T_{n-n_{2}} \oplus \ldots \rightarrow T_{n}$ by $\Psi(s_{n-n_{1}} \oplus s_{n-n_{2}} \oplus \ldots) = \sum_{i=1}^{d} u_{i} x_{i}$.

By the uniqueness of representation of any element in $T_{n}$ as $\sum_{i=1}^{d} u_{i} x_{i}, u_{i} \in T_{n-1}$, $\Psi$ is well-defined. Trivially $\Phi$ and $\Psi$ are linear. It is equally clear that $\Psi$ is onto $T_{n}$ so the sequence (2) is exact at $T_{n}$.

Let $L_{i}= C \cap T_{i}$; we show that we can induce (1) from (2). If $t_{1}, \ldots, t_{d} \in T_{n-1}$ then clearly $\sum t_{i} x_{i} \in T_{n}$ hence $\sum t_{i} x_{i} \in \alpha_{n}$, where $\alpha_n = L_n$. Therefore $\Psi$ induces a map $\psi: T_{n-1} \oplus \ldots \oplus T_{n-1} \rightarrow T_{n} / \alpha_{n}$.

$$
\psi: A_{n-1}^{\oplus k} \longrightarrow A_n \longrightarrow 0.
$$

We consider $\Phi$. Suppose that $s_{n-n_{1}}, s_{n-n_{2}}, \ldots, s_{n-n_{k}}$ are in $\sigma_{n-n_{1}}, \sigma_{n-n_{2}}, \ldots, \sigma_{n-n_{k}}$ respectively. We want to show that $u_{1}, \ldots, u_{d}$ defined by $\sum s_{n-n_{i}} f_{i}=\sum u_{i} x_{i}$ are in $\sigma_{n-1}$. Since $\Phi$ is linear it is enough to do it for $s_{n-n_{i}} \in \sigma_{n-n_{i}}$. Now $s_{n-n_{i}} f_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{d} s_{n-n_{i}} g_{i j} x_{j}$ where $f_{i}=\sum g_{i j} x_{j}$; if $s_{n-n_{i}} \in \sigma_{n-n_{i}}$ then $s_{n-n_{i}} g_{i j} \in \sigma_{n-1}$. But $u_j = \sum_i s_{n-n_i} g_{ij}$ hence $u_{j} \in \sigma \cap T_{n-1} = \sigma_{n-1}$. Thus $\Phi$ induces a map down to $\sigma_{n-1}$.

All that remains is to show exactness at \(A_{n-1} \oplus \cdots \oplus A_{n-1}\). We first show that \(\varphi \psi = 0\). If \(s_{n-n_{1}}, s_{n-n_{2}}, \ldots \in T_{n-n_{1}}, T_{n-n_{2}}, \ldots\), then \(\varphi \psi (s_{n-n_{1}} \oplus s_{n-n_{2}} \oplus \cdots ) = \sum_{i=1}^{d} u_{i} x_{i}\), where \(\sum u_{i} x_{i} = \sum s_{n-n_{i}} f_{i}\). Since the \(f_{i} \in \mathcal{O}_{R}\), we have \(\sum s_{n-n_{i}} f_{i} \in \mathcal{O}_{L}\); hence \(\sum u_{i} x_{i} \in \mathcal{O}_{L}\). Thus, \(\varphi \psi\) maps \(T_{n-n_{1}} \oplus \cdots\) into \(\mathcal{O}_{L}\), and so \(A_{n-n_{1}} \oplus A_{n-n_{2}} \oplus \cdots\) is mapped into \(0\) by \(\varphi \psi\).

We now want to show that if \(\psi (t_{1} \oplus \cdots \oplus t_{d}) \in \mathcal{O}_{L}\), then we can find \(u_{1}, u_{2}, \ldots, u_{d} \in T_{n-1}\) such that \(t_{i} - u_{i} \in \mathcal{B}\) for \(i = 1, 2, \ldots, d\), and such that \(\sum u_{i} x_{i} = \sum s_{n-n_{i}} f_{i}\) for some \(s_{n-n_{i}}\) in the appropriate \(T_{n-n_{i}}\).

Suppose then that $\sum t_{i} x_{i} \in \sigma$; being in $O$, which is generated as an ideal by the $f_{\ell}$, $\sum t_{i} x_{i} = \sum a_{k\ell} f_{\ell} b_{k\ell} + \sum c_{\ell} f_{\ell}$ where the $a_{k\ell}, b_{k\ell}, c_{\ell}$ are homogeneous and $b_{k\ell}$ is of degree $\geq 1$. Since $\sum t_{i} x_{i} \in T_{n}$, by picking out the parts of the right degree we may assume that the $a_{k\ell} f_{\ell} b_{k\ell}$, $c_{\ell} f_{\ell}$ are all in $T_{n}$. Since the $b_{k\ell}$ are of degree at least 1, $b_{k\ell} = \sum d_{k\ell m} x_{m}$, hence

$$
\sum a_{k\ell} f_{\ell} b_{k\ell} = \sum a_{k\ell} f_{\ell} \left( \sum d_{k\ell m} x_{m} \right) = \sum \left( \sum_{k,\ell} a_{k\ell} f_{\ell} d_{k\ell m} \right) x_{m} = \sum d_{m} x_{m},
$$
where $d_m = \sum_{k,\ell} a_{k\ell} f_{\ell} d_{k\ell m}$.

where $d_{m}=\sum_{k,l} a_{kl} f_{l} d_{klm}$ so is in $C$. If $\sum_{l} c_{l} f_{l} =\sum_{i=1}^{d} u_{i} x_{i}$ we have $\sum_{i=1}^{d} t_{i} x_{i}=\sum_{m=1}^{d} d_{m} x_{m}+\sum_{i=1}^{d} u_{i} x_{i}$. Hence $t_{i}-u_{i}=d_{i} \in C$. But $\left(c_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus c_{d}\right) \Phi=u_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus u_{d}$ by definition of $Q$. Hence we have proved the exactness at $A_{n-1} \oplus \ldots \oplus A_{n-1}$. This proves part (1) of the theorem.

We now consider formal power series in $t$ with integer coefficients; we declare $\sum_{n=1}^{\infty} c_{n} t^{n} \geq \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} d_{n} t^{n}$ if $c_{i} \geq d_{i}$ for all $i$. From part (1) we have, with $b_0 = 1$,

$$
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty} b_{n} t^{n} \geq \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} d \cdot b_{n-1} t^{n}-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty} \sum_{i: n_i \leq n} b_{n-n_i} t^{n}.
$$

From the definition of $r_{i}$,

$$
\sum_{i,m} b_{m} t^{n_{i}+m}=\sum_{i} t^{n_{i}} \sum_{m=0}^{\infty} b_{m} t^{m}=\left(\sum_{i=2}^{\infty} r_{i} t^{i}\right)\left(\sum_{m=0}^{\infty} b_{m} t^{m}\right).
$$

Let $P_{A}(t)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty} b_{m} t^{m}$. From the above

$$
P_{A}(t)-1 \geq d t P_{A}(t)-\left(\sum_{i=2}^{\infty} r_{i} t^{i}\right) P_{A}(t),
$$

hence

$$
P_{A}(t)\left(1-d t-\sum_{i=2}^{\infty} r_{i} t^{i}\right) \geq 1 .
$$

But then we immediately see that if the coefficients of $\left(1-d t-\sum_{i=2}^{\infty} r_{i} t^{i}\right)^{-1}$ are positive then an infinite number of the $b_{n}$ are not $0$, hence $A$ is infinite dimensional.

**THEOREM 7.2.** If the coefficients in the power series expansion of $\left(1-d t+\sum_{i=2}^{\infty} r_{i} t^{i}\right)^{-1}$ are positive, then the algebra is infinite dimensional over $F$.

We leave it as an exercise to show that if $r_{i} \leq \left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)^{2}$ for all $i$ (where $d$ is a positive integer) then indeed all the coefficients of $\left(1-d t+\sum_{i=2}^{\infty} r_{i} t^{i}\right)^{-1}$ are positive. Thus part (2) of Theorem 7.1 is proved.

We use Theorem 7.1 to construct a finitely generated nil algebra which is not nilpotent. This settles the Kurosh Problem in the negative.

**THEOREM 7.3.** If $F$ is any countable field there exists an infinite dimensional nil algebra over $F$ generated by three elements.

Proof. Let $T=F\left[x_{1}, x_{2}, x_{3}\right]$ be the free algebra over $F$ generated by the three non-commuting indeterminates $x_{1}, x_{2}, x_{3}$; $T$ is graded, $T=T_{0} \oplus T_{1} \oplus \ldots \oplus T_{n} \oplus \ldots$. Consider the ideal $T'=T_{1} \oplus T_{2} \oplus \ldots$ of $T$. $T'$ is countable so we enumerate its elements as $s_{1}, s_{2}, \ldots$. Let $m_{1} \geq 2$ be an arbitrary integer and let $s_{1}^{m_{1}}=s_{12}+s_{13}+\ldots+s_{1 k_{1}}$ where $s_{1 j} \in T_{j}$. Choose an integer $m_{2}>0$ so that $s_{2} \in T_{k_{1}+1} \oplus \ldots$; $s_{2}^{m_{2}}=s_{2, k_{1}+1}+\ldots+s_{2, k_{2}}$ with $s_{2 j} \in T_{j}$. Thus we may choose integers $k_{1}<k_{2}<\ldots$ such that $s_{i}^{m_{i}}=s_{i, k_{i-1}+1}+\ldots+s_{i k_{i}}$ with $s_{i j} \in T_{j}$. Let $O$ be the ideal of $T$ generated by the $s_{i j}$. By construction of the $s_{i j}$, the parameter $r_{k}$ in Theorem 7.1 is at most 1. Since $d=3$ (the number of generators), we have $r_{k} \leq\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)^{2}$. By Theorem 7.1, $T / O$ is infinite dimensional over $F$. Moreover, by construction $T' / O$ is a nil algebra. This is the required example since $T / O$ is generated over $F$ by three elements.

This result is not the best possible for as Golod has shown (in a proof which is not difficult) given any field $F$ there is an infinite dimensional nil algebra over $F$ generated by two elements.

We use Theorem 7.3 to construct an example of an infinite torsion group generated by three elements, which is therefore a counter-example to the Burnside Problem.

**Theorem 7.4.** If $p$ is any prime number there is an infinite group $G$ generated by three elements in which every element has order a power of $p$.

*Proof.* Let $F$ be the prime field with $p$ elements and let $\mathcal{C}$ be the ideal in $T = F[x_1, x_2, x_3]$ constructed during the proof of Theorem 7.3. Let $A = T / \mathcal{C}$ and let $a_1, a_2, a_3$ be $x_1 + \mathcal{C}, x_2 + \mathcal{C}, x_3 + \mathcal{C}$ respectively. Let $G$ be the multiplicative semi-group generated by $1 + a_1, 1 + a_2$ and $1 + a_3$. If an element is in $G$, it is of the form $1 + a$ where $a \in T/\mathcal{C}$ hence $a$ is nilpotent; if $a^{n}=0$ then $a^{p^{m}}=0$ for some $m$ so that $(1+a)^{p^{m}}=1$ since we are in characteristic $p$. Thus $G$ is a group in which every element has order a power of $p$. We claim $G$ is infinite, for if $G$ is finite the linear combinations of elements in $G$ over $F$ would form a finite dimensional subalgebra $B$ of $A$. But since $1+a_i$ and $1$ are in $G$, $a_i \in B$ hence $A = B$, contradicting that $A$ is infinite dimensional. This finishes the proof.

We now turn to other things with the advice to the reader to read the entire Golod-Shafarevich paper and its sequels by Golod. We have only considered here a small portion of the wonderful things they do.

It was mentioned in (N) that the question whether a right primitive ring need be left primitive was open. Recently G. Bergman has given an example of a ring which is right primitive but not left primitive. We now shall exhibit this example.

Let $Q$ be the field of rational numbers and let $Q(x)$ be the field of rational functions in the indeterminate $x$ over $Q$. In $Q(x)$ the mapping $\alpha: r(x) \rightarrow r\left(x^{2}\right)$ defines a monomorphism into but not onto. Let $A$ be the ring of all polynomials $r_{0}(x)+r_{1}(x) y+\ldots+r_{n}(x) y^{n}$, with $r_{i}(x) \in Q(x)$ where equality and addition are as usual but where $y r_i(x)=r_i\left(x^{2}\right) y=\alpha(r_i(x)) y$. $A$ is a non-commutative integral domain where all left ideals are principal, since we have a left division algorithm. It is in A that we shall find the right, but not left, primitive ring $R$.

### LEMMA 7.1.
We first show the following lemma.

Any subring $B$ of $A$, containing $x$ and $y$, which is an algebra over $Q$ is right primitive.

*Proof.* We shall exhibit a faithful, irreducible right $B$-module.

Given $r \in Q(x)$ we define $r^{*} \in Q(x)$ by

$$
r^{*}(t) = \frac{r(\sqrt{t}) + r(-\sqrt{t})}{2} \quad \text{for } t \geq 0,
$$
so that in particular, substituting $t = x^2$, we have $r^{*}(x^2) = \frac{r(x) + r(-x)}{2}$.

We define a right $A$-module structure on $Q(x)$ by defining: $r \cdot y = r^{*}$, $r \cdot q = r q$ for $r, q \in Q(x)$. We claim this endows $Q(x)$ with the structure of a right $A$-module. We merely must check: for $r, q \in Q(x)$, $(r \cdot y) \cdot q = r \cdot (y q) = r \cdot (\alpha(q) y) = (r \alpha(q)) \cdot y$, that is, $r^{*} q = (r \alpha(q))^{*}$. Now $(r^{*} q)(x^{2}) = r^{*}(x^{2}) q(x^{2}) = \frac{1}{2}(r(x)+r(-x)) q(x^{2}) = \frac{1}{2}\left(r(x) q(x^{2}) + r(-x) q(x^{2})\right) = \frac{1}{2}\left(r(x) \alpha(q)(x) + r(-x) \alpha(q)(x)\right) = (r \alpha(q))^{*}(x^{2})$. Thus $r^{*} q = (r \alpha(q))^{*}$ and so we see that $Q(x)$ becomes a right $A$-module under the defined action.

From the definition of the action we immediately see:

$$
\begin{aligned}
x^{n} \cdot y^{m} &= x^{n / 2^{m}} & \text{if } 2^{m} \mid n \\
&= 0 & \text{if } 2^{m} \nmid n
\end{aligned}
$$

Let $M$ be the $B$-submodule of $Q(x)$ generated by $x$. We claim that $M$ is both irreducible and faithful as a $B$-module.

Let $0 \neq f \in M$ where $f = \frac{p(x)}{q(x)}$ and $p(x), q(x)$ are polynomials in $\mathbb{Q}$.  
Let $n = \deg p(x)$; choose $m \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $2^{m} > n$.  

Then  
\[
f \cdot \left(q(x) x^{2^{m}-n} y^{m}\right)
= \left(p(x) x^{2^{m}-n}\right) y^{m}
= c x^{2^{m}} y^{m},
\]  
where $c \neq 0$ is the leading coefficient of $p(x)$ (so that $p(x)x^{2^{m}-n}$ has leading term $c x^{2^{m}}$).  

Thus $x^{2^{m}} y^{m}$ is in the submodule generated by any non‑zero element of $M$.  
Since $x$ generates $M$ and $x^{2^{m}} y^{m}$ is a nonzero multiple of $x$, any nonzero submodule contains a generator of $M$. Therefore $M$ is irreducible.

Suppose now that $0 \neq b=\sum r_{i}(x) y^{i}$ in $B$ annihilates $M$. Let $x p(x) \neq 0$ be a polynomial so that all $x p(x) r_{i}(x)$ are polynomials and let $0 \neq x p(x) b=\sum_{i} s_{i}(x) y^{i}$ where the $s_{i}(x)$ are polynomials. Thus $\sum s_{i}(x) y^{i}$ is in $B$ and annihilates $M$. Let $j$ be the least integer such that $s_{j}(x) \neq 0$ and let $d=\max \left(\operatorname{deg} s_{i}(x)-\operatorname{deg} s_{j}(x)\right)$. Pick $n$ so that $2^{n} \geq \operatorname{deg} s_{j}(x)$ and $2^{n-j-1} \geq d$. Let $s_{j}(x)$ have degree $m$. Consider $x^{2^{n}-m} \cdot\left(\sum s_{i}(x) y^{i}\right)$. In the term $x^{2^{n}-m} s_{j}(x)$ the highest power of $x$ occurring with non-zero coefficient is $x^{2^{n}}$ hence in $x^{2^{n}-m} s_{j}(x) \cdot y^{j}$, $x^{2^{n-j}}$ occurs with non-zero coefficient. For $t>j$ the highest power of $x$ in $x^{2^{n}-m} s_{t}(x)$ is $2^{n}-m+\operatorname{deg} s_{t}(x) \leq 2^{n}+d$ by definition of $d$, so in $x^{2^{n}-m} s_{t}(x) \cdot y^{t}$, $x$ occurs at most to the power $\frac{2^{n}-m+\operatorname{deg} s_{t}(x)}{2^{t}}$; since $y^{2} = x$ (or more generally, $y^{2^{t}}$ scales the exponent of $x$ by $1/2^{t}$), we have

$$
\frac{2^{n}-m+\operatorname{deg} s_{t}(x)}{2^{t}} \leq \frac{2^{n}+d}{2^{t}} \leq 2^{n-t} + \frac{d}{2^{t}} \leq 2^{n-j-1} + \frac{d}{2^{t}} \leq 2^{n-j-1}+2^{n-j-1}=2^{n-j}.
$$

In other words $x^{2^{n-j}}$ occurs with non-zero coefficient in $x^{2^{n}-m}\left(\sum s_{i}(x) y^{i}\right)$ whence $x^{2^{n}-m} \cdot\left(\sum s_{i}(x) y^{i}\right) \neq 0$, contrary to $\sum s_{i}(x) y^{i}$ annihilating $M$. This proves the lemma.

Let $v_{n}$ be the valuation induced by the $n$-th cyclotomic polynomial $p_{n}(x)$ on $\mathbb{Q}(x)$.

**Lemma 7.2.** If $n$ is odd $v_{n}(r(x))=v_{n}\left(r\left(x^{2}\right)\right)$.
*Proof.* Since $p_{n}(x)$ is irreducible over $\mathbb{Q}$, any polynomial in $\mathbb{Q}(x)$ having a common root with $p_{n}(x)$ has $p_{n}(x)$ as a factor. Now $p_{n}\left(x^{2}\right)$ for $n$ odd has as roots $\pm \sqrt{\theta}$ where $\theta$ ranges over the roots of $p_{n}(x)$. Since $n$ is odd, $p_n(x)$ and $p_n(-x)$ are coprime, and the roots of $p_n(x^2)$ are precisely $\pm\sqrt{\theta}$; hence $p_{n}(x) \mid p_{n}(x^{2})$. Similarly $p_{n}(-x) \mid p_{n}\left(x^{2}\right)$. Thus on comparing degrees, $p_{n}\left(x^{2}\right)=p_{n}(x) p_{n}(-x)$, hence $v_{n}\left(p_{n}\left(x^{2}\right)\right)=v_{n}\left(p_{n}(x)\right)$. If $\left(p_{n}(x), q(x)\right)=1$ then $\left(p_{n}(x), q\left(x^{2}\right)\right)=1$ hence $v_{n}(q(x))=0$. But then for any polynomial $f(x)$, factoring $f$ into irreducibles shows $v_{n}(f(x))=v_{n}\left(f\left(x^{2}\right)\right)$. From this we get the result for rational functions and the lemma is established.

We define $v_{n}$ on $A$, for odd $n$, by

$$
v_{n}\left(\sum r_{i}(x) y^{i}\right)=\min_i v_{n}\left(r_{i}(x)\right) .
$$

From Lemma 7.2, $v_{n}(r(x))=v_{n}(r(x^2))$ hence $v_{n}$ defines a valuation on $A$. Let $R=\left\{z \in A \mid v_{n}(z) \geq 0 \text{ for all odd } n>1\right\}$. Clearly $R$ is an algebra over $\mathbb{Q}$ and contains $x$ and $y$, hence by Lemma 7.1 $R$ is right primitive. We now prove

**Theorem 7.5.** $R$ is right primitive but not left primitive.

Proof. Let $R = \mathbb{Q}\langle x, y \mid xy = yx^2\rangle$ and let $A = \mathbb{Q}(x)[y]$, where $\mathbb{Q}(x)$ is the field of rational functions. Let $I$ be a maximal left ideal of $R$; we shall show that $I$ contains a non-zero two-sided ideal of $R$, therefore $R$ cannot be left primitive.

Consider the left ideal $AI = A \cdot I$ of $A$; if $AI \neq A$ it is generated by some element $g = a_0(x) + a_1(x)y + \ldots + a_m(x)y^m$, $m > 0$, $a_m(x) \neq 0$, since the non-zero elements in $\mathbb{Q}(x)$ are invertible in $A$. Define the valuation $V_n: A \to \mathbb{Z} \cup \{\infty\}$ for odd $n$ by $V_n(\sum b_i(x) y^i) = \min_i \{ V_x(b_i(x)) \}$, where $V_x$ is the $x$-adic valuation on $\mathbb{Q}(x)$. Choose $n$ odd so that $V_n(g) = 0$. Given any $a = \sum b_i y^i$ there is an $i$ so that $V_n(a) = V_n(b_i)$; let $\delta(a) =$ largest $i$ so that $V_n(a) = V_n(b_i)$. Clearly $\delta(ac) = \delta(a) + \delta(c)$ for all $a, c \in A$.

Now since $V_n(g) = 0$, $\delta(g) = m$ where $m$ is the degree of $g$ in $y$. If $u \in AI$ then $\delta(u) \geq \delta(g) > 0$. Let $p_n(x)$ be the $n$-th cyclotomic polynomial; $p_n(x)$ is in $R$ and $V_n(p_n(x)) = 1$. Also $p_n(x) \notin I$ because it is invertible in $\mathbb{Q}(x)$ (and hence in $A$), and $AI \neq A$. Since $I$ is maximal, $R = I + Rp_n(x)$ so for some $i \in I, r \in R$, $1 = i + rp_n(x)$; thus $1 - rp_n(x) \in I$. Then $1 - rp_n(x) \in AI$, so $\delta(1 - rp_n(x)) \geq \delta(g) > 0$. But $\delta(1 - rp_n(x)) = \delta(1) + \delta(rp_n(x)) = 0 + \delta(r) + \delta(p_n(x)) = \delta(r) + 0 = 0$ because $\delta(p_n(x)) = 0$ (since $p_n(x) \in \mathbb{Q}(x)$ and $V_n(p_n(x))=1$ implies the index for the minimum valuation is $0$). Thus $0 = \delta(1 - rp_n(x)) \geq \delta(g) > 0$, a contradiction. Hence $AI = A$.

Since $AI = A$, we have $1 \in AI$, so $1 = \sum a_j i_j$ for some $a_j \in A, i_j \in I$. Clearing denominators from the $a_j$ (multiplying by a suitable $q(x) \in \mathbb{Q}[x]$), we obtain $q(x) = \sum r_j i_j \in I$ with $q(x) \neq 0$. Thus the non-zero two-sided ideal $R q(x) R$ is contained in $I$. Therefore $R$ cannot be left primitive.

Therefore if $I$ is a maximal left ideal of $R$ then $A I=A$. Thus $1=a_{1} i_{1}+\cdots+a_{s} i_{s}$ for $a_{j} \in A, i_{j} \in I$. We can find $w \neq 0 \in Q(x)$ so that for all $n$, $V_{n}(w a_{i}) \geq 0$ for $1 \leq i \leq s$. Thus $w a_{1} \in R$ hence $w=w\left(a_{1} i_{1}+\cdots + a_{s} i_{s}\right)=\left(w a_{1}\right) i_{1}+\cdots+\left(w a_{s}\right) i_{s}$ is an element of $RI \subseteq I$. Since $w \in I$, $Rw \subseteq I$. However, since $w$ is invertible in $A, V_{n}\left(w^{-1} t w\right)=V_{n}(t)$ for all $t \in A$, in particular $w^{-1} Rw=R$ implies. Thus $Rw=wR$ leading to $wR=Rw \subseteq I$. Thus $I$ contains the two-sided ideal $wR$ of $R$. This proves the theorem.

We conclude this chapter with an example, due to Sasiada, of a simple radical ring.

Let $F$ be any field and let $A$ be the ring of formal power series over $F$ in the two non-commuting variables $x$ and $y$. Let $A^{\prime}$ be the set of all elements in $A$ whose constant term is $0$.

It is easy to verify that $A^{\prime}$ is a radical ring. Suppose for the moment that we know that $\mathbf{x}$ is not in the two-sided ideal of $A$ generated by $x-y x^{2} y$. Let $U$ be an ideal of $A^{\prime}$ which contains $x-y x^{2} y$ and is maximal with respect to the exclusion of $x$. Let $R=A^{\prime} / U$; $R$ is a radical ring having a unique minimal ideal $S \neq (0)$ which is contained in every non-zero ideal of $R$ and which contains $\overline{x}=x+U$. From this it is trivial that $S$ is a radical ring devoid of non-trivial two-sided ideals. To know that $S$ is simple we need but know that $S^{2} \neq (0)$. But in $R$, $\overline{x}=\overline{y} \overline{x}^{2} \overline{y}$ where $\overline{y}=y+U$, so that $\overline{x}=(\overline{y} \overline{x})(\overline{x} \overline{y}) \neq 0 \in S^{2}$, hence $S^{2} \neq (0)$. Thus $S$ would be a simple radical ring. To produce the desired example we must merely show that $x$ is not in the two-sided ideal of $A$ generated by $x-y x^{2} y$. The proof we give here for this fact is from the recent paper by Cohn and Sasiada.

We introduce some notation. With $A$ and $A^{\prime}$ as above, let $M$ be the two-sided ideal of $A$ generated by $\overline{u}=x-y x^{2} y$ and let $E$ be the set of elements in $A$ having constant term 1. We begin with

**LEMMA 7.3.** If \(c = \sum_{i=1}^{n} a_i \otimes b_i\) and \(b_1 \notin A'\), then there exist \(a_1' \in A\), \(b_1' \in A'\), and for \(i > 1\) elements \(a_i' \in A\), \(b_i' \in A'\) such that
\[
c = a_1' \otimes b_1' + \sum_{i=2}^{n} a_i' \otimes b_i'.
\]

*Proof.* Since \(b_1 \notin A'\), we have \(b_1 = \beta b_1'\) for some \(\beta \neq 0\) in \(F\) (the base field) and some \(b_1' \in A'\). For \(i \ge 2\), let \(\beta_i\) be the constant term of \(b_i\). Then
\[
a_1 \otimes b_1 + \sum_{i=2}^{n} a_i \otimes b_i
= \left( \beta a_1 + \sum_{i=2}^{n} \beta_i a_i \right) \otimes b_1' + \sum_{i=2}^{n} a_i \otimes (b_i - \beta_i b_1').
\]
This gives the required form with
\[
a_1' = \beta a_1 + \sum_{i=2}^{n} \beta_i a_i, \quad b_1' = b_1', \quad \text{and for } i > 1: \; a_i' = a_i, \; b_i' = b_i - \beta_i b_1'.
\]

**LEMMA 7.4.** Let \(x, y \in A\). Suppose \(c = \sum_{i=1}^{n} a_i \otimes b_i\) with \(b_1 \notin A'\) has the specific form \(c = c x + d y\) for some scalars \(c, d \in F\). Then, applying Lemma 7.3, there exist \(b_i^* \in A'\) such that
\[
c x + d y = c \otimes (1 + b_1^*) + \sum_{i=2}^{n} a_i \otimes b_i^*.
\]

Proof. Using Lemma 7.3 we may suppose, given the hypothesis $b_{i}^{\prime} \notin A^{\prime}$, that $b_{1} \in E$ and $b_{i} \in A^{\prime}$ for $i>1$, without changing the original $a_{i}$ for $i>1$. Write $b_{1}=1+b_{1}^{\prime} x+b_{1}^{\prime \prime} y, b_{i}=b_{i}^{\prime} x+b_{i}^{*} y$ for $i>1$; substituting these in $c x+d y=\sum_{i=1}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}$, using $u=x-y x_{n}^{2} y$ and comparing coefficients of $x$ yields $c=a_{1}+a_{2} u b_{1}^{\prime}+\sum_{i=2}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}^{\prime}$. Now $1+u b_{1}^{\prime}$ is invertible in $A$ and its inverse, $1-u b_{1}^{\prime}+\left(u b_{1}^{\prime}\right)^{2}=\ldots +(-1)^{m}\left(u b_{1}^{\prime}\right)^{m}+\ldots$ is of the form $1+u g$ for $g \in A$. Thus we have $a_{1}=c(1+u g)-\sum_{i=2}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}^{\prime}(1+u g)$; substituting this value for $a_{1}$ in the given expression for $c x+d y$ yields

$$
\begin{aligned}
c x+d y & =c(1+u g) u b_{1}-\sum_{i=2}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}^{\prime}(1+u g) u b_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i} \\
& =c u(1+g u) b_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}^{*},
\end{aligned}
$$

where $b_{i}^{*}=b_{i}^{\prime}(1+u g) u b_{1}+b_{i}$ is in $A^{\prime}$. Since both $b_{1}$ and $1+g u$ are in $E$, $(1+g u) b_{1}$ is in $E$ so of the form $1+b_{1}^{*}$, $b_{1}^{*} \in A^{\prime}$. This is precisely the assertion of Lemma 7.4.

**Lemma 7.5.** Suppose the product $d y$ has the form $d y=\sum_{i=1}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}$, where $n$ is the minimal such for $d y$. Then each $b_{i}$ is in $A^{\prime}$.

Proof. Suppose some $b_{i} \notin A'$, say $b_{1}$; using the previous lemma, with the constant term $0$, yields $0 = \sum_{i=2}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}'$ contradicting the minimal nature of $n$.

We now come to the heart of the discussion. This is

**LEMMA 7.6.** $M$, the ideal generated by $u=x-y x^{2} y$, contains no monomial.

Proof. Suppose that the lemma is false; then there is some monomial $c \in M, c=\sum_{i=1}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}$. For all such monomials pick a monomial $c$ where $n$ is minimal possible. For this $n$ pick $c$ of lowest degree lying in $M$. We assert that $c$ must finish in $x$; if not, $c=c_{1} y$. By Lemma 7.5 all the $b_{i} \in A'$ and writing $b_{i}=b_{i}' x+b_{i}'' y$ and substituting in the relation for $c$ gives $c_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}''$ on comparing the coefficient of $y$. Since $c_{1}$ is of lower degree than $c$ this is not possible. Hence we know that $c=c_{1} x$ and $c_{1} x=\sum_{i=1}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}$.

If all the $b_{i}$ are in $A'$ writing $b_{i}=b_{i}' x+b_{i}'' y$ will lead to the contradiction $c_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n} a_{i} u b_{i}'$. Thus we may suppose $b_{1} \notin A'$; by Lemma 7.4, $c_{1} x=c_{1} u d_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n} a_{i} u d_{i}$ where $d_{1} \notin A', d_{i} \in A'$ for $i>1$.

We claim that for any $r \leq n$ we can find monomials $p_{1}, \ldots, p_{r}$ of positive degree and elements $d_{1}, \ldots, d_{n}, A$ (depending on $r$) such that
\begin{equation}
c_{1}p_{1} \cdots p_{r} x = c_{1} \sum_{i=1}^{r} p_{1} \cdots p_{i-1} u d_{i} + \sum_{j=r+1}^{n} a_{j} u d_{j},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
d_{i} \notin A^{\mathfrak{t}} \quad \text{for some } 1 \leq i \leq n.
\end{equation}

Note that our preceding discussion established this for $r=0$. We now proceed by induction.

Suppose for $r<n$ elements $d_{1}, \ldots, d_{n}$ and monomials $p_{1}, \ldots, p_{r}$ of positive degree can be found satisfying (1) and (2). We want to pass to $r+1$. We divide into two cases.

1. If $d_{j} \in A^{\mathfrak{t}}$ for all $j>r$ then $d_{i_{0}} \notin A^{\mathfrak{t}}$ for some $i_{0} \leq r$. Write $d_{j}=d_{j}^{1} x+d_{j}^{2} y$ for $j>r$ and $d_{i}=\lambda_{i}+d_{i}^{1} x+d_{i}^{2} y$ for $i \leq r$. Substituting in (1) and equating multipliers of $x$ yields that

$$
\begin{aligned}
c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r} &= c_{1} \sum_{i=1}^{r} \lambda_{i} p_{1} \cdots p_{i-1} + c_{1} \sum_{i=1}^{r} p_{1} \cdots p_{i-1} u d_{i}^{1} \\
&\quad + \sum_{j=r+1}^{n} a_{j} u d_{j}^{1};
\end{aligned}
$$

hence if $s$ is the least $i$ such that $\lambda_{i} \neq 0$, on transposing we get

$$
-\lambda_{s} p_{1} \cdots p_{s-1} u = \sum_{i=1}^{r} c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{i-1} u d_{i}^{1} + \sum_{j=r+1}^{n} a_{j} u d_{j}^{1}.
$$

where $f \in A'$ (since the $p_i$'s are of positive degree). Transpose the $s$-th term of the first sum on the right to the left hand side. We then have

$$
\begin{aligned}
c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{s-1}\left(\lambda_{s}(1+f)-u d_{s}'\right) &= \sum_{\substack{i=1 \\ i \neq s}}^{r} c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{i-1} u d_{i}' \\
&\quad + \sum_{j=r+1}^{n} a_{j} u d_{j}' .
\end{aligned}
$$

But $\lambda_{s}(1+f)-u d_{s}'$ is invertible in $A$ giving us $c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{s-1}$ as a combination of the $u d_{i}'$ and $a_{j} u d_{j}'$ terms from the right-hand side, involving $n-1$ terms. This contradicts the choice of $n$ and so case (1) is not possible.

II. Suppose, then that $d_{j} \notin A'$ for some $j>r$, say for $j=r+1$. Applying Lemma 7.4 to (1) gives

$$
\begin{aligned}
c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r} x &= \sum_{i=1}^{r} c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{i-1} u d_{i} + c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r}\left(1+u d_{r+1}\right) \\
&\quad + \sum_{j=r+2}^{n} a_{j} u d_{j},
\end{aligned}
$$

where all the $d_{k}'', 1 \leq k \leq n$ are in $A'$. Writing $d_{k}=d_{k}' x+d_{k}'' y$ and substituting in this leads, on comparing multipliers of $x$, to

$$
\begin{aligned}
c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r} u x^{2} &= \sum_{i=1}^{r} c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{i-1} u d_{i}' + c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r} u d_{r+1}' \\
&\quad + \sum_{j=r+2}^{n} a_{j} u d_{j}' .
\end{aligned}
$$

If in (3) all the $d_{k}^{\prime}$ are in $A^{\prime}$, we can write $d_{k}^{\prime}=e_{k}^{\prime} x+e_{k}^{\prime \prime} y$, substitute, pick out the multiplier of $x$ to get

$$
\begin{equation}
\begin{split}
c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r} x y &=\sum_{i=1}^{r} c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{i-1} u e_{i}^{\prime}+c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r} u e_{r+1}^{\prime} \\
&\quad +\sum_{j=r+2}^{n} a_{j} u e_{j}^{\prime}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
$$

If in (4) all the $e_{k}^{\prime}$ are in $A^{\prime}$, we can repeat the now-familiar argument to get

$$
\begin{equation}
\begin{split}
c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r} y &=\sum_{i=1}^{r} c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{i-1} u f_{i}+c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r} u f_{r+1} \\
&\quad +\sum_{j=r+2}^{n} a_{j} u f_{j}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
$$

Since this is a minimal representation of $c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r} y$, invoking Lemma 7.5, we have all the $f_{k}^{\prime}$ in $A^{\prime}$ and writing $f_{i}=g_{i}^{\prime} x+g_{i}^{\prime \prime} y$ leads to a cancellation of $y$ and the result

$$
\begin{equation*}
\begin{split}
c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r} &=\sum_{i=1}^{r} c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{i-1} u g_{i}^{\prime \prime}+c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r} u g_{r+1}^{\prime \prime} \\
&\quad +\sum_{j=r+2}^{n} a_{j} u g_{j}^{\prime \prime}.
\end{split}
\end{equation*}
$$

Hence,

$$
c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{r}\left(1-u g_{r+1}^{\prime \prime}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{r} c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{i-1} u g_{i}^{\prime \prime}+\sum_{j=r+2}^{n} a_{j} u g_{j}^{\prime \prime}.
$$

But $1-u g_{r+1}^{\prime \prime}$ is invertible so we get a representation of the monomial $c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{n}$ in $n-1$ terms of the form $s_{1} u t_{1}$; this contradicts the choice of $n$. Hence in (3) or (4) some $d_{i}$ or some $e_{i}$ is not in $A^{t}$. In either case this completes the induction.

The inductive step having been made we have that there exist $p_{1}, \ldots, p_{n}$ monomials of positive degree and $d_{1}, \ldots, d_{n} \in A$ such that

$$
c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{n} x=\sum_{i=1}^{n} c_{1} p_{1} \cdots p_{i-1} u\, d_{i}.
$$

But this puts us back into case I of our argument, which we saw was not possible. Hence the lemma is proved.

Since $M$ contains no monomials it does not contain $x$. Hence, as we saw earlier, this forces the existence of a simple radical ring.

As Hamsher has pointed out this also enables you to construct a countable simple radical ring. Let $F$ be countable; in $A$ consider the subring generated by $x$ and $y$; it too is countable. Adjoin all the quasi-inverses of its elements to it and consider the subring now generated; it is countable. Continue and take the direct limit to get a countable radical subring $T$ of $A$ containing $x$ and $y$. In $T$, $x$ is not in the ideal generated by $x-y x^{2} y$; now do as before, taking an ideal $M$ of $T$ maximal with respect to exclusion of $x$ and containing $x-y x^{2} y$. The minimal ideal of $T / M$ is a countable simple radical ring.

## Bibliography

1. G. Bergman, "A ring primitive on the right but not on the left", Proc. Amer. Math. Soc., 15 (1964), pp. 473–475.
2. P. M. Cohn and E. Sasiada, "An example of a simple radical ring", J. Algebra, 5 (1967), pp. 373–377.
3. E. S. Golod, "On nil algebras and finitely approximable p-groups", Izv. Akad. Nauk SSSR Ser. Mat., 28 (1964), pp. 273–276.
4. E. S. Golod and I. R. Shafarevich, "On the class field tower", Izv. Akad. Nauk SSSR Ser. Mat., 28 (1964), pp. 261–272.
5. E. Sasiada, "Solution of the problem of the existence of a simple radical ring", Bull. Acad. Polon. Sci., 9 (1961), pp. 257–260.
